# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4

## Climate Change and Its Impact on Africa

According to the World Resources Institute, failure to meet the UN’s Climate targets agreed in Paris likely means that 118 million people in Africa will be pushed into extreme poverty by 2030. The African Union expects that 15% of Africa’s hard-earned GDP growth will be wiped out if climate change continues unabated.

## Energy Access and Consumption in Sub-Saharan Africa

While Africa has historically low emissions, this statistic is misleading given Africa's lack of energy access and its corresponding low rate of consumption. The AfDB estimates that the per capita consumption of energy in sub-Saharan Africa (excluding South Africa) is 180 kWh, compared to 13,000 kWh per capita in the United States and 6,500 kWh in Europe. The average Ethiopian family uses less than one hundredth of the power of one American.

Approximately 550 million people in Sub-Saharan Africa do not have access to electricity and must rely on kerosene and diesel generators for lighting, while another 900 million lack access to clean cooking fuels and technologies, causing rapid deforestation and dangerous levels of indoor air pollution. 78% of businesses across the continent experience disruptions to energy causing them to resort to dirty and polluting diesel generators.

Africa’s energy consumption and manufacturing output needs to triple over the next two decades to create the economic opportunities and jobs that can lift its people out of poverty. In a business-as-usual scenario, with both increasing energy use and energy intensity, Africa’s emissions would double by 2050, derailing the Paris Agreement climate targets.

## Infrastructure and Economic Development

Africa has the opportunity and challenge of needing to build much of its infrastructure, manufacturing capacity, and economy. This requires accelerated financial support to enhance mitigation and adaptation action and address inequities in access to climate finance. It is now estimated that the conservative $100B per year financing pledged for developing countries at the 2009 Copenhagen climate talks will only be achieved in 2023 despite ongoing commitments made including at COP -26 in Glasgow in 2021.

Private sector financing complementing this critical financing gap is required to mobilize targeted capital to meet the energy challenge facing the Continent.

## KawiSafi Fund I Impact

KawiSafi Fund I achieved significant impact in its two focus countries Kenya and Rwanda – with combined 23.4M direct and 19.9M indirect lives impacted and over 20M tonnes of CO2e as of 30 June 2022. While we continue to be committed to supporting Kenya and Rwanda achieve their NDC’s, we are planning to expand our impact beyond these two countries given the significant need highlighted above.

KawiSafi Fund II intends to focus on priority counties where it has NOLs. As of June 15, 2023, we received 6 compliant NOLs from Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda, and Zambia. The Accredited Entity is in advanced discussions with several other countries, including (without limitation) Ghana, Senegal, South Africa. These countries all expressed significant interest in the project, but haven’t been able to complete their internal sign-off processes to submit compliant NOLs for various reasons.

Additional countries may be added to the programme after initial Board approval, provided they submit a compliant NOL and the addition is approved by the Board.

## Focus Countries Selection

To arrive at the focus countries, the team conducted a rigorous market evaluation and selection exercise considering (i) market gaps and need for climate financing to meet NDC targets; (ii) investment ecosystem; (iii) Acumen’s prior experience and reach; and (iv) country alignment to KawiSafi Fund II’s target sectors to identify where the Fund’s investments can address climate change mitigation priorities of each particular country and increase adaptative capacity to climate risks.

Taken together, interventions in these countries have the potential to improve the resilience of approximately 3.5 million people and avert 20.9 million tonnes of CO2e.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5

## 6. The KawiSafi Fund II

The KawiSafi Fund II is an ambitious $200 million venture equity Fund intending to utilize a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into the African region. The Fund expects to continue the success demonstrated in the deployment of KawiSafi Fund I and target firms with business models that deploy low-carbon technologies in an inclusive and sustainable way and that enable people to participate and flourish in a green economy and growth out of poverty. As at the date of submission of this proposal, the project has received six (6) No-Objection Letters (NOLs) from the Governments of Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda and Zambia.

## 7. The KawiSafi team

The KawiSafi team has developed an indicative advanced pipeline of over 40 investment opportunities with an aggregate deal value of $500 million across our focus verticals highlighted below. This is out of a larger pipeline pool of ~200 companies, with an estimated deal value of just under $2B across the ten countries of focus.

## 8. Building on KawiSafi Fund I experience

Building on the KawiSafi Fund I experience that identified and supported the emerging energy access ecosystem, KawiSafi Fund II has identified three thematic verticals that represent the new landscape for off-grid innovation, with proximity to its existing portfolio and universe of investment activity. These are:

1. **Energy Transition**: Firms delivering on global and national energy transition strategies through rapid deployment of low-carbon renewable power generation in renewable and commercial and industrial (“C&I”) markets, residential mini- and metro grids, financial services for renewables, and waste-to-energy circular economy models;
2. **Productivity**: firms focusing on low-carbon productivity and efficiency markets including low-cost internet access, “smart” green manufacturing, recycling / circular economy, clean cooling, and agro-processing technology;
3. **Mobility & Logistics**: firms involved in the rapidly evolving mobility & logistics markets where unique electric vehicle battery business models and artificial intelligence are driving rapid innovation in fragmented markets and creating new opportunities for climate-resilient public and private transportation systems.

## 9. KawiSafi II focus

KawiSafi II will focus on late-venture / early-growth stage companies with ticket sizes of $1-$10 million and on its core expertise of scaling businesses. The Management team sees the company-building role of KawiSafi II to be particularly important as markets recover and restructure in the context of a post-COVID 19 world. The larger fund and ticket size reflect the evolution of the investment landscape in Africa since Fund I was launched, with more investable businesses, sharper growth curves, and greater need for capital deployment. There remains a significant gap in equity funding at this stage of growth across African markets, where a company has launched and started to gain traction, but still needs capital to reach commercial scale.

KawiSafi Fund I's ticket sizes were $500k-$6M and investments were at late venture / early growth stage; KawiSafi II intends to invest across the same stages but will have the ability to invest slightly larger tickets, which our experience and research shows is required by the market.

## 10. KawiSafi Fund II builds on track record

KawiSafi Fund II builds on the track record and experience of Acumen as an Accredited Entity (“AE”) with two GCF-supported funds under management – the KawiSafi Ventures Fund and the Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund (ARAF). The KawiSafi Ventures Fund (approved in 2015 and launched in 2016) (“KawiSafi Fund I” or “KawiSafi I”) was one of GCF’s first investments: a $67M venture fund focused on renewable off-grid energy access in Kenya and Rwanda.

Key achievements of KawiSafi Fund I as at 30/06/2022 have been:

- Deployment of over $44M in 10 investments representing residential solar, biogas, PAYGO technology, consumer financing and C&I energy.
- A portfolio that has impacted over 43M people in Kenya and Rwanda with ~50% of households being below the poverty line.
- KawiSafi Fund I’s portfolio has pro-actively promoted gender diversity and inclusion, with our portfolio companies employing ~870 women and training over 4,900 women in the larger ecosystem.
- KawiSafi Fund I has cumulatively averted 17.8MtCO2e in Kenya and 2.4MtCO2e in Rwanda.

> The AE is in advanced discussions with several other countries

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6

## Leveraging public with private capital

KawiSafi Fund I has leveraged approximately $580M additional debt and equity capital to its portfolio (14x its investment; 47x GCF capital).  

As of 31/12/2022, KawiSafi Fund I has received one partial distribution from a portfolio company and is working with the companies to support subsequent equity raises and/or exit planning.

KawiSafi I has a robust data and impact tracking framework which reports on impact metrics to its shareholders and other stakeholders on a quarterly basis. For KawiSafi I, the team has worked extensively with the GCF portfolio management team on the APRs and ensuring that methodologies and impact processes are robust. The team works with a third party impact monitoring and evaluation firm, 60d ecibels, to develop and improve its impact processes on an ongoing basis. An independent evaluation is due in October 2023 under the terms of the KawiSafi Fund I project implementation plan.

## Climate results /benefits

11. KawiSafi Fund II focuses its efforts on private investment pathways identified in priority NDC’s to achieve both mitigation and adaptation impacts and benefits in the highest-impact countries listed above. The focus of KawiSafi Fund II is on supporting country climate action plans to avoid business as usual emissions (BAU) and support lower income communities to adapt and become more resilient to climate impacts. The Fund intends to focus on venture-backed companies that can deliver the highest CO2 mitigation and population resilience outcomes per $ invested.

The Fund is targeting an aversion of 20.9 million tCO2eq through its investments in energy, productivity, and mobility. Further, the Fund’s interventions seek to drive increased MW of low-emission energy capacity installed across the identified countries. Finally, the scaling of companies rolling out two, three and four-wheeler electric vehicles ("EVs") across various markets on the continent aims to increase the use of low-carbon transport and significantly reducing carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) in urban and peri-urban areas.

The Fund is also targeting reduced vulnerability and strengthened resilience of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly to effects of climate change. This is expected to be achieved through enhancing their resilience and adaptive capacity by:

1. Reducing reliance on climate vulnerable generation such as hydropower, fossil fuels, and vulnerable grids;
2. Increase the use of climate information in decision-making and climate-response planning through expansion of internet connectivity and supply chains in rural populations;
3. Strengthening food-systems which support agricultural communities to adapt to the changing realities of climate change.

Specifically, investments in innovations in the agriculture-energy nexus such as solar powered irrigation and cold storage are expected to increase farm communities’ resilience to changes in precipitation as well as enhance food security through reducing post-harvest losses helping lower food loss.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### I. Climate Context by region

Africa is the most vulnerable continent to climate change impacts under all climate scenarios above 1.5 degrees Celsius. Despite having contributed the least to global warming and having the lowest emissions, Africa faces exponential collateral damage, posing systemic risks to its economies, infrastructure investments, water and food systems, public health, agriculture, and livelihoods, threatening to undo its development gains.

Much of the continent is reported to have already seen increases of approximately 0.7°C. According to the IPCC, temperature increases in sub-Saharan Africa are projected to be higher than the global mean temperature increases (at global warming of 1.5°C and at 2°C). Even if the mean global temperature anomaly is kept below 1.5°C, regions between 15°S and 15°N are projected to experience an increase of hot nights, as well as longer and more frequent heatwaves. Increases would be even larger if the global mean temperatures were to reach 2°C of global warming, with significant changes in the occurrence and intensity of temperatures in all sub-Saharan regions.

The African Development Bank expects that 15% of Africa's hard-earned GDP growth will be wiped out if climate change continues unabated. Failure to meet the Paris Agreement goals will likely push over 100 million people into extreme poverty by 2030, with Sub-Saharan Africa facing the highest increase in the poverty rate.

Warming in Sub-Saharan Africa, which has 95% of rainfed agriculture, is expected to be higher than the global average, and many regions of the continent are expected to get less rainfall. With Africa’s rate of population growth, it is unlikely that food supply will keep up with demand. Africa’s reliance on agriculture for GDP growth and employment adds to the continent’s vulnerability to the effects of climate change, as does other weather-sensitive activities, such as herding and fishing, leading to income loss and increased food insecurity.

### Evolution of precipitation patterns (left) and temperature patterns (right) in Africa

McKinsey predicts that by 2050, nearly the entire African continent will be experiencing at least a 2.6-3.0°C temperature increase, and some regions in Northern and Southern Africa are projected to exceed increases of 3.5°C. McKinsey also projects that drought severity will evolve to be more pervasive by 2050.

**Source:** “How will African farmers adjust to changing patterns of precipitation?” McKinsey Global Institute, 2020.

**Note:** Climate projections based on RCP 8.5 CO2 concentration—a high emissions scenario that reflects no further decarbonization.

## 2. In selecting focus countries, the KawiSafi team conducted a rigorous research and analysis exercise considering in particular:

- **I.** Accredited Entity’s expertise and prior experience across different markets in Africa;
- **II.** KawiSafi II’s pipeline of investable opportunities; and
- **III.** Country needs and climate priorities.

This exercise led to the identification of priority countries for KawiSafi II: Cote d'Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda, and Zambia (NOLs obtained as at 15 June 2023); Ghana, Senegal, South Africa (NOLs at advanced stages).

KawiSafi II has further segmented these countries into three “archetypes”, which have different levels of market maturity and investment needs. KawiSafi II intends to pursue a differentiated investment strategy to have mitigation and adaptation impact across each of these different archetypes. The types of investment in each country will vary depending on the archetype and accompanying market conditions.

## 3. In East Africa, which contributes approx. 1.43% of global GHG emissions¹⁵, climate variability will likely have significant impacts on the region. Increasing temperatures and shifting rainfall patterns are already affecting economic growth, livelihoods, food security, health, and ecosystems¹⁶,¹⁷. With a population of over 460 million, the region is one of the world’s most rapidly urbanizing areas; however, 70% of the population still live in rural areas where people rely heavily on rainfed agriculture. Projections indicate that reduced crop productivity in much of the region is expected to increase poverty and food insecurity.

Warming temperatures and changing precipitation patterns are expected to decrease subsistence and cash crop production, which remain a critical source of livelihood and economic activity across the region. Increasingly severe flooding and drought, in addition to rising evaporation rates and shifting rainfall patterns, will likely diminish East

## Country selection framework

We selected and prioritized the focus countries based on three criteria:

- **54 African countries**
- **Acumen’s expertise**
- **Prior investing experience and in-country relationships that KawiSafi can leverage (e.g., Energy Access Relief Fund, Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund)**
- **KawiSafi’s pipeline**
- **Existing pipeline and market knowledge based on our five years of experience investing in the region**
- **Country needs and climate priorities**
- **A diverse and complement set of countries that allow for knowledge transfer and scaling within the region**

Examples of how some of these countries needs align with the KawiSafi Fund II mandate are highlighted below.

## Kenya

Droughts and floods are the main climate hazards in Kenya, whereas variable rainfall is threatening the stability of hydropower, the country’s major electricity source. The economy is highly dependent on climate sensitive sectors such as agriculture, energy, manufacturing, and tourism. Climate change impact has caused considerable losses across the country’s different sectors over recent years; economic losses resulting from droughts and floods are estimated at 3% of Kenya’s Gross Domestic Product (GDP). Kenya’s total GHG emissions in 2017 were 49.7 MtCO2e with the energy sector accounting for 63% of emissions. In its updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC), submitted in December 2020, the country increased its ambition to abate emissions by 32% (up from 30%) by 2030 relative to the business as usual (BAU) scenario. Kenya has committed to bear 21% of the mitigation costs (US$3.7B) and 10% of the adaptation cost (US$4.4B) and would require international support for the remaining $53.9B implementation costs. Among the priority mitigation activities include use of clean, efficient, and sustainable energy technologies to reduce over reliance on fossil and non-sustainable biomass fuels, enhancing energy efficiency, achieving a tree cover of 10% of land area and clean transport. Adaptation focus includes promotion of climate smart agriculture. In Kenya, the agriculture sector in particular is highly susceptible to the vagaries of weather, including temperature increase, precipitation changes, and extreme events. Dry weather conditions in 2017 led to a decline in the production of most agricultural commodities, and an increase in the number of livestock slaughtered as farmers and pastoralists tried to cushion their losses. Recurring droughts have forced an estimated 30% of livestock owners out of pastoralism in the past 20 years. Fisher communities report that fish are moving from in-shore to deeper waters and artisanal fisher communities lack the technologies to safely fish in deeper waters. Adaptation actions become a priority, to maintain or increase production and enhanced resilience of the agriculture systems. KawiSafi II intends to invest in resilient technologies such as solar irrigation, that support increased agricultural productivity, increased water harvesting & storage.

Uganda also relies significantly on biomass energy. Currently only 5.9% of the population has access to clean cooking energy sources, which has led to a significant rate of deforestation in the last decade. From 2002 to 2020, it is estimated that Uganda lost 7.6% of its total tree cover. Two-wheeler motorcycles or “boda-bodas” have a high prevalence in cities such as Kampala (estimated to have over 200,000 riders). Motorcycles are both most prevalent vehicle type and the fastest growing vehicle category according to estimates from the Ministry of Transport and Works. ## Uganda's Adaptation Needs and Climate Vulnerability

In terms of the program's responsiveness to Uganda's adaptation needs, there is strong alignment with Uganda's final (2022) NDC, which states that priority adaptation actions for the energy sector include promoting renewable energy and energy efficiency technologies, and improving access to and utilization of electricity from sustainable sources.

Furthermore, Uganda's NDC highlights a commitment to promoting efficient mobility within cities in their priority adaptation actions for cities and the built environment.

Looking at resilience to climate change, Uganda is vulnerable to rising temperatures and more infrequent and unpredictable rainfall patterns. Changing rainfall patterns in Uganda are expected to increase the incidence and severity of both droughts and floods. Uganda scores 6.3 in the 2022 INFORM Risk Index which puts it in the 15% of countries most at risk due to drought. The incidence of droughts in Uganda has increased over the last 20 years. The districts in the cattle corridor from Western and Central to mid-Norther and Eastern Uganda are most vulnerable to droughts.

Approximately 75% of the population lives in rural areas and are engaged in some form of agricultural activity, the vast majority relying on rainfed agriculture, with access to modern irrigation equipment standing at below 5% of all farmers. KawiSafi looks to invest in climate resilient technologies in agriculture such as low-carbon irrigation technologies that address lack of water access, that impacting both crop yields and dairy production, improving this sector's adaptive capacity to adverse effects of climate change.

## Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) Energy Access and Climate Vulnerability

Despite efforts in the last decade that improved energy access rates from 11% in 2009 to 19% in 2017, the DRC's 68 million people is the second largest population without access to electricity in Africa (behind only Nigeria). The investment needs of the power sector exceed the government's fiscal capacity, and private capital is needed to meet the funding gap, especially after the COVID-19 pandemic.

If electrification efforts follow the same pace as during the last decade - Business As Usual (BAU) scenario, it is estimated that over 100 million people – or 80% of total population in the DRC will still live without electricity by 2030 and the country will sharply miss its ambitious target of universal access by 2030.

97% of the total energy consumption in DRC comes from renewable energy, 82% of this relates to traditional use of biomass – specifically wood and charcoal with disastrous consequences to the country's forest. It is estimated that DRC has lost approximately 15.9Mha, equivalent to an 8.0% decrease in forest cover since 2000, leading to 9.71Gt of CO₂e emissions.

Furthermore, the high degree of the country's fragility – weak governance, conflict and security issues and poor transport infrastructure – has led to underinvestment in the national grid thereby increasing the urgency of alternative decentralized solutions.

The KawiSafi Fund II programme is also responsive to the DRC's adaptation needs, as it aligns with the NDC's prioritization of energy access as a key adaptation commitment. Climate projections for the coming decade predict an increase in temperatures of 3 to 5 degrees, a decrease in precipitation, and an increase in its variability, as well as an increase in extreme events.

The country's adaptation plan identified the sectors of water resources, forestry, agriculture and the coastal zones as the most vulnerable to the effects of climate change. In the agriculture sector, for example, climate change will likely lead to a reduction in the overall productivity yield due to degradation of conditions needed for optimal plant growth, e.g., increased evaporation due to higher temperatures, reduced rainfall.

Given the sectors dependence on rainfall, this creates a food security risk. KawiSafi II plans to invest in climate smart agriculture technologies that leverage low-carbon technologies such as solar irrigation to improve resilience to the sector.

## Rwanda

While the Country is targeting universal electrification by 2024, it is estimated that over 80% of the country still rely on biomass for cooking leading to significant deforestation in the Country.

KawiSafi Fund II intends to respond to this need by supporting businesses offering cost-competitive scalable clean cooking solutions in the country.

Climate assessments project that the mean annual temperature in Rwanda will increase by 1.4–2.3°C. The duration of heat waves is projected to increase by 7–22 days. The prevalence and intensity of flooding is also expected to increase, causing higher numbers of landslides.

Over the past 30 years, the frequency and intensity of extreme events such as floods and droughts have increased in Rwanda. Floods and landslides were increasingly reported in the high altitude western and northern provinces. These increase vulnerability of key social-economic sectors such as transport given the current state of transport infrastructure. This increased risks in other key sectors such as agriculture (food losses due to lack of access to markets).

KawiSafi’s investment in Climate smart technologies such as transport data analytics that inform key transport network investments, will likely lead to the development of sustainable transport networks and systems that are resilient to adverse climate change effects.

Further, underground transmission cables and transmission infrastructure are prone to damage and undermining from flooding, whilst increased wind speeds can damage transmission lines and pylons. Elevated temperatures and extreme weather events can damage control systems, leading to lack of service.

Around 50% of Rwanda’s electrical power generation comes from small-scale hydropower projects. Central and Southern regions are projected to see a decrease in annual rainfall which will likely result in a decrease in river recharge and reservoir storage.

KawiSafi Fund II intends to increase Rwanda’s adaptive capacity to any of these events by supporting the development of climate proof infrastructure as highlighted in the Country’s Climate Programme launched in 2021.

Across West Africa’s 17 countries, climate change has been referred to as a “threat multiplier” as intensifying heat is expected to contribute to more prolonged droughts and rainfall, which in turn will likely affect land-use patterns for farmers, herders, and their livestock.

The region contributes approx. 2.03% of global GHG emissions, dominated by emissions from Nigeria.

Despite being the largest emitter in the region, less than half of Nigeria’s population has access to grid-connected electricity, and power supply only meets an estimated third of demand creating strong opportunities for off grid solutions.

Climate change is estimated to cost between 6-30% of Nigeria’s GDP by 2050, amounting to between US$100-460 billion, as climate hazards like flooding cause internal displacement and causalities.

The Nigerian government has set various goals including converting 61% of households to using LPG or improved cookstoves, ensuring 30% of on-grid electricity from renewables and increasing/restoring forest cover, among others.

The national policy includes the energy sector as a key target for adaptation as well, articulating a commitment to climate change resilience through the promotion of a diverse energy mix with an increasing proportion of renewables and clean technologies, plus the enhancement of energy efficiency.

KawiSafi intends to increase adaptative capacity of the agriculture sector in Nigeria which is one of the sectors most sensitive to climate change.

Under a BAU scenario, agricultural productivity could decline between 10 and 15% by 2080. In some parts of the North, the decline in yield in rain-fed agriculture could be as much as 50%. ## Senegal

Senegal is the second fastest growing economy in West Africa. In 2015, the country published the "Plan for an Emerging Senegal" (PSE), with sustainable development as one of its key strategic axes. PSE is targeting a reduction of 3.5M tons of Co2e in the next 25 years.

The Senegalese Government’s “National Rural Electrification Program” aims to achieve 100% and 90% energy access in urban and rural areas, respectively, by 2025. The need for clean energy is important, as emissions in the energy sector more than doubled since 2010, overtaking the agricultural sector as the largest emitting sector.

Meanwhile, Senegal is vulnerable to climate hazards, specifically extreme drought, and extreme wet events. Given its special geology (sediment deficit, natural instability of slopes, and surface runoff), rising temperature is a major threat, which could put ~75% of households living in coastal areas at risk.

Current predictions indicate that the average temperatures in Senegal are likely to increase by up to 1.8 degrees Celsius by 2035, while annual precipitation will likely decrease and sea levels will likely continue rising. These changes are already affecting the availability of freshwater.

Climate change risks, including desertification, are predicted to have adverse effects on food security, access to drinking water, public health and livelihoods. An overall decrease in the amount of rainfall, combined with increased variability and intensity, is expected to reduce agricultural yields by 30%.

KawiSafi will invest in low-carbon technologies such as improved irrigation to improve and maintain crop yields, and other rain-dependent farming practices that build resilience to climate change’s adverse effects on the sector.

## Ghana

Ghana is one of Africa’s fastest growing economies and has made significant strides in poverty reduction, but climate variability and change pose a threat to future growth and development. Rising sea levels, drought, higher temperatures, and erratic rainfall negatively impact infrastructure, hydropower production, food security and coastal and agricultural livelihoods.

Increased risk of flooding and soil erosion caused by heavy rainfall events is likely to result in increased repair and maintenance costs for infrastructure. A report by GCA estimates that by 2050 climate risks could cause damage worth $3.9 billion to Ghana’s infrastructure with the greatest risks to water, energy, and transport.

KawiSafi Fund II intends to invest private capital to deploy decentralized renewable energy solutions; as well as increase adaptive capacity of communication and mobility assets that could be critical in disaster management.

Focusing on Agriculture which is the largest employer within the Ghanaian economy suffers the most from climate change. The distribution of rainfall is the single most important affecting the sector. The increased variability of rainfall increases the risks associated with farming as farming becomes almost impossible.

It is projected that rainfall patterns will experience greater variability which will impact crop production and the livelihoods of many in rural areas. In Ghana, Agriculture is predominantly rain-fed, and any changes in rainfall pattern would have serious impact on productivity.

Current projections on climate indicate that rising temperatures and frequent droughts will increase the incidences of bushfires and environmental degradation. The changes in the climatic conditions in the past have deepened rural vulnerability to poverty and enhanced the process of land degradation and desertification. Investments in agriculture are becoming expensive, risky and less profitable.

KawiSafi will invest in low-carbon technologies such as improved irrigation to improve and maintain crop yields, and other rain-dependent farming practices that build resilience to climate change’s adverse effects on the sector.

## Priorities and Climate Change Impact

Priorities include improving affordable electricity access, increasing the use of renewable energy sources, improving energy efficiency, renewing vehicle fleet, and promoting mass transportation. In terms of adaptation, climate change threatens to push almost a million additional Ivorians into extreme poverty and increase the risk of water stress.

Cote d’Ivoire has ambitious mitigation targets, aiming for 98.9% reduction in total GHG emissions compared to the BAU in 2030 (68.5% conditional and 30.4% unconditional), which equates to a mitigation level of up to 650 Mt CO2. The estimated total cost to achieve this is $22 billion.

The future climate projections of Cote d’Ivoire by 2050 show temperature increase in the range of +1.3 to 2.3 degrees, rainfall changes from -2 to +7% with increased frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events, change in length of dry spells from -8 to +1 days and sea level rise from +18 to 45 cm.

Climate risks are recognized in several important sectors of the Côte d’ Ivoire economy: coastal fisheries could see a 26 percent reduction by 2050, substantial loss in surface area suitable for cocoa cultivation due to rising air temperatures, water vulnerability or stress, increased coastal erosion and loss of forest cover due to the use of woods for fire and dependence on charcoal.

The estimates from the World Bank suggest that Côte d’Ivoire could incur losses of CFAF 380 to 770 billion due to climate change between 2040-2100³⁹. With the agriculture sector, human capital, and infrastructure being impacted the most¹⁵.

The agricultural sector is mainly rain-fed and hence particularly vulnerable, with projected reductions in vegetation and decreased ecosystem productivity due to soil degradation, reduction of water availability and droughts between 2015-2100. As a key sector in Ghana, KawiSafi will invest in low-carbon technologies such as improved irrigation to improve and maintain crop yields, and other rain dependent farming practices that build resilience to climate change’s adverse effects on the agriculture sector.

Southern Africa contributes approx. 2.19% to global GHG emissions – 63% of which are driven by South Africa and Angola. Extreme weather events associated with climate change are already affecting livelihoods, economies, and the environment, in turn exacerbating existing vulnerabilities in the region. The region faced its worst drought in 35 years during the 2016 El Nino period.

## South Africa

The country is facing the impact of climate change, with the national average temperature increasing more than twice the rate of global temperature increase since 1990. ## III. The Proposed Solution: KawiSafi Fund II

1. Finding ways to drive a step-change in investment through blended finance structures is critical to achieve climate goals. KawiSafi Fund II intends to allocate $200 million in climate-focused venture capital targeted at supporting inclusive green growth across Africa. The Fund itself will have a blended structure and include a concessional first loss layer to crowd in commercial investors who have historically perceived the region and industry as high-risk. This structure is designed to expand the pool of climate investors into the continent and increase private sector climate financing beyond the traditional multilateral and bilateral development donors. Investing at an inflection point for company growth and scale, KawiSafi Fund II will seek to crowd in equity co-investors in company financing rounds, as well as provide the critical equity layer that allows for additional debt leveraging. KawiSafi Fund I has to date leveraged approximately $580M of additional debt and equity capital to its portfolio companies (13x its investment). Should the success of KawiSafi I be replicated, the new KawiSafi Fund II could unlock over $2B in funding for the continent.

2. Building on KawiSafi Fund I experience supporting the evolution of the off-grid energy ecosystem, KawiSafi Fund II intends to invest in three core sectors namely: (1) Energy Transition; (2) Energy Productivity and (3) Mobility & Logistics. These are highlighted below (and further detail is included in Section B.3 of this document):

### Adaptation impact being targeted: A look at rural electrification in West Africa (Nigeria and Senegal).

#### a. Direct and indirect impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through climate-resilient technologies

### Climate risk vulnerability: Energy Transition in rural electrification

Access to energy is still a major gap, for example in Nigeria and Senegal. Providing access for off-grid households to a clean, modern, self-contained energy source extends significant adaptation benefits to those that are otherwise dependent on traditional, “dirty” fuels. Use of traditional fuels contributes to deforestation and can also result in reliance on volatile prices and supply chain disruptions. Grid infrastructure in certain regions is vulnerable to climate shocks such as floods and other weather events, and therefore more localized, climate-proof generation and distribution infrastructure has an important role to play in securing energy supply to vulnerable communities, as well as improving energy systems recovery. Finally, clean modern energy (such as standalone solar) reduces the health burden on households due to indoor air pollution and respiratory illnesses. Improved health increases resilience in the face of worsening climatic conditions.

### Adaptation Impact thesis: Energy Transition in rural electrification

An investment in climate-resilient energy systems addresses the vulnerability in a climate change context. We anticipate the following impacts from our investment in these technologies:

#### a. Improved recovery and/or resilience of energy system post extreme weather events

#### b. Individuals who benefit from expanded energy access in other ways, for example through access to the internet which facilitates the use of climate information services

#### c. The adaptation co-benefits of more resilient health and education infrastructure, through their improved power supply

#### d. The adaptation benefits of businesses receiving a more reliable power supply, notably the enhanced resilience of their service and the improved reliability of their employment

#### e. The improved resilience of the energy network through the use of more energy-efficient appliances.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 17

## ● Support the adoption of improved and/or new climate-resilient livelihood options (Adaptation)

### Adaptation impact being targeted: A look at the agriculture sector

a. Direct and indirect impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through resilient agriculture technologies that achieve food security as well as health, well-being and water security outcomes

### Climate risk vulnerability: Agriculture sector

Agriculture is one of the sectors most vulnerable to changes in the climate. Across the continent, yields are expected to decline most severely in countries at lower altitudes. Projected losses in production threaten national economies, not to mention threatening food security. In Africa, major grains including wheat, maize and sorghum are projected to suffer mean yield losses of 8% by 2050, with some crops, notably wheat, expected to experience a yield loss of 17%. There is a major need for innovative solutions to offer productive use equipment for small-scale farmers such as solar-powered irrigation systems.

### Adaptation Impact thesis: Agriculture sector

An investment in climate smart agriculture technologies addresses the vulnerability in a climate change context. We anticipate the following impacts from our investment in these productive technologies:

a. Increased crop output (yields per hectare) and dairy productivity (liters per cattle head) compared to baseline scenario despite worsening climatic conditions. Additionally, improved ability for farmers to do multiple crop cycles within a year, given year-round access to water source.

b. Reduction in operating costs, vs. diesel fuel, for those farmers already using diesel powered water pumps. Customer spend is approximately 40% less weekly than for traditional water pumps.

c. Increased income and take-home pay for farmers and their households, by increasing the volume of marketable produce as well as improving quality.

d. Increased produce available in the local market for consumption within vulnerable communities. Enhanced food security and strengthened resilience in the face of drought, hunger, and loss of output.

## 5. Mobility and Logistics: Companies involved in the rapidly evolving electric mobility and logistics markets across Africa

Mitigation and adaptation impacts expected from this segment include:

● Increased deployment of low-emissions transport infrastructure (Mitigation)

● Support increased data collection and efficiency in urban transport and logistics systems – through electrification and investment in innovative shared public mobility business models (Mitigation and Adaptation)

### Adaptation impact being targeted: Transport Sector – Transport network technologies

a. Direct and indirect impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through climate-resilient technologies

b. Direct and indirect impacts on infrastructure and build environments through adoption of sustainable climate-smart mobility technologies.

### Climate risk vulnerability: Transport sector

African urban and intercity transport systems are vulnerable to multiple types of climate impact: sea level rise and storm surge, higher frequency and intensity of extreme wind and storm events, increased precipitation intensity, extreme heat and fire hazard, overall warming, and change in average precipitation patterns. The increased frequency and intensity of extreme climate event challenges the year-round availability of critical transport services: roads are damaged more often or are more costly to maintain; expensive infrastructure assets such as ports, railways or airports can be damaged by storms and storm surges, resulting in a short-life cycle and capacity than they were originally designed for. Critical infrastructure such as bridges continue to be built based on data and disaster risk patterns from decades ago, ignoring the current trend of increased climate risk. For Sub-Saharan Africa alone, it is estimated that climate change will threaten to increase road maintenance costs by 270% if no action is taken. The transport data gap in sub-Saharan Africa is massive. In many developing countries, even the largest cities may not have the capacity to collect any transport data or provide basic information to users, such as public transit maps or schedules. This makes it difficult for residents to optimize their journeys and makes it even harder for local authorities to plan and operate efficient transit networks.

# Transport Infrastructure and Climate Adaptation

## Adaptation Impact Thesis: Transport Sector

An investment in climate smart mobility technologies addresses the vulnerability in a climate change context. We anticipate the following impacts from our investment in these productive technologies:

1. **Vulnerable populations** will have better access to early warning systems and emergency response measures as they pertain to the transport network (e.g., not to travel by certain modes of transport, districts/boroughs to avoid, where evacuation channels are most congested, etc.)

2. **Urban populations** will have access to real-time information about optimal transport routes and transport networks. The optimization of the transport infrastructure will improve its affordability and efficiency, thereby improving its accessibility for users from vulnerable populations.

3. **Public service providers** will have better ability to offer emergency healthcare and other critical public services as through the technologies they will be able to identify routes of high-demand and to serve them efficiently.

4. **Municipal and local governments** will be better able to monitor transport infrastructure in real-time, responding to maintenance needs quickly and ensuring infrastructure is climate-proof. Data-driven approaches to transport maintenance enable preventative maintenance and more cost-effective maintenance to limit the damage caused by extreme weather events.

5. **Municipal and local governments** are able to plan transport networks more efficiently to reduce congestion and ensure good access to exit and evacuation routes – critical in the face of higher probability of extreme weather events.

## Definition of Fund II’s strategy in terms of segments and ticket size as well as formed key criteria considered in the prioritization of the countries of focus across Africa.

2. KawiSafi Fund I's ticket sizes were $500k-$6M and investments were at late venture / early growth stage; whereas KawiSafi Fund II’s ticket sizes aims to be $1M-$10M but investments will still be at late venture / early growth stage. In other words, KawiSafi II intends to invest across the same stages but will have the ability to invest slightly larger tickets, which our experience and research shows is required by the market. With respect to the Energy Transition vertical, KawiSafi II seeks to continue to invest in renewable energy businesses, including residential solar, commercial & industrial solar, battery storage, and other businesses as referenced in the Eligibilty Criteria below.

3. KawiSafi II through its themes, structure and investment instruments is designed to complement existing climate financing projects, as follows:

### Private sector led: KawiSafi II differentiates itself from existing interventions such as the Global Environment Facility and the Climate Investment Fund as it is a private sector Fund complementing Public Sector energy transition efforts by meeting the dual challenge of 1) leveraging catalytic capital to unlock private and institutional capital towards the development of decarbonized, distributed energy infrastructure in Africa; and 2) providing critical essential services to underserved populations on the continent. While public sector initiatives are critical to achieving the goals of the Paris Accord through significant large-scale interventions such as South Africa’s COP26 hailed deal where the country received $8.5B to help end its reliance on coal, private sector initiatives ensure that these are sustainable by developing profitable, scalable business models that also encourage economic growth. There is a significant market need in this segment as highlighted above – with the KawiSafi team identifying $1.98B in potential deal value from over 200 pipeline opportunities across energy transition; energy productivity and clean mobility segments in Africa.

### Equity investments: KawiSafi Fund II will seek to identify replicable business models in these segments and invest equity financing which support further leveraging in their capital structure. This should be a complement to other GCF funded projects such as the Universal Green Energy Access Programme (FP027) and the AfDB Leveraging Energy Access Finance Framework (FP168) which provide subordinated debt, receivables financing, credit enhancement guarantees and technical assistance to support growth of distributed renewable energy projects and impact. Both types of financing are critical to increasing the pools of financing (including local currency financing) that is available; as well as improving the risk profile of low carbon investments in Africa.

### Supported by focused (“smart”) TA grants: KawiSafi II is raising a technical assistance facility (the “TAF”) alongside KawiSafi Fund II to complement its equity investments with focused grants to both (i) enhance the impact of its portfolio and (ii) support development of the ecosystem for high-impact climate solutions in Africa. KawiSafi II is well-positioned to implement the TAF because it is an impact investor with a track record of success and established relationships in the focus markets. The TAF will likely be additive and complementary to the work of existing TA facilities and expects to leverage regional and local partnerships to drive successful implementation, deliver value for money, and ensure country ownership.

## V. Alignment with GCF’s Private Sector Strategy

KawiSafi Fund II is closely aligned to the key priorities set out in GCF’s Private Sector Strategy (19 May 2022):

# B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

## 1. KawiSafi II aims to address the twin challenges of poverty and climate change.

In Sub-Saharan Africa, people lack access to essential goods and services while facing increasing risk and volatility driven by climate change. Investing in innovative companies that increase access to energy, productivity, and transport directly addresses these challenges. The target companies directly offer essential goods and services to individuals and businesses, while also supporting increased employment and access to opportunity. Lack of access to these essential goods and services has resulted in income inequalities which historically have had the poor suffer the indignity of poverty wages and denied basic rights – especially amongst women and girls.

## 2. The KawiSafi Fund II theory of change is centered around the following paradigm shift:

- **IF** the KawiSafi Fund II unlocks capital for low-carbon productive businesses,
- **THEN** the ecosystem for investment in and growth of such businesses should be catalyzed and accelerated, resulting in adoption of clean-smart technologies at scale, reduced GHG emissions, and strengthened adaptation to climate change,
- **BECAUSE** KawiSafi II’s demonstration effect of the financial and impact potential should lead to more available funding and more low-carbon productive businesses emerging in Africa.

The theory of change addresses the key market barriers and risks as follows:

### Barrier 1: Limited availability of flexible equity capital for late venture/ early growth companies in low-carbon and climate-smart sectors.

Limited access to finance is one of the most significant barriers hindering the transfer of necessary low-carbon and climate resilient technologies in Africa. With a limited universe of credit-worthy, grid-tied projects, the distributed energy sector emerged to meet consumer needs. However, these companies do not benefit from the same long-term contracts and risk mitigation strategies. Instead, they either rely on many micro-payments from individuals in the residential segment, or contracts with SMEs in the C&I segment, which require targeted sources of capital able to assess the unique risks involved.

There is a need for late venture and early growth equity capital to catalyze capital crowd-in. There is not enough local institutional capital available with a mandate to invest in green projects. There is an urgent need for innovative financial solutions to de-risk institutional investors, pool funds at scale and provide the much-needed equity capital for renewable energy companies in Africa.

# Barrier 2: Nascency of many clean technologies in focus countries and lack of both consumer awareness and technical expertise

Many of the critical technologies required to facilitate low-carbon transition are still at a nascent stage in Africa, e.g., in the area of renewables mini-grids, energy storage and electrical vehicle charging infrastructure. Similarly, the lack of proven models, technical capacity, knowledge gaps and limited consumer awareness hinder scaling the sector, especially with regards to de-risking projects through appropriate capital structuring, institutionalizing best practices, operational and governance standards as well as innovative business models—all things that are necessary to design and scale up commercial feasibility of projects and maturation of the sector. Investing in high-quality management teams and facilitating technical support (through the TA) intends to serve to address this gap.

# Barrier 3: Limited understanding of link between climate mitigation and adaptation, how to integrate adaptation practices into target sectors, understanding of methods and data tools for tracking

Across the continent, governments have notably initiated mitigation and adaptation target setting in line with their NDC efforts towards reducing emissions and adapting to the impacts to climate change. However there remains a need for further dialogue linking the integration of mitigation and adaptation practices into target sectors that could further increase attractiveness of these nascent, climate-focused sectors. Providing technical assistance alongside equity investing into these sectors should support enhancing climate and ESG impact through learnings, best practices and innovations. These efforts should deepen adaptation impacts, and promote best practices in climate impact knowledge and data.

# Barrier 4: Technical and STEM-related climate technologies are often gender exclusive and women are less represented

While energy is a critical enabler in reaching development goals and improving social well-being, the benefits of increased access to modern and cleaner energy services often fail to accrue evenly to men and women. The energy industry has significant gender disparities in terms of employment participation, control over decisions, assets and resources, capacity, and skills. Therefore, there exists an opportunity to close these identified gender gaps, through the provision of technical assistance to bridge gender inclusivity and transformation in climate smart solutions. These should in turn improve the resilience of women to adverse effects of climate change.

# 3. KawiSafi Fund II will seek to address these barriers as follows:

First, through investment of risk capital in the scaling of businesses providing renewable distributed energy and storage solutions, KawiSafi Fund II will seek to support the rapid acceleration of supply of these essential products and services to millions of people which is expected to in turn demonstrate the technological suitability of these products and commercial viability of these businesses. KawiSafi’s investment expects to be critical to crowding in additional capital for these companies which will likely not only avert carbon emissions by displacing fossil fuels but also unlock significant productivity, employment, and well-being benefits in these communities.

For example, investment in a company providing energy storage solutions that replace Back Up Generators (BUGs) in Nigeria will seek to not only avert carbon emissions from diesel replacement, but also prove the technology creating a new skill base and generating employment in this new supply chain with more opportunities for women; increase reliability of power for users and reduce air pollution when deployed at scale.

Second, to accelerate the shift to low emission transport, KawiSafi Fund II will seek to supplement public sector efforts by investing in segments ripe for private sector intervention such as two and three-wheeler EVs and associated charging. This capital will seek to support the roll out of new technology, validate innovative financing business models being piloted to unlock affordability and access to electric transport in Africa. At scale, these technologies are expected to drive down emissions from transport that are estimated to account for nearly a quarter of all energy related CO2 emissions.

The innovative EV financing structures such as Battery as a Service (BaaS) will likely also reduce the total cost of ownership of these products and increase residual income for riders which is expected to generate new employment opportunities in the sector.

Further, KawiSafi Fund II will seek to invest in businesses that utilize innovative pay as you go models to build agricultural infrastructure that supports income generation and increases smallholder farmers adaptive capacity to the effects of climate change. Increasing access to these products will likely also empower smallholder farmers (who in most countries of focus have a larger female representation) will likely empower these women to expand income generating activities – for instance through value addition from solar powered grinding or milling of farm produce.

Given the nascency of this segment, KawiSafi Fund II activities will seek

To meet this demand sustainably, Africa must prioritize reducing its greenhouse gas emissions, particularly those related to energy production and consumption. One way to achieve this is through the implementation of renewable energy and energy storage solutions, which can significantly reduce energy-related emissions. Additionally, Africa must address emissions from transport and logistics, which can be accomplished through the use of electron and shared mobility solutions, as well as greening private fleets. Finally, Africa can reduce household and industrial GHG emissions through the adoption of “green productivity” solutions, including energy efficiency, sustainable agriculture, water and energy conservation, waste reduction, and environmentally friendly materials. ## Outcome 4: Improved resilience of lower-income populations due to better adaptability to climate change

The resilience of lower-income populations is improved, through access to more resilient energy and transport infrastructure, strengthened food security, and access to a broader range of reliable climate-proof opportunities and tools for employment, income generation and economic opportunities. A co-benefit of the improved resilience is the increase and support for work opportunities for men and women.

As described in section B.1. above, the region faces severe climate risks including increased drought/extreme rains; exposure to extreme heat; increased weather volatility. By investing in the target energy and climate solutions, the businesses served will likely be more resilient to these shocks and can better serve low-income customers. In this context it is imperative to develop businesses that enable populations to diversify their incomes either by expanding climate sensitive factors or by promoting adaptation measures that increase the resilience of these communities.

As an example, investment in cold chain technologies has the potential to improve food security through the reduction of fresh produce wastage as a result of spoilage and post-harvest losses, as exemplified by Fund I investee company InspiraFarms. Cooling technology unlocks farmer/traders’ ability to optimize market access, while for the health care sector, it improves the access to essential and lifesaving medicines and vaccines in the hardest to reach areas. Investment in productive use technologies should create more resilient communities in some of the most underserved areas of the continent.

## Outcome 5: Enabling Environment: Strengthened ecosystem for low-carbon productive businesses, technology & innovation, and gender-inclusive climate solutions

By moving towards financial sustainability, businesses have available to them additional capital for investment in R&D and technological innovations, catalyzing the development of new low-carbon technologies to support the energy transition. Further business sustainability is expected to increase employment, income, and gender-inclusive opportunities for low-income people through low carbon businesses.

IRENA estimates that 26 million jobs can be created in the sector by 2050, with increased investment in renewable energy. By 2022/23, it is estimated that the climate tech sector could support 1.3 million FTE jobs across 4 major regions: South Asia (740K), Eastern Africa (350K), West Africa (150K) and Central Africa (70K), with women-led roles are expected to make up ~27% of these jobs.

According to Forbes, for every direct job created in the renewable energy sector, ~5 more are created in the broader economy (indirect jobs). Increased investment in this sector is expected to increase development in the region by 6.4% by 2050. # B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
## 1. KawiSafi Fund II is a climate focused fund, sponsored by Acumen Fund and focused on low carbon technologies that meet the essential needs of growing African markets. The fund seeks to make a significant step-change in impact by investing at the nexus of climate and energy poverty. Its goal is to reduce vulnerability and strengthen resilience of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly to effects of climate change and avert 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq.

## 2. The Management team builds directly from its experience of deploying its predecessor KawiSafi Fund I – a late venture stage equity fund focused on the fast-growing off-grid, distributed renewables ecosystem in East Africa. Areas where we can build from the successes of KawiSafi Fund I notably include the development and maturation of the off-grid energy sector (e.g., standalone solar, renewables mini-grids, C&I solar) since Fund I launch in 2016. The investment strategy for Fund I was explicitly intended to support the off-grid energy ecosystem across the value chain. The Fund I portfolio was constructed in line with that mandate and represents some of the strongest companies across the off-grid ecosystem. Now that the sector has grown and evolved so substantially, the investment focus for KawiSafi Fund II reflects the new landscape for off-grid innovation, building from the current investments. In addition, the KawiSafi Fund I strategy was very focused on East Africa, namely Kenya and Rwanda. We have observed how the leading companies expanded beyond their initial markets into broader regional opportunities. Within our own portfolio, d.light, Bboxx and BioLite started from original core markets in Kenya and Rwanda and expanded into additional countries including DRC, Nigeria, Senegal, Uganda and Zambia, among others. The business models are tailored to local needs in each case, with distinct partners for certain markets. The learnings from those portfolio successes have informed our geographic focus for Fund II. We also note the strong performance on lives impacted and carbon emissions averted in Fund I. We seek to build on those impacts as well as continue to evolve our impact approach for Fund II. While residential solar has driven strong people impacts, we see the need to deepen our measurement approach for other types of investments. We also embrace more rigorous measurement of adaptation and resilience benefits. We have demonstrated strong mitigation impacts across renewable energy applications and seek to continue that focus and drive for more ambitious targets.

## 3. KawiSafi Fund II will likely also benefit in learning from the challenges and obstacles that Fund I faced. We note that Fund I received backing from supporters of our impact mission but did not benefit from wide institutional investment backing. We seek to broaden our institutional investor base and bring additional types of capital into the sector. The first loss capital in Fund II is designed to provide much-needed risk protection for a broader set of institutional investors. Fund II is sized so that we can continue to follow on and avoid dilution in fast-growing companies. Finally, we note that our companies face a wide range of challenges, while they build their companies, from managing integrated business models, operational

## Challenges of Product Development and Distribution, Team Challenges, Capacity for ESG

We seek to build on our strong Fund I TA program to offer a broader set of supports to allow companies to incorporate best practices across their activities.

## KawiSafi Fund II Focus Areas

4. KawiSafi Fund II is focused on three thematic verticals representing the current opportunity for innovation and impact, building from the KawiSafi Fund I portfolio and universe of investment activity. These are:

### Energy Transition

Firms delivering on global and national energy transition strategies through rapid deployment of low-carbon renewable power generation in renewable and commercial and industrial (“C&I”) markets, renewables-based mini- and metro- grids, financial services, grid management/edge ecosystem/ Internet of Things (IoT), and waste-to-energy circular economy models.

### Productivity

Firms focusing on low-carbon productivity and efficiency markets including low-cost internet access, “smart” green manufacturing, recycling / circular economy, clean cooling, and agro-processing technology.

### Mobility & Logistics

Firms involved in the rapidly evolving mobility & logistics markets where unique electric vehicle battery business models and artificial intelligence are driving rapid innovation in low-income markets and creating more climate-resilient, low-emission transportation systems.

## Investment Stage and Ticket Size

5. KawiSafi Fund II will focus on late-venture / early-growth stage companies with ticket sizes of $2-$10 million and on its core expertise of scaling businesses. The Management team sees the company-building role of Fund II to be particularly important as markets recover and restructure in the context of a post-COVID-19 world. The larger fund and ticket size reflect the evolution of the investment landscape in Africa since Fund I was launched, with more investable businesses, sharper growth curves, and greater need for capital deployment. It also reflects KawiSafi II's thesis that some of its portfolio companies are expected to exceed performance expectations and we should selectively back them with larger follow-on investments. Fund II is expected to remain anchored investing in companies that enable low-carbon growth while expanding to a wider geographical mandate with a focus on 10 African markets.

## Program Design

6. The program is uniquely designed to address financial, technical, policy and knowledge gaps hindering the uptake of innovative technologies and business models required for the transition to low carbon and climate resilient pathways in Africa. In particular, the program consists of two components (I) Equity and (II) Technical Assistance Facility. The program is in line with NDCs as outlined in section B.1.

### Component 1: Equity Investment into KawiSafi II

1. KawiSafi II is a $200M Equity Fund to leverage private and institutional capital at the fund level for further investment into low-carbon projects in Africa. The key output of the program is long-term investment of Equity capital in (1) Energy Transition, (2) Productivity, (3) Mobility & Logistics projects with strong climate impact and innovation potential. These sectors correspond to the following GCF result areas (as set out in detail in the theory of change and logframe):

- Energy access and power generation (MRA1)
- Low-emission transport (MRA2)
- Buildings, cities, industries and appliances (MRA3)
- Most vulnerable people and communities (ARA1)
- Health, well-being, food and water security (ARA2)

2. Investment Pipeline: The KawiSafi Fund I experience demonstrated the market opportunity for investments in the target sectors. In KawiSafi Fund II's investment pipeline, the KawiSafi team has developed an indicative advanced pipeline of 40+ investment opportunities with an aggregate deal value of $500 million across our focus verticals. The Fund is however tracking a larger pipeline pool of ~200 companies with aggregate deal value of $2 billion across the 10 focus markets.

## 3. Portfolio composition:
KawiSafi Fund II will invest $1-10 million per company, in an expected 15-20 companies. No single exposure will exceed 20% of the total committed capital. In line with private equity best industry practices, KawiSafi Fund II has appropriate limits on investments and industry concentration to ensure risk diversification.

## 4. Financial instruments:
KawiSafi Fund II shall invest a mix of equity, equity linked instruments (including warrants and preference shares) as well as convertible and/or non-convertible securities depending on the investment structure, efficiency for upstreaming capital and tax optimization.

## 5. Geographies:
KawiSafi Fund II will invest only in companies that have a substantial sales or operating presence in one or more of the focus countries where NOLs have been obtained: Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda, Zambia (NOLs secured as at 15th June 2023).58 KawiSafi Fund II will not exceed exposure of more than 30% of investable capital in any single country.

## 7. Minimum threshold criteria by Technology/Sub-sector  

KawiSafi Fund II applies certain minimum thresholds for a company to be eligible for investment which are specific to different technologies / sub-sectors:  

- **Solar PV installations**: KawiSafi II may invest in solar installations of different capacities. These solar installations will likely use as back-up either an accompanying battery storage system, and/or be connected to the national grid, and/or use diesel generator as back-up provided that the solar installation aims to reduce diesel consumption at site by more than 50%. This criterion recognizes that solar installations require back-up energy to cover peak loads and it is not always economically viable to install battery storage at site. GCF funds will not be used to finance fossil fuel assets, including financing of new diesel gen-sets as back-up for solar PV installations and mini-grids.  

- **Energy efficiency**: KawiSafi II may invest in companies selling energy efficient devices/services. In such cases, KawiSafi will only proceed with an investment where the improvements in energy efficiency are at least 20% efficiency gains, against a conservative baseline. The KawiSafi team will conduct comprehensive due diligence prior to investment in order to assess the appropriate baseline and validate the potential of the company’s product/service to deliver the minimum threshold efficiency gains.  

- **Clean cook technology**: KawiSafi may invest in companies manufacturing, financing, and/or distributing clean cookstoves and/or producing, distributing clean fuels for household cooking or industrial use. In such cases, KawiSafi II will only invest in companies with renewable fuel technologies, such as improved biomass, biogas, electric, and biofuels. Kaw iSafi II will not invest

## 8. Excluded activities

KawiSafi Fund II invests exclusively in low-risk and high impact companies in the focus verticals, and above related technologies / sub-sectors. In doing so, KawiSafi Fund II applies the IFC exclusion list and will not invest in companies that have investments or activities in any of the excluded areas. The full exclusion list is found in Annex 6: ESMS.

## 9. Investment process

In selecting and diligencing companies in which to invest, KawiSafi Fund II will apply a rigorous screening approach to assess eligibility. Below is a summary of KawiSafi’s investment process and a highlight on the screening and assessment criteria:

### 9.1 Fossil-fuel based cooking technologies

KawiSafi Fund II will not invest in companies that use fossil-fuel based cooking technologies, such as LPG.

### 9.2 Cold chain storage & cooling solutions

KawiSafi II is aligned with the transition to low global warming potential refrigerants. Any cold chain storage companies which KawiSafi II invests in will be aligned with the goals and requirements of the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, under which hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs) are being phased down and replaced with climate-friendly refrigerants that do less damage to the climate.

As such, investments supported by KawiSafi II will promote the use of climate-friendly cooling equipment/appliances commercially available for the cooling applications that either:

- (i) provide cooling services without the use of refrigerants (e.g. fans and passive cooling solutions); and/or
- (ii) use refrigerants that are aligned with the Kigali amendment and meet relevant safety standards, as commercially available in respective local markets.

### 9.3 Biofuels

KawiSafi may invest in companies producing biofuels for household/industrial use or for alternative transport systems. In such cases, KawiSafi will only invest in companies using the following as feedstock:

- (i) Waste oil/fat;
- (ii) Seeds or crops that are cultivated in dedicated plantations;
- (iii) Biomass residues (e.g. agricultural residues, wood residues, organic wastes).

KawiSafi II will only invest in such companies where their activities do not contribute to deforestation or loss of biodiversity i.e. the feedstock is sustainably sourced and does not contribute to GHG emissions.

In making such assessment, KawiSafi II will consider the criteria set out in CDM methodologies ACM0017: Production of biofuel and AMS-III.AK.: Biodiesel production and use for transport applications.

### 9.4 Asset finance / Financing platforms

KawiSafi II includes in its mandate companies that offer financing solutions into the KawiSafi II verticals (energy transition, productivity, mobility), recognizing the critical importance of asset finance to unlock growth in low-carbon sectors.

At the time of investment, where KawiSafi II invests in such a financing company, it will be contractually agreed that such financing shall be used to fund activities that are in line with KawiSafi II’s climate mandate.

It should be noted that KawiSafi cannot control what such financing companies may or may not finance at an indeterminate point in the future (after the KawiSafi II investment has been used by the company in line with KawiSafi II’s climate mandate).

## KawiSafi II’s investment process

KawiSafi II’s investment process is guided by three main policies:  
1. **Impact Policy**, Impact Measurement and Management Procedures  
2. **Responsible Investing Policy**  
3. **ESMS** (included in Annex 6)

These policies elaborate how the KawiSafi team evaluates and ensures positive climate impact and the approach to managing environmental, social and governance (ESG) risks and performance in its investments.

KawiSafi II will invest in exclusively low- to medium-risk and high-impact low carbon projects. KawiSafi II applies the IFC Exclusion List, and has created a list of Excluded investment activities that the KawiSafi II will use to guide their investment process. This Exclusion list is included in Annex 6: ESM

The KawiSafi team will not invest in a company or will accept investment from investors with business activity that is included in the list. The KawiSafi team will use the exclusion list as a screening tool for investments.

## Screening and Initial diligence

In this stage, KawiSafi II will identify investment opportunities and select them based on their ability to achieve and align with the stated impact and financial goals of the fund.

### Financial/Commercial assessment

KawiSafi assesses the information provided by the company against the team's market knowledge and research. This will include assessing the business model for aspects such as unit economics, management team experience, fit for the local market, potential for regional expansion, poverty and climate impact. We will also assess the viability of the business based on strength of existing infrastructure, for example the coverage of battery charging infrastructure to support EV business models.

### Climate/Impact assessment

Impact assessment at KawiSafi II is guided by three main policies: Impact Policy, Impact Measurement and Management procedures, Responsible Investing Policy and the ESMS (included in Annex 6). Assessment of the climate impacts of potential investments takes place during the ‘initial DD” and “Impact assessment” stages.

Fund must demonstrate i) their potential to make a significant contribution to KawiSafi II’s two overarching climate targets:  
1. Avoiding / averting 20.9 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent during Fund II life cycle  
2. Strengthening resilience and adaptation for 3.5M individuals

KawiSafi shall assess each firm against the following to assess their eligibility:

### a) Mitigation impacts

Aligned with the KawiSafi II methodology (leveraging global standards such as CDM methodology) to calculate emissions reduction impacts, potential investments are assessed for their mitigation potential. Financial projections are used together with the KawiSafi II mitigation calculation methodology to estimate potential future mitigation impacts. Investments are then categorized as having High, Medium or Low mitigation potential. Decisions to progress will be based on the outcomes of this assessment.

### b) Adaptation impacts

The KawiSafi team will conduct an assessment of each investment

## Opportunity’s Potential for Adaptation Impacts
