## Businesses that meet the threshold scores pass to the next stage of the investment process (“preliminary investment memo”). Through the screening and due diligence (DD) process, KawiSafi seeks to guide impact investment decision making and identify areas where it is able to strengthen financial and impact management within the investee company.

## Investment Approach

1. The team’s experience leading transactions and managing investments has generated important insights in identifying investment targets, negotiating appropriate terms, and managing investments to scale and ultimately exit.
2. Identify firms with potential to become market leaders – Seek companies with demonstrated traction and replicability in their markets (product-market fit, proven technology) that are poised to scale with KawiSafi’s capital and partnership. Back management teams with maturity, drive and commitment that have genuine vision to build a regional/global leader and a demonstrated ability to attract low-cost capital.
3. Disciplined valuation and deal execution – KawiSafi’s transaction negotiation strategy is backed by insights from a unique private transaction database based on its direct exposure to ~200 private investment opportunities in Africa. The investment team focuses on the terms that matter and takes a pragmatic approach to valuation, using contingent / variable mechanisms to bridge any gaps in expectations when the opportunity warrants it. The team led 9 of 10 of its investments from Fund I so is used to developing its own theses around how to value a company (rather than rely on others’) and also had to make difficult decisions to drop out of deals when the terms became unfavorable.
4. Support portfolio maturation – KawiSafi II team members bring deep market and operational expertise in de-risking companies for impact at scale, having extensive experience as operators, investors, and advisors in the Fund’s focus markets. The KawiSafi II team plays a hands-on role in portfolio engagement and provides tailored support to its investees focusing time and resource where its marginal value-add is greatest. The team’s Board representatives play an active role post-investment with a focus on running an effective board, ensuring management has strategic focus, sourcing talent from our network, and making connections to institutional capital.
5. Actively pursue exits – Lack of liquidity is a key factor in the African investment ecosystem – the market is evolving, but exits are still a challenge for all investors. As such, KawiSafi II takes a pro-active approach to assessing and creating opportunities for liquidity. It is one of the key lenses through which the KawiSafi II team evaluates opportunities: Who is going to acquire this asset and why? What would they need to believe to do so? What metrics would the company need to hit? How would such a buyer (strategic or other) value the business? We develop exit scenario plans for our investees and discuss/revise these accordingly on a frequent basis. We use our Board participation to push the agenda for exit options and ensure that the investee is doing everything it can to position itself as attractive to target acquirers.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 39

## Outcome 1: Reduced energy-related GHG emissions from decentralized renewable energy solutions, including solar, wind, hydro, and biofuels

In Africa, the energy transition equation is unique. More than 40% of the population still lacks access to the grid and per capita energy consumption is on average only 1/35th of energy consumption in Europe. Unaffordable and inadequate household energy presents adverse consequences that are amplified by poverty and a changing climate. African countries need to significantly increase their power output in the coming decades to meet the demands of a growing population and raise standards of living.

This presents an opportunity for an accelerated energy transition. Africa has already shown a rapid deployment of renewable energy generation over the past decade. Many African countries are now generating a majority of power from renewable sources and have introduced favorable policies to promote adoption of renewable energy by households and businesses alike. This is expected to have significant mitigation and adaptation impacts across Africa, specifically:

- Increase energy access through investment in decentralized renewable energy for residential use (Mitigation and Adaptation)
- Increase access for reliable, low carbon energy for industrial growth in Africa (Mitigation)
- Support reduction in changes in land use – especially deforestation (Mitigation)
- Support development of climate-resilient infrastructure (Adaptation)

## Outcome 2: Reduced transport and logistics-related GHG emissions from electric and shared mobility solutions, and greening of private fleets

Mobility and Logistics together comprise the "distributed infrastructure" that moves people and goods enabling market transactions and allowing economies to flourish. The World Bank’s Logistics Performance Index ranks Sub-Saharan Africa below all other regions, with particular challenges in infrastructure and logistics competence. This creates a drain on most people in Africa and particularly on poorer populations who suffer disproportionately from increased pricing of essential goods and constraints on individual mobility.

New business models incorporating advanced data and sensor technology as well as more efficient vehicles create opportunities to improve service and increase coverage while reducing costs.

- Increased deployment of low-emissions transport infrastructure (Mitigation)
- Support increased data collection and efficiency in urban transport and logistics systems – through electrification and investment in innovative shared public mobility business models (Mitigation and Adaptation)

## Outcome 3: Reduced household & industrial GHG emissions from "green productivity" solutions, including energy efficiency, agriculture-water-energy, waste solutions, materials

"Productivity" here focuses on the ways in which greater renewable energy and clean technology access for households and enterprises can be harnessed to help grow economies and reduce poverty across Africa. Productive use appliances can be transformative from an income generation perspective, particularly for the poor, it presents income diversification benefits from economic activities that are vulnerable to climate change, for instance farmers adopting solar irrigation to become more resilient to adverse weather patterns affecting rainfall patterns.

The World Bank estimates that over 80% of Africa's employment is generated in the informal sector – through micro-entrepreneurs and small enterprises serving poor and emerging middle-income consumers. At the same time, 70% of businesses across Africa report power-outages as having a significant impact on productivity and pay high costs for back-up power. There is a widening Productivity Gap in the region today because, whilst access to energy is increasing, businesses and individuals often lack the opportunity to utilize this energy for productive ends.

The productivity gap is a challenge across agricultural, commercial, and industrial sectors where energy access can be harnessed with the right technologies to create value. Mitigation and adaptation impacts expected from this segment include:

- Increase energy efficiency of manufacturing and other energy intensive industries in Africa (Mitigation)
- Support for development of low-carbon urban infrastructure (Adaptation)
- Increase adaptive capacity of populations to climate events or hazards – through energy efficient cooling; waste to energy innovations and provisions of climate related information (Adaptation)
- Support increased access to equipment and resources (data, agro-processing, irrigation, cooling, etc.) for climate-resilient agriculture; expected to increase capacity of agriculture dependent rural populations

## Key Outputs and Activities under Component 1

1. The investment team brings a combined 40+ years of experience investing in energy and climate technology in Africa and other emerging markets. The team has a deep understanding of renewable energy technologies and is embedded in the local markets across Africa where it invests with proprietary access to deal flow. Through KawiSafi I, the team has built up an unparalleled reputation as a leading energy investor in Africa, having invested in many of the companies at the forefront of the distributed renewables transformation in Africa. KawiSafi II should benefit from the continuity, insights, experience, and market reputation of the team that has led to the ongoing success of KawiSafi I.

2. Output 1: Equity capital and support for scaling of low-carbon and climate smart businesses.

3. Output 2: Wider, access to and use of clean energy, alternative transport, and productive use assets and services by households and businesses.

The KawiSafi team targets to deploy equity capital to scale up low-carbon and climate sensitive SMEs across sub-Saharan Africa, scaling up the adoption of low-carbon technologies at household and business level. The efforts here tie directly to the fund outcomes of i) reduced energy-related GHG emissions from renewable energy and energy storage solutions, ii) reduced transport and logistics-related GHG emissions from electric and shared mobility solutions, and greening of private fleets and iii) reduced household GHG emissions from “green productivity” solutions, including energy efficiency, agriculture-water energy, waste solutions, materials.

Below we list the anticipated activities that are designed to lead to these expected outputs:

## Activity 1.1: Fundraising, Market Screening, Pipeline Development, and Equity Investment

### Fund formation and Fundraising

The KawiSafi Fund II is an ambitious $200M Fund intending to utilize a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into the African region. The Fund seeks to continue the success demonstrated in the deployment of KawiSafi Fund I and target business models that deploy low-carbon technologies in an inclusive and sustainable way and that enables people to participate in a green economy to grow out of poverty.

- Sub-Activity 1.1.1: Fund formation. Establishment of the Fund entity and all partnership terms agreed.
- Sub-Activity 1.1.2: Fundraising. Engagement with development finance institutions, institutional investors, and family offices to achieve final close of the $200M Fund.

### Market screening and Pipeline Development

The KawiSafi team has deep experience and expertise in distributed renewable energy, financial inclusion and PAYGO (BNPL) technologies across Africa. It has screened and tracked the success of over 300 Africa-focused businesses comprising over $1B in aggregate deal value, many of which leverage technology to achieve radical affordability for lower-income markets. This represents a qualified pipeline for Fund II investments.

- Sub-Activity 1.1.3: Research and sourcing. The KawiSafi team has a high-visibility presence in its target sectors, and is in regular contact with entrepreneurs, investors, advisors and accelerators. The Management team builds on a two decade-long experience of Acumen and directly from its experience of deploying its predecessor KawiSafi I since 2016.

KawiSafi I identified an early-stage market where technology tailwinds were driving systemic cost reductions in photovoltaic (PV) and appliance efficiency. Since then, the team has co-invested with over 20 investors, crowding in more than $400M in follow-on debt and equity, collaborated with local research institutions and accelerators, and become the partner of choice for late venture/early growth funding in climate sectors. The team meets companies in the earliest stages and monitors progress over time until a slightly later round of investment, providing de-risked access to the leading companies.

When a particular segment is of interest, the team also pursues a top-down approach to identifying all the relevant companies in that segment and pursuing those with the most compelling proposition. The team has privileged access to the energy and climate technology investments in its markets.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 41

## Equity Investment via investment decisions to most impactful, fast growth companies

KawiSafi follows a rigorous and structured investment process from sourcing through evaluation, due diligence and transaction documentation.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.4: Initial Diligence

The initial diligence phase is focused on information provided by the company, tested against the team’s market knowledge and research. If a company’s business model meets criteria considering unit economics; management team experience; fit for local market; potential for regional expansion; poverty and climate impact, it advances to the next stage. The team is uniquely suited to conduct this up-front analysis using market intelligence, comparing to other early-stage businesses in the sector, and expert networks leveraging local and international entrepreneurial and financial track records.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.5: Preliminary Investment Memo

The preliminary investment memo is the first stage at which an opportunity is presented to the Investment Committee. KawiSafi’s Investment Committee is composed of established investment professionals with deep relevant expertise, including local and international private equity, development finance and operational experience.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.6: Conduct Climate Impact and Commercial Diligence

The DD process and company selection is based on the positive assessment of its expected contribution to climate change adaptation and mitigation. Following an IC approval based on the Preliminary Investment Memo, the team proceeds to full commercial diligence. This phase includes primary research comprised of interviews with company representatives, customers, competitors, technical experts and others to fully inform the team’s view on the business model and opportunity.

The team utilizes due diligence checklists informed by prior transaction experience, and tailors each process to address the unique risks and opportunities of each potential investment. The IC members also provide input on critical areas for diligence. This information is presented to the IC at an interim meeting, where parameters for investment terms are discussed and agreed in principle.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.7: Agree terms and complete diligence

The team negotiates terms with the company to execute a term sheet. Terms are determined based upon the team’s insight from a range of local transactions and structured to manage the specific risks and opportunities of each investment. Upon execution, the team engages external advisors for legal and ESG due diligence. Any findings from the external diligence are reflected in the transaction documents, which include an ESG Action Plan and, where applicable, a 100-Day Operational Plan. The final terms and diligence findings are presented to the IC for final approval.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.8: Investment documentation and closing

The investment terms are formally documented with external counsel advising. Investment documentation includes impact, gender, and ESG provisions. The transaction must be reviewed by the KawiSafi Board of Directors for consistency with all regulations and governance agreements, and then may proceed to disbursement.

## Activity 1.2: Post-Investment Engagement

KawiSafi is an active investor, taking a board seat and otherwise being actively involved in the oversight and management of its investments. The team’s deep experience with scaling enterprises is a complement to investee management teams. In particular, the investment team’s perspective on industry best practices and local context are sought out by entrepreneurs and co-investors alike, considering the unique challenges of building a fast-growing business in emerging markets, and specifically in Africa.

KawiSafi’s experienced team is deeply rooted in this rapidly evolving market which has allowed it to identify and close deals with the most competitive and sought-after firms. The team has a demonstrated advantage based on its proximity to markets, understanding of context, political and execution realities, competition, and ability to assess the quality of management teams. In addition, the Fund’s reputation for international leadership and value-add beyond pure capital has given it a powerful competitive advantage in both pricing and portfolio.

The team also tracks news on every portfolio company, and the partners and reports on a frequent basis.

The KawiSafi team oversees compliance with the shareholder agreement for each deal and works closely with its legal team to ensure all applicable regulatory frameworks are adhered to. Fund II’s exit strategy is based on selling shares through a company sale to strategic buyers, financial buyers, initial public offering, or management team of the investee company “management buyout,” or alternatively through a secondary sale of shares to a later-stage investor.

KawiSafi II seeks to create sustained, positive impact through their investments, even after exit. This is achieved by building sustainable business model with positive impact mandate and seeking out potential buyers with commitment to continue such a mandate.

## Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) to support capacity and knowledge gaps and ecosystem development

1. To maximize the mitigation and adaptation impact of the Equity investments and augment the activities of the KawiSafi Fund II, Acumen is raising a $10 million Technical Assistance Facility ("TAF") alongside the Fund. The purpose of this Grant funded TAF will be to support both portfolio companies and the wider energy and climate ecosystem in focus African markets. The portfolio support will be targeted at assisting companies to improve their business and operational capabilities ensure they maximize their climate impact while having strong management systems to address any environmental, social and governance risks that may arise.

2. The design of the TAF builds on the experience of the KawiSafi Fund I technical assistance facility, which as of 31/12/2022 had deployed more than $1.2 million in capital into programs focused on consumer protection and gender & diversity. The design also reflects important priorities based on our close interaction with KawiSafi I portfolio companies and the broader climate venture ecosystem. The TAF builds on this experience with a focus on targeted interventions in three sub component areas: (1) Climate & ESG, (2) Gender & Diversity, (3) Technology & Innovation.

3. The TAF (which makes focused or "smart" TA grants) is an essential complement to the KawiSafi Fund II (which makes late venture stage Equity investments) because it strengthens, supports, and accelerates development of the ecosystem which enables private sector activity and clean technology deployment. Equity investments such as those provided by KawiSafi II play a critical role in supporting companies to reach scale and maturity. However, without a conducive ecosystem (information, resources, systems, policies, collaborations) for climate solutions, there will likely be fewer climate technology companies being set up, their rate of growth will likely be slower, their failure rate will likely be higher, and ultimately their chances of having climate impact at scale will likely be constrained. By providing TA alongside KawiSafi II, this will seek to allow the Fund to both (i) have broader impact beyond the immediate scope of its equity investments and (ii) optimize the opportunities for its portfolio companies to have as much mitigation and adaptation impact as possible.

These are actors primarily working at the ecosystem level e.g. policy formulation for whom it would be valuable for TAF to align with and establish knowledge sharing channels. ### Specialized actors

These actors offer a suite of specialized TA services typically targeted at more mature businesses and are able to rapidly crowd-in resources and expertise within their areas of specialization.

### Market systems actors

These are actors focused on stimulating pipeline development and early-stage innovation. TAF can potentially work with these actors to amplify TAF activities, especially in new target markets to optimize for speed to impact.

For new target markets for TAF, working closely with partners with traction in these markets should help TAF to accelerate delivery. TAF beneficiaries shall include both KawiSafi II investee companies and other eligible beneficiaries. Other eligible beneficiaries shall include:

- Research firms carrying out market studies, climate impact assessments, etc.;
- Training and capacity building organizations (e.g. ESOs) doing skills development for e.g. female technicians, female climate entrepreneurs, etc.

The Eligibility Criteria for companies supported under the TAF shall be the same as for Component I: Equity Fund, as set out in B.3 above in detail. The companies supported under the TAF must be fall under the technologies and business models set out in these Eligibility Criteria.

Beneficiaries under the TAF will receive support in different forms depending on the context and the needs of the beneficiaries, and alignment with the TAF mandate. This support can include:

- Grant funding for gender & diversity, climate adaptation, or technology & innovation pilots or initiatives;
- Technical assistance in the form of professional services / expert consulting provided by third parties;
- Market studies or research to be made publicly available.

## Activity 2.3: Technical assistance for climate solutions and outcomes that are gender inclusive and transformative

The objective of this sub component is to address inequality on the basis of gender, affecting women’s capacity to adapt to and address climate change challenges, as well as ensuring that women’s central role in business and household is harnessed to promote climate-smart behavior change. This is through providing services and funding to facilitate mainstreaming of gender initiatives within the countries of focus for KawiSafi II. The fund is also committed to investing in companies with safe and equitable workplaces and seeks to support companies in providing protections against SEAH.

KawiSafi II’s gender assessment indicated a high vulnerability to climate change for women in the focus countries with more than 60% of the female population in climate-vulnerable employment notably in the agriculture and services sectors. Further, for the countries highlighted, between 77% (Kenya) and 91.5% (DRC) of women are self-employed. This indicates that most women in these countries are likely to be disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards and to overall changes in economic activity. Left unmitigated, these effects will likely exacerbate existing gender inequality, worsening the outcomes for women’s livelihood.

Female access to credit remains low with data indicating as little as 2.4% of the female population has access to formal credit limiting purchasing power, decision-making, and the realization of entrepreneurial aspirations. There are too few female managers and professionals in management positions across the board: Zambia and Ghana have the highest % of female professionals at 38% and 41% respectively, while Cote D’Ivoire only has 12.7% female professionals recorded.

KawiSafi’s Funds (KawiSafi I and KawiSafi II) have a strong gender commitment, including robust Gender Action Plans and a Gender component of the KawiSafi Fund I technical assistance facility. As the Funds incorporate the 2X criteria into their activities, continued support is required from the TAF to continue strong progress on gender goals. The TAF activities will be focused around the goals to address the financing gap for women-led enterprises, gender inclusion within enterprises providing low-carbon technologies across Africa, and inclusion and increased access to clean energy products for female consumers.

The activities will be delivered for the benefit of female jobseekers, employees, leaders, entrepreneurs and policy makers relevant to the climate ecosystem in focus countries. The outputs provide targeted pathways for female participation in the low-carbon economy through improving skills development, access to capital, and accelerating female-led low-carbon ventures. Activities include:

### Sub-Activity 2.3.1 Support activities to promote skills development and leadership representation of women in new climate-tech sectors

TAF will seek to identify critical skills gaps in key climate sectors and work closely with local and global partners, support the delivery of skills development programmes targeted at increasing access to employment opportunities for women in the sector. TAF will also seek to facilitate increased representation of women with STEM skills on the boards of climate sector organizations to support improved gender equality. This will include activities such as gender & diversity training for portfolio company boards (including unconscious bias training, sexual harassment, etc.), recruitment of women to boards and advisory committees and senior advisor roles, and also at company management team level.

### Sub-Activity 2.3.2 Support initiatives to increase entrepreneurial participation of women in the climate sector

TAF will seek to support access to finance initiatives and entrepreneurial support for women-owned/led start-ups in focus markets. This will include supporting women-led enterprises with access to capital, training, mentorship, and networking opportunities to help them grow and succeed in the climate sector.

The incubators/accelerators/ESOs selected as partners under this sub-activity shall be selected based on (i) experience working with early-stage entrepreneurs, especially women (ii) presence and networks in KawiSafi II focus countries (iii) expertise in climate technology sectors in line with KawiSafi II’s mandate and (iv) operational capabilities and organizational resources. ### Sub-Activity 2.3.3 Develop programmes that promote accelerated uptake of clean technology products by women - working with local partners to identify critical barriers to uptake specific to local contexts and design interventions to address barriers to uptake for women, particularly low-income women. This will also include working with climate sector firms to foster gender integration into product design, marketing, and deployment in relevant markets.  
Local partners are local NGOs and service providers (e.g. ESOs, training companies, consulting firms, research institutes etc.) based in KawiSafi II focus countries.

### Activity 2.4: Technical assistance for accelerating the deployment of new technologies and innovative business models  
The climate transition is knowledge and skills driven. In the last decade, rapid innovation has enabled the development of low-carbon technologies and the evolution of business models that have enabled commercialization. For example, the global cost of solar energy decreased by over 80% since 2010 driven by cheaper technology and growing demand.  
Consumer uptake of renewable energy solutions, particularly solar, has been fueled by greater public awareness and innovative financing such as catalytic grants for research and entrepreneurship, consumer education campaigns, household incentives, and accessible credit terms.  

The pace of change required to meet the global net zero emissions reduction targets is such that without collaboration, cross-pollination of ideas, information exchange, and sharing, the world is at risk of not reducing emissions fast enough to restrict global heating. The objective of this activity set is to boost the pace of innovation and adoption by enhancing eco system collaboration, cross-sector cooperation, and productive partnership through curating, collating, and disseminating knowledge.  

The activities will be delivered for the benefit of entrepreneurs, policymakers, regulators, businesses, consumers, and other technology pioneers in the focus countries.  
Outputs under this sub component are bolstering the evidence base of climate business models and building more technology use cases. In Cluster A countries this will be largely case study driven and through role modeling successful ventures and applications from that focus country or similar focus country. In Cluster B countries, the evidence base will likely be enterprise archetypes and significant quantifiable results that enable scale. Activities include:  

#### Sub-Activity 2.4.1 Support market acceleration by working with climate and tech incubators and accelerators and industry bodies to support innovation around new technologies and business models - this includes: e.g. funding an accelerator cohort for a nascent climate technology in collaboration with a reputable accelerator manager; e.g. the Africa Center for Clean Cooling in Kigali on a partnership. ### 11. TAF mandate and sizing  
Acumen’s experience with KawiSafi Fund I informs the size and activities of the proposed TAF. The design also reflects important priorities based on the team’s close interaction with KawiSafi I portfolio companies and the broader climate venture ecosystem over the past 5 years.  

The proposed $10M Technical Assistance (TA) Facility is 5% of target fund size of $200M. We consider this allocation below the benchmark data. According to a Convergence Study which reviewed 136 technical assistance facilities, the size of TA facilities on average was ~ 12% of the investment fund amount. Further, a 2014 World Bank policy research paper stated that technical assistance provided to investee companies of private equity and venture capital funds ranges between 7-15% of the value of a given investment depending on the company, and that technical assistance facilities themselves range between 6 -20% of the total fund size, with an average of around 10%. Proprietary research conducted by SME investment manager Jacana Parteners (previously InReturn) showed an average TA facility funding-to-fund size ratio of around 11% based on an analysis of ten funds in Africa.

This is further underscored by the expanded geographic focus that will likely result in increased complexity of delivery given more nuanced operating terrain.  

Leveraging local partners can not only foster greater inclusivity but also optimize the relevance and effectiveness of TA solutions delivered, especially for local country programs.  

#### TAF will seek to proactively map relevant local partners in the respective geographies and design outreach strategies to engage and collaborate with local partners.  

#### Knowledge sharing and exchange will be key to optimal partnerships in order to foster cross-pollination across partners and geographies.

## Flexible and responsive TA delivery approach

COVID-19 was an unanticipated shock to global markets, and many TA’s were not able to respond to changing beneficiary needs precipitated by the pandemic. A survey by FMO of more than 900 MSMEs (a majority from sub-Saharan Africa) showed that less than 10% of these took action and invested in organizational transformations which undermined their adaptation efforts to the pandemic. This underscores the important role of responsive TA in helping to foster the sustainability of green businesses already operating in challenging market environments.

- TAF is targeted and clearly focused on three themes, however, within that, it has been designed to support a breadth of activities thereby maximizing the responsiveness and flexibility to evolving beneficiary needs.
- The facility will seek to be implemented in partnership and close collaboration with beneficiary businesses and the TAF governance and communication approach builds in high-frequency communication and dynamism that maximizes responsiveness.

## Demand-driven TA delivery

One of the key learnings from KawiSafi Fund I is the importance of the unique vantage point KawiSafi and its team has, from working closely with entrepreneurs. The KawiSafi II team is well positioned to support the proactive identification of TA needs and filter these through to the TA team. Where the TA is able to address these, the results are often preventative, in mitigating potential adverse impacts to the business. As the geographic scope of the TA broadens, the synergies between KawiSafi II and the TA will likely become more important in helping to proactively surface nuanced market-specific TA needs, especially those that may likely impact a cross-section of businesses in the target sectors. Furthermore, the TA needs to be able to respond to the most critical needs of the businesses in its operating environments both in terms of supporting Fund investee companies and non-investee companies.

- Strengthen synergies with TAF via structured feedback mechanisms between the TAF and KawiSafi Fund II to ensure emerging TA needs are swiftly filtered through to TAF to inform overall programme planning and scoping. Similarly feeding back insights from TAF activities to KawiSafi Fund II to the extent that this is useful in guiding fund activities will likely be built into the TAF reporting activities.

## Focus on continuous improvement

A learning orientation is key to ensuring TA support continues to be relevant and effective. This will likely rely on stronger feedback mechanisms and experimental delivery approaches such as proof of concepts that foster learning. Continuous adaptation should be entrenchedin the TA delivery approach to allow the TA to enhance its solutions building on from evolving findings on the ground. In addition, learnings generated should also be widely shared via various channels/ forums in order to both validate and transfer knowledge.

- TAF will seek to develop project reporting elements that ensure learnings from projects are captured and reviewed for future project design and planning. This should include collating feedback from relevant stakeholders e.g., TA beneficiaries, partners, etc.
- For longer-term TAF initiatives interim reviews should be built into programme delivery as well as adhoc beneficiary feedback to support ongoing optimization of delivery

## Optimise for speed to deployment of TA

Expedient TA deployment is critical to ensure alignment with fund activities. A lag in technical assistance deployment can undermine the relevance of the technical assistance activities due to a misalignment with the emerging needs on the ground. The energy sector in particular is increasingly dynamic and being able able to provide timely support is key. This is especially true when providing ecosystem-level support related to specific interventions that can help unlock context-specific barriers.

- TAF will seek to work closely with GCF to support expedient delivery of relevant proposals

## 1. Executing Entities (EEs)

The Executing Entities for KawiSafi Fund II are:

### a. The Manager: Marula Partners LLC, a Delaware (US) company

Marula Partners is the appointed investment manager of the KawiSafi II LP, an Ontario limited partnership, which will be the direct investing entity. The KawiSafi II investment team and Marula Partners personnel are the same individuals. The Manager shall be the primary EE.

### b. The General Partner: KawiSafi II Capital Investments LLC (GP), a Delaware (US) company

100% owned by Acumen Capital Partners LLC, itself a Delaware (US) company 100% owned by Acumen Fund, Inc (the "Accredited Entity" or "AE").

## 2. Role of the Accredited Entity

The AE will support both the EE and GCF with an advisory role and on monitoring and reporting, as expected to be described in the subsidiary agreement.

Acumen, through its General Counsel, CFO, Legal Counsel, ESG Insights Manager, and Risk and Compliance Manager will advise the team on GCF expectations, areas of expertise, and investment considerations, prepare related pass-through documentation to ease the process vis a vis investee, provide support to the team and investees in areas of ESG.

## 3. Fund Administrator and Auditor

The Fund has identified a third-party Administrator that will be hired at fund closing. The administrator will perform asset and valuation calculations, maintain books and records, support financial statement preparation and reporting, liaise with auditors, pay fund expenses from its bank account, calculate distributions and returns, prepare and submit regulatory filings, monitor and report on compliance anti-money laundering and supervise dissolution of the fund. The administrator will be a separate and unaffiliated entity.

The Fund will appoint an independent Auditor at Fund Closing.

The Investment Committee (IC) is the final decision-making body in the investment process. IC will assess the business model for its financial viability, social impact and climate contribution and make a final decision to approve each transaction. The primary responsibilities of the IC will be to:

1. Review/approve/reject transactions, at key decision gates in the investment process and at exit
2. Review current pipeline, post-investment management activities and investment outcomes
3. Review investment and portfolio performance against annual goals and recommend potential corrective action, if necessary
4. Review and provide guidance on investment and portfolio management strategy

KawiSafi II will have an IC of up to 5 members. The IC members will comprise experts with a combination of (i) investment experience (ii) Africa regional experience and (iii) climate expertise. The Manager is currently recruiting at least 2 additional IC members in line with this profile.

The IC will be governed by a terms of reference and engagement acceptance letter which each IC member shall sign. ## 6. Impact Review Process

The Impact Thesis (especially, climate mitigation and adaptation) for each investment will be presented as part of the Investment Memo to the Fund’s Investment Committee for consideration and approval. The Fund’s contribution to impact is achieved through the provision of capital as well as through Management support and strategic engagement with investee companies through the life of the investment. Consistent with good practices for ESG, KawiSafi Fund II will articulate its impact expectations in its Investment Agreements.

## 7. Impact Management Plan

The Impact Management Plan will include (but not be limited to) the following contents:

1. Articulation of the Impact Thesis  
2. Reference to any secondary research or primary company data in support of the Impact Thesis  
3. Identification of the key impact metrics (and any secondary metrics) for the investment using the attached Indicator Framework as a basis, which focuses on climate adaptation, mitigation and people in poverty through metrics including: GCF Results Areas, lives impacted with access to energy-enabled services, employment generation, gender impacts and poverty reach.  
4. Projected annual and aggregate impact across the impact metrics (with sensitivity analysis based on base/down scenarios)  
5. Assessment of any risks of negative impacts from company’s business, and how to mitigate, including potential for support from the TAF, particularly with respect to gender, consumer protection and ESG.  
6. Plan for how to work with the company to ensure achievement of impact targets  

## 8. Limited Partner Advisory Committee (LPAC)

KawiSafi Fund II will have an advisory committee (LPAC) to serve as an adviser to the Manager and the General Partner and to consult with them concerning the Fund’s activities and operations, including, in particular, with respect to the identification and resolution of conflicts of interest.

## 9. Governance for the TAF  

The TAF sits alongside but exists independently from the KawiSafi II Fund, with separate management and governance arrangements.

For the implementation of TAF, the AE shall sign a Management Agreement, in the form of a grant agreement, with the EE (the “Management Agreement”). The EE shall develop and present for approval by the Technical Assistance Committee (“TAC”) a TA manual governing the operations and grant-making processes of the TAF (“TA Manual").

## 10. Experience and track record of the AE and EEs with respect to the activities (sector and country/region)

KawiSafi Fund I, launched in 2016, is a first-of-its-kind vehicle that invests in venture stage companies in the distributed renewable energy ecosystem in Africa. The team recognized the vast potential of the off-grid energy sector to make a substantial impact in electrification rates in Sub-Saharan Africa through ambitious and sustainable business models. The sector has grown exponentially since 2010 to reach 420 million users of off-grid solar today, many of whom did not have access to modern energy sources before. There is now a $1.75 billion annual market for off-grid solar products and services. KawiSafi I has been at the forefront of this trend, having invested in three of the market leaders (d.light, Bboxx and BioLite) to take them regionally across Africa and, in some cases, into Asia.

## 11. As of 30/06/2022, KawiSafi Fund I has achieved:

- 110 million lives impacted with energy access globally, of which 43 million lives are impacted in East Africa (the primary geographic focus of Fund I). This represents 4.3X the fund’s impact target in East Africa.
- 24 million tons of CO2 equivalent averted globally. This represents 24X the fund’s global emissions target.

# B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 1. Funding from GCF will enable the launch of KawiSafi Fund II (KawiSafi II), focused on providing energy and climate solutions for underserved people in poverty in Africa, driven toward positive environmental, social and financial outcomes.

An anchor investment of $52.5M from GCF, including $40M of first loss capital for the Equity Fund, $10M senior capital for the Equity Fund, and $2.5M grant for the TAF, is expected to be catalytic, as it will de-risk the investment for more risk-averse private sector investors, required to meet the scale needed in our target sectors. It is well documented that public funds alone will not likely be sufficient to meet the global challenge of climate change. This is perhaps no truer than in Africa where the risks are perceived as high and the local capital markets are shallow. In energy access and climate solutions, these disparities run even deeper.

~550 million people lack access to energy in Sub-Saharan Africa, and available energy services are inadequate.

## 2. KawiSafi I (the first fund) was launched to catalyze a nascent market segment and move it toward maturity.

Beyond the risk of investing into an underserved region, Fund I invested in new business models as they scaled up from pilot and proof stage to become strong regional companies. The energy and climate sectors in Africa have benefited from capital providers at the earliest stages of company development, where philanthropic organizations have invested time and resources to support and promote high-potential companies but have limited capacity to continue to fund as companies scale.

In turn, there are a number of later stage strategies that benefit from longer company track records and/or the ability to enter into long-term contracts with credit-worthy counterparties. These infrastructure and later-stage private equity strategies have tools to de-risk the investments and deploy larger ticket sizes, thereby attracting more institutional capital.

KawiSafi’s investment strategy is designed to address the massive gap in between these funding stages, backing companies that have proven customer demand and need to scale their operations and continue investing in new product development. KawiSafi Fund II will continue to direct capital into new business models with high opportunity to address market gaps.

## 3. A key part of KawiSafi Fund II’s theory of change is to mobilize capital from large institutional investors, such as global asset management firms.

GCF’s investment in KawiSafi I helped to mobilize investment from global asset management firms such as AXA and Goldings, as well as family foundations. For KawiSafi II, we are setting out an even more ambitious goal to raise a significant share of our Fund from private investors.

However, the feedback from private institutional investors has been clear. While they are excited about KawiSafi II’s mission, they require downside protection. Catalyzing this category of investors is essential to meet the target fund size of $200 million and make a meaningful difference in the target sectors.

4. From our consultation with private investors, we learnt that while many of these investors are interested in KawiSafi Fund II and see it as well aligned with their climate targets and objectives, they still perceive the expected returns as too low and the risk of loss of investment too high. For this reason, we are requesting GCF funding as Junior Equity.

While DFI and concessional funders are important partners for KawiSafi, their funding alone is inadequate to address the massive climate and poverty challenges facing Africa. Fund II’s target size and segments are designed to address these challenges and build sustainable, scaling companies. To meet that need, we need to attract capital from commercial investors, who have announced trillions in commitments to the climate challenge but are sitting on the sidelines when it comes to emerging markets in general and Africa in particular.

These asset managers need the capacity to reach targeted market segments, backed with the expertise to navigate local context, however this diverges from their traditional investment strategies and desire to deploy large amounts of capital in few transactions. In our markets, investors are unable to rely on contractual guarantees or corporate credit ratings that are typically utilized for risk management in the energy and climate sectors.

To attract these investors to essential investments in more challenging markets, the Junior Equity will improve the risk-return profile by both mitigating risk and enhancing return.

5. We request a total $50 million equity commitment from GCF, including $40 million of Junior Equity and $10 million of Senior B Equity. In addition, we are requesting $2.5M of Grant capital for the Technical Assistance Facility.

The Junior Equity is designed with two features: 1) first loss protection (to manage downside risk) and 2) a differential preferred return (to improve upside potential). The first loss protection absorbs any losses of the KawiSafi Fund II in the event that the Fund does not achieve positive returns. The differential preferred return allows the Senior A investors to receive distributions up to a higher preferred return before the Junior Equity participates in a lower preferred return, and then each would ratably share in Fund excess returns above the hurdle rate.

The Junior Equity is intended to benefit only private investors in the Senior A tranche, not development finance investors. Concessional capital providers are expected to participate in a separate, Senior B tranche.

## Blended Finance Structure:

### Two Waterfalls for Focused Downside Protection

| Senior A Equity | Junior A Equity | Senior B Equity |
|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|
| $~70M (54%)     | $~60M (46%)     | $~70M           |
| [65]%           | [35]%           |                 |

#### Downside Protection for Senior A:
- Junior A Equity only benefits the Senior A, targeted to commercial, private investors.

#### How it works:
- All equity from Senior A, Junior A and Senior B to be drawn down pari passu.
- All equity from Senior A, Junior A and Senior B will cover expenses and fees proportionately.
- Reflowed funds for distribution will flow proportionately into the A waterfall and the B waterfall.
- In the A waterfall, the senior contributions are paid in full before the junior contributions.
- The effect is that in the event the fund returns below $200M, the Junior equity would bear the deficit below $200M, up to $60M, in favor of the Senior A Equity.

# B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

KawiSafi Fund II’s business model is to drive sustainable growth of portfolio companies to drive strong exits upon investment maturity.

1. KawiSafi seeks to ensure that the positive impact is maintained even after exit through building sustainable scalable businesses with demonstrated impact mandates and seeking out potential buyers who share the commitment to continue such a mandate.

## Blended Finance Structure:

### Different Preferred Return for Senior A and Junior A

| Flow of Funds for A | Senior A Equity | Junior A Equity |
|---------------------|-----------------|-----------------|
| 1. Return of Capital to Senior A | $~70M (54%) | $~60M (46%) |
| 2. Return of Capital to Junior A |  |  |
| 3. Preferred Return of 8% to Senior A |  |  |
| 4. Preferred Return of 3% to Junior A |  |  |
| 5. Carried Interest catch up to GP/Manager (and thereafter 20% share in Fund returns) |  |  |
| 6. Proportionate share of the 80% excess returns between Senior and Junior |  |  |

### How it works:

- After the Senior A and Junior A receive Return of Capital, Senior A will receive Preferred Return on Investment of 8%
- Then, Junior A receives a Return on Investment of 3%
- After the GP catch up, excess returns allocated to the A Equity are shared pari passu between Senior A and Junior A.
- The effect is that in the event the fund returns below a 8% return, the Senior A receives priority in the return. After giving effect to projected excess returns, the Senior A would have an enhanced overall return.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 1. Overall impact targets:
KawiSafi Fund II will seek to identify investments that promote radical affordability of energy-enabled and climate-smart products and services, reducing vulnerability and strengthening resilience of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly to effects of climate change and avorting 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq, demonstrating that the focus African markets can leapfrog fossil-fuel grids to clean technologies.

### 2. Under Mitigation:
KawiSafi Fund II expects to achieve the following:

- Through its investment in distributed solar generation, avert 20.9 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) during the Fund's lifetime.
- Through investments in firms deploying innovative business models that drive climate-smart technology adoption across Africa, KawiSafi Fund II will work to increase the number of people with access to low-emission and low-carbon energy, productivity, and mobility solutions.
- In addition to availability of residential lighting and the direct health-related benefits of transitioning from dirty fuels such as kerosene, access to power also has the potential to catalyze economic development in rural areas and creates more job opportunities as well as increased opportunities for higher incomes. Benefits are captured in ARA1.

- In addition to residential lighting, KawiSafi Fund II is expected to make investments in C&I solar power generation; battery storage applications targeted at displacing diesel generators; as well as in energy productivity which includes deployment of renewable energy at mobile towers sites that are currently powered by diesel generators resulting in significant CO2eq emissions.
- The Fund also expects to support the reduction in energy intensity of industries by seeking out firms with technologies that facilitate green manufacturing through integration of renewables and energy-efficient technologies, as well as support clean cooling interventions. (MRA3)

### 3. The third segment of focus for KawiSafi Fund II will be mobility and logistics through investments to scale companies rolling out two, three and four-wheeler electric vehicles ("EVs") across various markets on the continent, thus increasing the use of low-carbon transport and significantly reducing carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) in urban areas.

Through investments in the Energy, Mobility, and Productivity (clean cooling, agriculture-energy, green manufacturing) sectors, KawiSafi Fund II aims to reduce climate vulnerability of approximately 3.5 million people by enhancing their resilience and adaptive capacity in the following ways:

- Adverse changes to the quantity and quality of both surface water and groundwater – due to projected trends in temperature and precipitation across the continent – are expected to have significant impacts on food security.
- Furthermore, unpredictable, and extreme weather events often result in increased post-harvest losses and food supply chain disruption.
- KawiSafi Fund II will seek to invest in resilient food-systems which support agricultural communities adapt to the changing realities of climate change.
- Through the energy productivity segment, KawiSafi Fund II will invest in innovations in the agriculture energy nexus – such as solar powered irrigation; as well as cold chain investments which together are expected to improve predictability of farming and durability of produce to reduce post-harvest losses.
- Cold chain and storage solutions are expected to help strengthen food security and reduce food loss in this case.
- These benefits are captured under ARA2 and ARA1.

# D.2.1 Potential for scaling up and replication

1. KawiSafi II intends to build on KawiSafi Fund I (the world’s first climate change fund focused on SMEs that serve BoP populations in developing countries). KawiSafi Fund II will seek to demonstrate the scalability and replicability of the approach. The larger Fund, investing across a wider geographical footprint, will take the lessons learnt in deploying the first fund and (with continued GCF partnership) allow KawiSafi to demonstrate that nations can leapfrog fossil-fuel grids to clean energy. The Fund expects to create a blueprint to a clean-energy future that advances GCF’s goals and that can be replicated in multiple geographies.

2. The Fund’s investments will seek to develop, prove, and scale innovative clean energy technologies and products that will promote low-carbon growth, and support lower income people to live a life that is more deeply empowered and enabled, with more opportunity and less risk. Success stories from such investments should attract further private sector capital into those companies and others, increasing the scale and impact of clean energy companies operating locally and advancing GCF’s goals.

3. By investing in for-profit companies, KawiSafi II, GCF, and other investors will seek to create financially sustainable enterprises that continue to operate through internal profits and follow-on investment even after the KawiSafi Fund II has completed its investments. All the fund’s portfolio companies will have significant operations within the specific countries of focus. Scale built through the Fund’s investments will seek to generate strong exits for investors which will likely further build credibility of the African investment landscape.

4. KawiSafi Fund II will seek to invest in business models where innovation and scale are directly correlated to impact generated. This is expected to reduce vulnerability of approximately 3.5 million people (directly and indirectly) to the effects of climate change and avert 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq during the lifetime of the Fund. These investments are expected to create employment opportunities, build local institutional knowledge, and strengthen local supply and distribution channels in their countries of operation.

5. Equity investment is critical for nascent sectors such as in the agriculture-energy companies that are still proving out their business model and product market fit. KawiSafi II’s investment and role as investment lead will likely be important to: 1) further catalyze private sector investment by signaling commercial opportunity in these businesses; and 2) establish a strong balance sheet position that will allow the closing of low-cost debt required to achieve scale for wider income and productivity benefits for smallholder farmers.

In addition, Acumen has published well-received reports on energy featuring the work of its Pioneer Energy Investment Initiative (PEII) and KawiSafi Fund I. These include: *Accelerating Energy Access: The Role of Patient Capital; Fueling the Future: What’s next in Off Grid-Energy; and Lighting the Way: Roadmaps to Exits in Off-Grid Energy.*

2. Both Acumen Fund and KawiSafi Fund I held numerous open webinars to share and crowdsource knowledge on various innovative approaches to driving strong performance and impact. # D.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

## 1. Acumen is requesting that GCF invest equity capital into a 10-year Fund (plus up to two 1-year extensions, i.e., 12 years maximum). This vehicle, and GCF’s anchoring investment, will enable Acumen to draw in risk-averse capital from like-minded investors who are interested in the same goals but have a lower risk appetite. The proposed fund structure will be tiered and will include a Junior and Senior equity layer. The participation of GCF and other catalytic capital providers in the Junior layer will further de-risk KawiSafi Fund II and attract investments from private institutional capital providers who will benefit from downside protection and enhancement of returns.

## 2. We believe that an investment of $50M from GCF, including $40M in the Junior equity layer and $10M in the Senior equity layer, will be sufficient to unlock an additional $150M to achieve the desired Fund size of $200M. In addition to leveraging KawiSafi Fund II structure to attract additional capital, the investments made from KawiSafi Fund II will support company balance sheets to raise debt and set them up for even larger follow-on equity rounds (multiplying the positive social and climate impacts generated).

## 3. KawiSafi Fund II expects to raise a $10M Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) which is designed to maximize the climate mitigation and adaptation impact of our investments and augment the activities of the equity Fund. This grant funded facility will seek to assist both portfolio companies and the wider clean energy ecosystem. Portfolio companies will receive the necessary support to ensure they maximize both their financial returns and climate impact while having strong management systems to address any environmental, social and governance risks that may arise. The TAF also intends to support the portfolio companies develop the tools and capacity needed to measure their ESG and climate impacts, which is a current gap in the market. The ecosystem support will go towards collaboration with early-stage incubators and accelerators, as well as industry associations and academia to support development and maturation while ensuring that the industry weighs the impacts of its activities on all members of society, especially women.

As of 31/12/21, the KawiSafi I portfolio had pro-actively promoted gender diversity and inclusion, directly employing ~870 women and training over 4,900 women in the larger ecosystem. We expect that gender initiatives in Fund II will be more prominent, (as highlighted in the Gender Action Plan) and lead to widespread inclusion across the ecosystem and long-standing benefits to the female beneficiaries.

# D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Overview of Fund Focus

The fund will focus on Bottom-of-the-Pyramid (BoP) populations, where the investments would bring direct livelihood improvements, including increased incomes, safer and healthier household/business environments, improved food / water security, and increased access to information.

## Environmental Impacts

Environmental co-benefits will be determined for each of the projects funded under the Program. These co-benefits will tentatively include a reduction of air and water pollution, and improved resource-use through recycling and second life applications. CO2 emissions are expected to be averted through the replacement of kerosene, diesel, and other carbon-based fuels.

Furthermore, the transport sector accounts for around a quarter of energy-related CO2 emissions globally and is almost completely dependent on fossil fuels. Decarbonizing this sector is crucial to achieving the temperature goals of the Paris Agreement and improving air quality more generally.

## Health Benefits

Use of clean lighting and cooking alternatives has significant health benefits from the transition from dirty, pollutive, and damaging fuels (i.e., traditional open wood fires or kerosene) that cause significant household air pollution. Kerosene lamps emit smoke that contains large amounts of fine particulate matter (PM2.5). Typical PM2.5 levels in households using kerosene lamps have been found to be five times higher than the level deemed safe by the World Health Organization.

After replacing kerosene with solar lights, one study found that PM2.5 concentrations fell by as much as 80%. Unsurprisingly, lean data studies have indicated that over 89% of households report health improvements after buying a solar home system. Furthermore, 91% of households reported increased safety after purchasing a clean lighting or cooking product.

## Employment Impact

The renewable energy industry has the potential to generate significant employment in Africa. According to the AfDB and IRENA report, increased investment in renewable energy could create at least 26 million jobs by 2050. As of 2019, the off-grid electrification industry had reportedly generated 370,000 jobs.

Increased human capital infrastructure will be further required to deliver future energy access services. Compounding its positive impact, the industry has strong representation of women workers, and its wages are in line with (if not higher than) average national wages.

KawiSafi Fund II will invest in innovative companies that promote inclusive and sustainable economic growth, through full and productive employment for all. KawiSafi Fund II targets 1) creation of 17,000 direct jobs and 33,000 indirect jobs over the KawiSafi Fund II lifetime.

Through the Technical Assistance Facility and the Gender Action Plan, KawiSafi Fund II intends to increase employment of women across the African energy and mobility ecosystem and drive further investment in female owned and led businesses. The Fund will also support the increased participation of women in the early-stage climate investment ecosystem by partnering with accelerators and incubators to increase gender inclusion in their cohorts. This is further elaborated in the Technical Assistance Facility section and Gender Action Plan attached as Annex 8.

## Economic Co-Benefits

Off-grid solar is generating opportunities across the globe, creating new jobs and growing income. Recent research in East Africa found that 34% of households report an increase in economic activity because of their solar home systems. 28% of households using solar home systems report an average increase of $46 in monthly income – equivalent to 14% of per household national monthly income (based on gross national income).

In more than one-third of households, customers use their systems for business or income generation; 81% of which see this resulting in increased revenues. Improved access to light and productive power, as well as other low carbon technologies, is expected to support increased economic activity in rural communities and resultantly support increased resilience.

## Fund Objectives

KawiSafi Fund II will continue to target companies that i) accelerate the path towards universal access to affordable, reliable, sustainable, and modern energy and ii) take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts. Through these investments, KawiSafi II expects to reduce vulnerability of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly to the effects of climate change and avert 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq emissions over the life of the fund.

# D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

1. KawiSafi Fund II builds on KawiSafi Fund I’s local presence and expertise. The KawiSafi team is based in Nairobi, Kenya and plays an active role in the impact and investment communities there and travels frequently to Kigali, given Kenya and Rwanda are the two countries for KawiSafi Fund I . Its in-region perspective drives the investment strategy, network access, pipeline development, and broader stakeholder engagement.  

2. Acumen, as AE, is committed to local country operations and expertise. Acumen has local offices in each of the regions where it invests (East Africa, West Africa, India, Pakistan, and Latin America), staffed by local employees that understand consumer needs and cultural context, and bring extensive local networks. Acumen’s teams across the world work with relevant local government, NGO, and private sector partners to execute on its investment and impact goals and ensure alignment with local policies.  

3. KawiSafi Fund II builds on the KawiSafi Fund I geographic scope, intending to invest in multiple countries across Africa, with a focus on East, West, and Southern Africa. This geographic footprint is informed by the ground-up process of investing with Fund I, working with companies as they scale regionally, and identifying new opportunities in adjacent markets. This process has created a nuanced understanding of the ability to scale and replicate certain business models, and the need for new approaches based on local context. Based on the team’s market proximity, KawiSafi Fund II has identified an immediate pipeline of $500M, out of a wider opportunity pool of just under $2B across the continent. The KawiSafi team has invested time and resource into developing networks within the proposed KawiSafi Fund II focus countries and has leveraged these networks to identify best-in-class local companies that are meeting the needs of individuals and businesses in their countries.  

4. As highlighted in the market study, each of the activities indicated are aligned with countries’ national mitigation and adaptation plans. The tea conducted extensive desk research and analysis, engaging climate science experts, to understand the climate challenges and needs in each local country context. This has informed the KawiSafi Fund II investment strategy and mandate. The team commits to ensuring that this alignment with country climate priorities is maintained throughout the life of KawiSafi Fund II through regular dialogue with Government representatives and other stakeholders.  

5. In preparation of this Funding proposal, the KawiSafi team engaged public and private sector players across the KawiSafi Fund II focus countries to build relationships and ensure that the targeted interventions are aligned with individual country contexts. In addition to its public sector engagement, the team has been in active discussions with local investors, industry associations, early-stage accelerators, and entrepreneurs across the target markets. We have also reached out to broader civil society organizations as described in our Stakeholder Engagement Plan. The team has travelled on mission to Cote d’Ivoire, Ghana, Kenya, Nigeria, Senegal, Rwanda, and held online stakeholder webinars for each country with 150+ attendees in aggregate. The team has also entered into MOUs and NDAs with organizations within the focus countries for sharing pipeline companies and ongoing collaboration on KawiSafi Fund II. The KawiSafi team, with support of the AE, will continue this high level of engagement with local country stakeholders throughout the lifetime of the Fund.  

6. Together with the AE, the KawiSafi team has committed to have structured engagement with the NDAs in their countries of focus across the following different channels:  
   (i) KawiSafi will provide quarterly reports to the NDAs with comprehensive information on the Fund progress and activities within the countries;  
   (ii) KawiSafi will hold periodic 1-on-1 calls with the NDAs to present the Fund’s progress and insights, as well as hear latest government priorities in each country;  
   (iii) the AE and KawiSafi will hold an annual learning event to bring all NDA representatives together as well as CSO representatives and gender experts and ministries for shared learning and cross-pollination of ideas, either around the GCF annual meetings in South Korea or other location.

# D. GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 69

## 7. To date, KawiSafi Fund II has received six (6) NOLs in support of this proposal (Cote d’Ivoire, DR Congo, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda, Zambia).60

## D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

1. Acumen is requesting that the GCF invests $50M Equity capital into the Equity fund and $2.5M Grant capital into the TAF. This vehicle, and GCF’s anchoring investment, will enable KawiSafi II to raise additional capital from like-minded investors that want to achieve the same goals. We believe we can leverage GCF’s investment capital on a 4:1 ratio, targeting a $200M fund. We further believe that GCF’s $2.5M of grant capital could be leveraged on a 3:1 ratio, leading to a $10M Technical Assistance Facility.

2. Given the impact focus of KawiSafi II and the risk profile offered by the underlying investments (exacerbated by the perception of higher risk in emerging markets, particularly Africa), traditional private commercial capital is not interested under standard terms. For that reason, we are requesting that part of GCF’s commitment take the form of Junior Equity, providing a first-loss cover for senior, commercial investors, as well as return enhancement in upside scenarios. GCF would not be crowding out private capital, but rather catalyzing investment by private capital managers seeking to achieve social impact and financial return.

3. Acumen and KawiSafi have strong track records of deploying and returning capital. The appropriate internal controls are in place to monitor investments and report to investors.

4. The Fund seeks to generate positive financial returns to investors. This desire promotes sustainable business models in the underlying investee companies, keeping a focus on strong product-market fit, and appropriate pricing and credit policies, to ensure strong businesses achieve significant scale. In turn, it also builds the track record of impact funds to support future investment from return-seeking investors.

5. Based on our proposed investment vehicle and the resulting reduction cost of $13.67 per tCO2eq ($3.21 per tCO2eq for GCF capital), we believe this proposal to be competitive amongst other interventions that have aimed to reduce CO2 emissions.

6. EPA and other federal agencies use the social cost of carbon (SCC) to estimate the climate benefits of rulemakings. The SCC is an estimate of the economic damages associated with a small increase in carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions, conventionally one metric ton, each year. This dollar figure also represents the value of damages avoided for a small emission reduction (i.e., the benefit of a CO2 reduction). The EPA estimates the social cost of CO2 to be between $14 and $138.61

7. Market pricing for carbon credits has resulted in a range of pricing. Recent transaction activity in the KawiSafi Fund I portfolio indicated a range of $3 to $20 for expected carbon credit pricing for 2022-2023. We note that this pricing is affected by several factors, particularly regulatory environment, and has been subject to significant volatility over time.

8. We believe our proposal provides a cost-effective means of reducing CO2 emissions while also having significant mitigation and adaptation impacts for low-income customers and beneficiaries (including those living below the poverty line).

60 As of 15th June 2023, we received six (6) NOLs from Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda and Zambia. The AE is in advanced discussions with several other countries, including (without limitation) Ghana, Senegal, South Africa. These countries, amongst others, all expressed significant interest in the FP but haven’t been able to complete their internal sign-off processes due to COVID-19 and other reasons. Nevertheless, we expect to receive NOLs from most, if not all, of these countries before the publication deadline. Additional countries may be added to the programme after initial Board approval, provided they submit an NOL and the addition is approved by the Board.

## Replicability

There are few financing platforms/solutions to drive deployment of the necessary low-carbon and climate resilient technology in Africa.

Low  
Influx of institutional capital into the sector from multiple providers, deploying the much-needed equity capital at scale for renewable energy companies in Africa. The capital provided is fit-for purpose and applies best practices developed from KawiSafi I’s experience.

KawiSafi II will be established and provide financing ($200M over the investment period of the fund) across the clean-energy space. Financing solutions will be new to the market or build on existing best practices across different sub-sectors. Successful implementation of these structures/solutions will promote replication both within and outside Africa.

Further, KawiSafi II will support employment opportunities at the investee company level which will also help develop capacity within the sector.

## Sustainability

Investments into the climate technology sector and climate solutions are growing, however sustainable and profitable business models across various sub-sectors (distributed renewables, green productivity, e-mobility) are yet to be.

Low  
The creation of an enabling environment to support continued growth of the clean-energy sector in Africa, including new participants and sustainable business models. Sustained availability of financing for key segments of the climate technology sector and climate solutions where there is significant potential to reduce GHG emissions and improve climate resilience at the household and business level.

KawiSafi II will implement best in class governance, institutional, and fiduciary frameworks to provide financing solutions to the sector.

The exit strategy envisages that KawiSafi II will sell the investee companies to new or existing market participants (e.g., investors) who will continue to operate the investee companies and therefore continue to finance the sector.

## Core Indicator 6: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to technology deployment, dissemination, development or transfer and innovation

Necessary low-carbon and climate resilient technologies already exist in Africa. However, there is limited access to finance (acting as the most significant barrier). There is a need for innovative financial solutions to de-risk institutional investors, pool funds at scale, and provide the much-needed equity capital for renewable energy companies in Africa.

**Medium**

A mature market for low-carbon and climate resilient technologies. This will involve not only conversion to existing technology, but private sector investing further in research and development across the sector.

KawiSafi II will seek to identify investment opportunities that promote radical affordability of essential services, demonstrating that nations can leapfrog fossil-fuels to clean energy.

**Multi-countries**

## Core Indicator 7: Degree to which GCF Investments contribute to market development/transformation at the sectoral, local, or national level

Africa's clean technology sector has immense potential to leapfrog fossil fuels, however it remains in its nascency for the most part. Despite the regulatory and policy frameworks in place, the markets in most sub-sectors remain relatively limited and best practice business models are still being developed.

**Increases in capital (and financing options in particular) are needed to support large-scale investment that will contribute to overall market**

**Medium**

Strong financial markets offering a range of products to support investment of capital across the sector, which increases the penetration of low-carbon and climate resilient technologies. This should in turn, put downward pressure on the cost curve and make these technologies more attractive compared to traditional fossil fuel alternatives.

KawiSafi II will provide innovative financing solutions to investee companies across the space which will not only support scaling up of low-carbon and climate resilient technologies, but also have the potential to be replicated by other financiers to further scale-up the market impact
