# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 100

## Aligned on impact thesis and whose goals are aligned directly with the generation of both resilience and mitigation climate impacts in the countries of focus.

- Beneficiaries will be assessed by the portfolio companies, who are required to report data to KawiSafi II on a quarterly basis. The KawiSafi team will conduct periodic surveys and audits to ensure the data is accurate and up to date.
- Importantly, KawiSafi II ensures that it invests in companies that are impact aligned in terms of both climate resilience and mitigation impacts.
- In the investment legal documents, KawiSafi II requires its companies to report on impact data and the companies also enter into binding covenants to act in accordance with KawiSafi II's impact principles.

## On a quarterly and annual basis, KawiSafi II shall track reported impact, with support from independent impact experts to verify impact performance. The verification process is aligned with leading industry standards set for the sectors of focus including the energy transition, productivity and e-mobility sectors.

## ESG Reporting

- KawiSafi II investors will receive an annual report on the ESG performance of its investee companies.
- KawiSafi II will actively monitor ESG matters in portfolio companies from point of investment onwards. This will typically include:
  - Monitoring development of ESG risk areas,
  - Assessing progress made on the ESG Action Plan, and
  - Evaluating any changes in the business, which might create additional ESG risks.

## Annual review

- The Fund will conduct an independently run ESG review of each portfolio company using an ESG expert.
- The purpose of the annual review is to assess ESG performance of the portfolio companies, identify those companies facing significant challenges implementing ESG action plans (as well as those with superior performance), and identify new ESG risks related to the business models.
- Insights gathered will foster detailed discussion amongst the investment team and the IC to determine how to best support the portfolio companies going forward.
- Further, the fund will conduct both a mid-point and completion date review of the action plans to assess progress with the adoption of ESG gaps identified during the diligence stage and note additional gaps/inform additional initiatives to close on action items.

## Mid-term evaluation

- At the time of the Mid-term evaluation, it is not expected that significant development results will have been achieved, since the portfolio will not have achieved full maturity.
- Therefore, the implementation of the investment strategy will be the focus of the evaluation and will also cover aspects like the number of portfolio companies invested by the Fund, GHG avoidance, energy production, installed capacity, private sector capital catalyzed and total number of people served/impacted.

## Final evaluation

- For the final evaluations it is expected that the fund will have achieved maturity.
- The end of program evaluation of the Fund will comply with the GCF evaluation policy.
- An independent evaluation will track key performance indicators and results, as well as gender and other inclusion targets.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

As an investment fund focusing on innovative businesses in developing markets, KawiSafi Fund II, and the portfolio companies in which it plans to invest, may face a host of challenges. In line with Acumen’s Risk Management Approach, risks are assessed across the following categories:

1. **Regulatory:** Workplace safety; AML and Corruption; Tax matters; SEC; etc.
2. **Assets and Programs:** IP, Assets Under Management; Staff matters; Reputational risks;
3. **Geographies:** Regional security; Political and regulatory stability; Culture; and
4. **Conflicts and Operations:** Governance; Business continuity; Government donor complexity; Fund oversight.

Identified risks are prioritized based on estimated likelihood and estimated magnitude of impact of a risk event (on a scale of 1-3). The resulting logic concludes that more resources and focus are dedicated to risks where there is a high-likelihood and high-magnitude (3:3) of an adverse event and fewer resources and less focus are dedicated to low-likelihood and low-magnitude (1:1) risks. Ratings are based on the Risk Committee’s best understanding and knowledge of each risk, informed by available qualitative and quantitative data, but are ultimately subjective.

Responses to identified risks can range from continued monitoring to expert consultation, insurance, or proactive projects (e.g., new training programs, policies, processes, etc.), among other interventions. The Risk Manager makes recommendations, approves the final action plans, and is ultimately accountable for monitoring their implementation.

The Managing Director plans to regularly evaluate the overall risk of KawiSafi II and its investments. S/he will also prepare a periodic report for the IC, which identifies risks from the previous report and includes recommendations for procedures to mitigate and address such risks. A business risk assessment will be provided to the KawiSafi Fund II Board of Directors for their review and consideration.

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
|----------|-------------|--------|
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Execution Challenges & Risks

- KawiSafi II will invest in a mix of early growth stage, innovative companies. By definition, these enterprises operate with a lot of unknowns around their business model, company operations, and market dynamics. These enterprises may fail or may take longer than anticipated to achieve financially viable business models.
- KawiSafi II generally will seek significant minority stakes with strong contractual rights, though it sometimes may have a majority position in consortium with other co-investors. As such, KawiSafi II does not expect to control the activities of its portfolio companies.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

KawiSafi II has rigorous due diligence processes which include analysis of financial, operational, market, and ESS issues. The due diligence process provides the ability to assess high quality investment opportunities and select enterprises with strong management teams, high quality products, and strong brand names and reputation. The Fund will not only provide investment capital but expects to provide strategic advice and additional resources through such actions as sitting on the Board of companies, identifying and working with co-investors, providing access to local networks of experts and value-add partners, and offering specific technical assistance programs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 102

## Technical and operational Medium Medium

### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

### Regulatory Uncertainty
KawiSafi II will invest in companies providing energy solutions. Energy is a regulated industry and in some countries, there are subsidies in place to reduce the cost of power to end-users. This has the distortionary effect of crowding out private solutions and creating an even greater gap in generation capacity. Subsidies and other interventions make it more difficult for private providers to offer cost-effective, competitive solutions. Where they are in place, companies can assess the impact on pricing and whether a certain market is attractive for investment. Therefore, the key risk is changes to subsidy and other policies that benefit only certain providers and may deter investment.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The Fund will assess regulatory risk in the markets where it operates and will consult with local regulatory agencies to understand their planning processes and potential for changes. The Fund will also carry out regular regulatory and legislative reviews across its verticals and markets to ensure potential adverse impacts are mitigated. The Fund will invest in distributed energy strategies that have greater flexibility to move assets to adapt to adverse local conditions as necessary.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

### Category: Probability Impact
Other Medium Medium

### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

### Exit and Valuation Risks
- KawiSafi II will invest in companies in markets which have limited options for liquidity events / exits due to lack of developed financial markets, including lack of an abundance of private equity and venture capital firms. Many of the countries in which KawiSafi Fund II seeks to invest control, to some extent, the repatriation of investment income, capital and profit that result from foreign investment.
- Accounting standards in the countries in which KawiSafi Fund II seeks to invest do not generally correspond to international accounting standards, and national accounting, auditing and financial reporting standards are not yet in place in many target countries. Many portfolio investments will generate revenue that is denominated in foreign currency, and changes to exchange rates may affect the value of the Fund's investments in portfolio companies.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The Investment Team expects to maintain ongoing relationships with potential strategic acquirers of portfolio companies and growth stage investment firms to help improve exit opportunities for Fund investments. KawiSafi II will carefully monitor investments and utilize regional teams to stay abreast of changing markets and company conditions.

KawiSafi II will require its portfolio companies to have annual financial audits to improve transparency of financials and accounting systems.

In addition, KawiSafi Fund II may utilize alternative investment structures reflective of market conditions. In addition to investing equity, KawiSafi Fund II may structure debt instruments and equity-like instruments that have a pre-determined liquidation date to help improve the likelihood of financial exits. The team has also used vehicles such as dividends and warrants to help offset the unpredictability of markets.

## Technical and operational Medium Low

### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

#### Human rights risks in the supply chain
- Polysilicon is the main raw material in the production of solar cells. Four of the world's five largest polysilicon producers are based in close proximity to one another.
- There has been growing unease about allegations of the use of forced labor in polysilicon-producing markets.
- The increased demand for polysilicon due to the energy transition has led to ongoing investments in capacity in these regions. There is a risk that labor and human rights issues across the supply chain persist jeopardizing the positive impact of the sector and affecting the rate of investment into low-carbon initiatives.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

- It is important to note that due to the expected size and nature of KawiSafi Fund II's investment portfolio, KawiSafi and its investee companies may have limitations in the information they can collect on human rights risks or the influence they can have over supply chain practices. However, it is imperative that this issue be assessed and addressed to the extent possible.
- The KawiSafi II team will take a risk-based approach (as with Fund I). The team will engage its portfolio companies that are either operating in, or sourcing from certain geographies under scrutiny (or other countries associated with human rights abuses) to enhance disclosure and transparency of labor conditions within their supply chain.
Specifically:
- KawiSafi II's investee companies will undergo ESG due diligence prior to investment as well as annual ESG monitoring. The analysis includes desk-based review, direct engagement with key individuals within the company and site visits from third party evaluators. The evaluation will directly cover aspects of 1) organizational context and geographic footprint to gain a high level sense of the extent to which the company is aware of/integrated with global initiatives to combat Human Rights and responsible supply chains; 2) aligned to core ILO standards on labor; 3) addressing the protection of Human Rights, responsible use of security forces; prevention of harmful child and forced labor; 4) freedom of association; and 5) ethical sourcing policies and practices; among others.
- The KawiSafi II team will seek to ensure that investees have effective Employee and Stakeholder Grievance Procedures — including training of staff, clear explanations and channels on company websites, and in HR Policies; and an anonymous reporting option.
- The KawiSafi II team will establish procedures to engage with companies on their human rights and labor policies, including support for strengthening supplier auditing and monitoring practices for Companies at risk. Engagement can be in multiple forms: through Board engagement; one-on-one meetings with management; as well as embedding strong requirements in the ESG Action Plans and related monitoring.
- The KawiSafi II team will report annually on the implementation of the above practices.
- The KawiSafi II team will also participate in multi-stakeholder coalitions. As a member of GOGLA, an international industry group for the off-grid solar sector, KawiSafi II will continue to support the public condemnation of any human rights violations in the solar supply chain as well as supporting the industry body develop strong supply chain traceability protocols, which can help prevent human rights violations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 104

## Specifically

Off-grid solar waste represents only a fraction of the total sum of e-waste produced, even in countries with a mature off-grid solar market. For example, in Kenya, off-grid solar waste is about 3% of the total e-waste generated and in Rwanda and Nigeria off-grid solar represents only 0.4% and 0.02% respectively. While data on solar waste is not available, it is important to note that Africa contributes the lowest amounts of e-waste per capita globally, estimated at 1.9kg/person. Compared to Asia (4.2kg/person) and Europe and the Americas which are estimated to generate 16.6kg and 11.6kg per person respectively.

## The challenges of distributed solar products in Africa include

- a shorter life span of components such as batteries
- location, as most of these products are deployed in off-grid/mostly rural communities
- difficulty in the collection of the waste components

## The generated waste contains toxic materials

such as lead, mercury, cadmium, arsenic, and polychlorinated biphenyls which are harmful to the environment and human health if improperly managed. In Africa, a lack of legal provisions and appropriate collection and recycling infrastructure means that safe transport, disposal, and waste handling is not formalized. Informal handlers trying to exploit the economic value contained in the products (e.g., copper and lead) with rudimentary recycling processes and little regard to emission control lead to adverse effects to the environment and public health.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The KawiSafi II team will mitigate this risk through I) portfolio company efforts; and II) ecosystem efforts.

- With regard to the portfolio of investments, KawiSafi II will:
  1. Invest in businesses that have embedded circularity into their business models – these include waste to energy businesses; recycling businesses; repurposing and re-use of second life lithium ion batteries among others. The opportunity is critical given the projected scarcity of these resources as global energy transition efforts continue to scale.
  2. The Fund team will evaluate, at time of investment, each pipeline Company's commitment to end-of-life product management. This could be through take-back and collection mechanisms; repurposing and refurbishment efforts as well as product design to enhance recyclability. The team will then continue to monitor this over the life of the investment.

- At an ecosystem level: the Kawisafi II team will participate in industry association efforts.
  - Acumen/Kawisafi is already a member of GOGLA and sits in multiple committees including the consumer protection committee that works on promoting best practice in e-waste across the sector. GOGLA is in active discussions with policy makers in Kenya, Ghana and Cote d'Ivoire on potential e-waste legislation.
  - The Fund also expects to support best practice efforts through the Technical Assistance Facility under activity 1.4 which seeks to support implementation of consumer protection standards, quality assurance, and best practices across the industries of focus.

#### Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

Risk of Conflicts in Fragile States  
Two of the countries of focus for KawiSafi Fund II – the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) and Nigeria are ranked 6th and 16th on the Fragile States Index respectively. The index reviews twelve conflict risk indicators including security apparatus, factionalized elites, group grievances, economic decline, uneven economic development, human flight and brain drain, state legitimacy, public service, human rights and rule of law, demographic pressures, refugees and IDPs and external interventions. While the DRC suffers from poor governance and infrastructure, weak basic services, ongoing conflict and has been a long-running humanitarian crises, Nigeria on the other hand ranks low on human flight and brain drain and provision of security in the North leading to high levels of IDPs and refugees. Other countries of Focus rank between 25th to 108th on the Fragile States Index reflecting different levels of perceived risk by country.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

At the portfolio level, the KawiSafi team will seek to ensure country diversification to limit significant exposure to any of the highly fragile countries. However, given the contextual nature of each territory, the team will carry out country-based risk assessments which will include both formal and informal political economy factors driving security of the investment; informal power relations and their impact on potential business performance. In investments in newer markets, KawiSafi will seek to partner with co-investors with experience navigating these risks in highly fragile countries.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

IFC categorization. Fund II is classified as I-2 by GCF’s Revised Environmental and Social Policy (2021), requiring medium level of intermediation.

### Overview of KawiSafi’s approach

KawiSafi has a systematic approach to integrating environmental, social, and governance (ESG) management into operations and investment activities, with robust policies in place. The principles and commitments stated in the ESG Policies are a cornerstone of all business operations of Fund II. Compliance with the ESG policy will be achieved through an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) Implemented at two levels.

- **ESMS at Portfolio Companies**: Portfolio Companies will be required to adhere to the ESG requirements of the Fund. Each Portfolio Company will establish and maintain its own ESMS to assess, address, and monitor ESG risks and impacts of company operations in line with KawiSafi Fund II requirements.
- **ESMS at Fund level**: The KawiSafi management team will establish and maintain the KawiSafi Fund II ESMS to assess, oversee, and support the management of ESG matters by the Portfolio Companies, as well as oversee ESG matters at overall Portfolio level.

The operational guidelines aim to provide the information needed to support the implementation and operationalization of the ESMS. They are based on the IFC Policy and Performance Standards on Social and Environmental Sustainability 2012, and its Guidance Notes, which have been harmonized with the GCF’s Environmental and Social Principles and Standards.

KawiSafi II therefore intends to implement an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) for Fund II as required by GCF’s Updated Environmental and Social Policy, included herein as Appendix 6. KawiSafi’s approach is aligned with international good practice, including GCF policies and safeguards, Principles for Responsible Investments (PRI), UN Global Compact, and the IFC Exclusion List and Performance Standards.

KawiSafi has a Grievance Policy, aligned with GCF standards.

### Approach to ESG Due Diligence and Monitoring

In-depth ESG due diligence is conducted for all investments with a third-party provider. Fund II will build on the experience of Fund I, where an ESG Action Plan is agreed upon and embedded in investment agreements. ESG risk and performance is monitored throughout the life of an investment, including a comprehensive, annual ESG data collection and review.

### Key risk considerations

Fund II will focus on commercial and residential low-income customers in Africa, and on technological interventions such as electric vehicles, cell towers, off-grid solar products, smart meter, and financing and software services to support these activities. Activities are not expected to have material climate risks, but rather to contribute to climate mitigation and adaptation.

Investee companies will be encouraged to monitor and manage their exposure to physical and transition risks of climate change, including in their supply chains.

### Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH) risk management

SEAH risk management is addressed in ESG assessments, which consider women in the workforce and company policies and procedures. KawiSafi investments seek to have a positive effect on the lives and livelihoods of women. KawiSafi intends to establish a SEAH policy in 2023.

### Indigenous people

Indigenous people are not likely to be directly affected through the activities of investee companies. KawiSafi monitors exposure to and impact on indigenous peoples in ESG due diligence and annual review.

### Community health, safety, and security, and the environment

Community health, safety, and security, and the environment may be impacted by use and disposal of off-grid solar, ICT, and electric vehicle technologies, including emerging risks associated with radiation from cell towers and smart meter. KawiSafi will monitor emerging practices for managing e-waste disposal, responsible sourcing of materials for manufacturing, and health effects of electromagnetic radiation. Fund I established strong consumer protection diligence, aligned to GOGLA consumer protection principles and post-investment support, which will be continued in Fund II.

KawiSafi is committed to improving the circularity of off-grid solar technology and minimization of e-waste and engages on this topic within GOGLA (global association for the off-grid solar energy industry). KawiSafi seeks to ensure that portfolio companies either have or will develop strong e-waste policies and procedures.

KawiSafi will continue to strengthen its monitoring and management of supply chain ESG management. A particular

## Gender Action Plan

The Gender Action plan further acknowledges that mitigation and adaptation initiatives are more sustainable, equitable and more likely to achieve their objectives when gender equality is integrated, especially given the disproportionate positive impacts of energy access to women and the key participation of women as consumers of energy services at the household level.

## Development Process

2. The process of developing this Action plan included reflections from KawiSafi Fund I performance to date and multiple discussions with the Accredited Entity (Acumen) on importance and best practice of integrating portfolio gender performance in venture capital fund structures. The team also reviewed gender-lens investing frameworks such as the 2X Challenge which supports increased funding for women across the investment value chain specifically promoting female entrepreneurship; leadership; employment; and consumption of energy services. In addition, the team reviewed existing literature by Acumen as well as from other similar development and investing organizations to understand the unique gender challenges of the energy industry – with specific focus on distributed energy for residential and productive use.

## Activities Designed to Drive Integration of Gender at KawiSafi Fund II

### Fund Level

At the Fund level, the KawiSafi Fund II team is committed to ensuring alignment to gender best practices, in terms of:

- A minimum of 50% female representation in KawiSafi Fund II team employment; and
- Integration of gender lens investing practices across the investment process to achieve portfolio-level impacts; and commitment to ongoing gender-disaggregated impact reporting, monitoring and development of Gender Action Plans for every investment made.

### Portfolio Level

At a portfolio level, we intend to:

- Evaluate all our potential investments based on their potential to drive gender inclusion and increase women’s access to economic opportunities. We will specifically use our capital to bridge:
  1. Existing funding gaps to female owned and led businesses. It is currently estimated that only 7% of emerging market venture capital goes to women-owned businesses67. Female founders are underrepresented in the sectors that attract the most financing, moreover research indicates that all-female teams remain less likely to receive financing than all-male teams, and they receive smaller amounts if they do (all-male founding teams receive US$ 25 for each US$ 1 going to all-female teams)68.;
  2. Businesses that provide decent work to women. According to studies, 35% of the workforce in renewable energy companies are women compared with 20-25% in the broader energy sector. KawiSafi Fund II will use capital to grow this proportion in our portfolio companies to 40% and above;
  3. Businesses that provide women access to energy services or that sell products that disproportionately improve outcomes for women. For instance, a high reliance on biomass for cooking in many countries means that women and children without clean cooking access spend an average of 1.4 hours/day collecting fuel. Greater access to energy services can improve women’s health and well-being, free up their time and enable their economic empowerment, thereby supporting the achievement of SDG 5.

- The Fund will also support portfolio companies in enhancing their gender inclusion and diversity practices and drive greater women’s economic empowerment outcomes through their business activities. This will be offered through development of Gender Action Plans for each investment made which will among other things support increased female leadership in management and Governance structures. The team also seeks to support companies in developing policies and procedures to prevent SEAH and provide gender-sensitive and survivor-centered incident responses. The team will implement this through direct participation of KawiSafi II’s board representative on the portfolio

## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy, and
  - Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.
  
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
## Page 124  

### Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
## FP210  

### Proposal name: KawiSafi Fund II  
### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund  
### Project/programme size: Medium  

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

### 1.1 Overview  

1. The KawiSafi II funding proposal is an evolution of the KawiSafi Ventures Fund (also known as KawiSafi I), which was launched by the accredited entity (AE), Acumen Fund (hereinafter referred to as Acumen), in 2016 and was one of the first funding proposals approved by the GCF Board. KawiSafi I is a USD 67 million fund (10-year lifespan) that has invested in low- and zero-carbon technology businesses in two countries: Kenya and Rwanda. The fund is still in its investment period, and it intends to make one more new investment in 2023, and then will reserve follow-on for existing investments to deploy during the management period. Remaining funds are expected to cover fees and expenses over the fund life. KawiSafi I investments have been focused on late venture-stage opportunities and relatively small ticket sizes, ranging from USD 500,000 to 5 million.  

2. KawiSafi II is a follow-on fund to KawiSafi I and is expected to be more ambitious in terms of capital raised, investment size and geographical reach. The Acumen funding proposal aims for a USD 200 million venture equity fund utilizing a blended finance structure that is intended to mobilize private sector climate investment across 10 African countries. It will target investments in companies at the late-venture/early-growth stage that have business models that deploy low-carbon technologies across energy transition, green productivity, mobility, and logistics. Investments are expected to range between USD 1 million and 10 million.  

3. As of the date of the funding proposal, KawiSafi II had received six no-objection letters (NOLs) from the governments of Côte d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of the Congo, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda, and Zambia. The AE team expects to receive four additional NOLs by the thirty-sixth meeting of the GCF Board (B.36), from Ghana, Kenya, Senegal, and South Africa.  

4. The requested contribution from GCF is USD 52.5 million, of which USD 50 million is in the form of equity and USD 2.5 million is in the form of grant. The grant funding is to establish and implement the technical assistance facility (TAF) to support both portfolio companies and the wider energy and climate ecosystem in the relevant African markets.  

### 1.2 Impact potential  
#### Scale: Medium  

5. KawiSafi II expects to invest in the energy, mobility, and productivity (clean cooling, agriculture-energy, green manufacturing) sectors. According to the project sponsor, these sectors are projected to avoid emissions of 20.9 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2 eq) and directly and indirectly strengthening the resiliency to climate impacts of approximately 3.5 million people.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
## Page 125  

6. These sorts of prospective impact estimates for fund vehicles are difficult to verify as they involve many variables (e.g. nature of investee companies, execution by investees, timing of investments and so on) that cannot be pre-determined for an equity fund. Fund structures by their nature are unable to provide certainty as to the ultimate composition of their portfolio. This is not necessarily an adverse problem as funds can incorporate a level of flexibility and market responsiveness that often ensures greater effectiveness over time in achieving climate impacts compare with a ready-made set of interventions. However, that does imply some uncertainty in assessing probable impact outcomes. In this regard, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) needs to look particularly carefully at other indicators to support a positive assessment including, but not limited to (i) track record of the fund manager, (ii) target sectors and concentration limits, (iii) indicative pipeline and illustrative investments, and (iv) the overall investment selection and screening process.  

7. In the case of KawiSafi II, GCF can take some comfort from the track record of KawiSafi Fund I, which has reported that its investments have cumulatively avoided 17.8 Mt CO₂ eq emissions in Kenya and 2.4 Mt CO₂ eq in Rwanda. Additionally, the KawiSafi I portfolio maps to an investment selection process that prioritizes business models with a clear mitigation and/or adaptation impact. The iTAP considers it reasonable to presume that a similar approach will carry over to the management of KawiSafi II. Further, in reviewing the KawiSafi II funding proposal, iTAP also noted that the indicative pipeline, concentration targets and overall selection criteria were reasonably aligned with achieving the impact target estimates made by the AE.  

8. Moreover, iTAP notes that the impact potential of early-stage equity investment in emerging market climate businesses can go beyond first-order mitigation and adaptation directly linked to those investments. Providing financial wherewithal to local firms in developing countries so that they can grow and deploy climate-friendly technologies across their markets is inherently impactful. It builds local professional and technical capacity and the networks necessary to transition emerging markets to low-carbon and resilient pathways. This is true for the companies receiving direct investments but also for the overall ecosystem of service providers, local financiers, and project development professionals. The economic activity and technology adoption that well-funded local climate businesses can engender in their own markets lays the groundwork not just for their success but for subsequent efforts by demonstrating that African markets can leapfrog fossil-fuel grids to deploy clean technologies.  

9. KawiSafi II expects to make investments in several sectors that have the potential to produce positive mitigation and resiliency impacts. One example is residential lighting, where a technology shift away from fuels such as kerosene should result not just in direct health-related benefits but also provide access to power with the potential to catalyse economic development in rural areas and create more job opportunities as well as increased opportunities for higher incomes.  

10. KawiSafi II also expects to make investments in commercial and industrial solar power generation; battery storage applications targeted at displacing diesel generators; and energy productivity, which includes deployment of renewable energy for mobile communications towers that are currently powered by diesel generators that result in significant CO₂ eq emissions.  

11. Additionally, the fund expects to make investments in the mobility and logistics sectors focusing on scaling up companies that market electric vehicles (EVs), with a view to increasing the use of low-carbon transport and therefore reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in urban areas. The focus of this aspect of the programme is on funding electric two-wheelers. The iTAP does have some concerns regarding the EV sector discussion in the funding proposal, which considers that assumptions concerning the number of EVs to be supported by the fund is overly optimistic, and the default values for distance driven and fuel saved are not conservative.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
Page 126  

In the case of public transport, iTAP noted methodological errors in the calculations. However, mobility interventions are only estimated to account for a relatively small minority of total expected mitigation impacts.  

12. The mitigation potential of the indicative investment pipeline provided by the AE is more apparent than the funding proposal’s adaptation claims. The AE claims that its portfolio companies will be able to reduce climate vulnerability and enhance the resilience and adaptive capacity of the geographical regions where they will operate. Acumen’s adaptation claims rest on broad statements, such as that the African continent will face adverse changes due to climate change that will impact food security (e.g. surface water and groundwater changes, harvest losses due to extreme weather events).  

13. More credibly, the funding proposal for KawiSafi II includes specific examples of where it expects to see adaptation outcomes, including investments in resilient food systems that could help agricultural communities adapt to a changing climate. Additionally, the funding proposal points to energy sector investments that can have impacts on resilience if applied to the agriculture sector (e.g. through solar-powered irrigation and cold chain storage). Nevertheless, iTAP considers that the adaptation impact potential of KawiSafi II is particularly difficult to assess from the outset. The investment processes proposed by Acumen provide some comfort on how the potential for generalized adaptation benefits will be considered. However, iTAP has reduced its assessment level here, in part because of uncertainties around specific adaptation outcomes.  

The iTAP encourages the AE and the GCF Secretariat to consider models that provide a more active role for the GCF in the assessment of climate impacts to better ensure alignment of interests. An active GCF voice around climate impacts is particularly useful where the relationship between the AE and the fund manager is incredibly close, as in KawiSafi II.  

14. Finally, iTAP notes that KawiSafi II expects to create a Technical Assistance Facility (TAF). The TAF will seek to assist both portfolio companies and the wider clean energy ecosystem in Africa. Portfolio companies will receive support to, among other things, develop the tools and capacity needed to measure their environmental, social and governance, and climate impacts, which Acumen notes is a current gap in the market. The ecosystem support will go towards collaboration with early-stage incubators and accelerators, as well as relevant industry associations and academia, to support development and maturation while ensuring that the industry shares the positive impacts of its activities among all members of society, especially women.  

15. Overall, iTAP regards the proposal’s impact potential as Medium.  
## 1.3 Paradigm shift potential     Scale: Medium  

16. KawiSafi II intends to build on KawiSafi I by demonstrating the scalability and replicability of the approach. If successful, KawiSafi II will raise significant private sector capital into a larger fund that will invest across a wider geographical footprint. This has the potential to demonstrate the investibility of low-carbon businesses in Africa and support firms that need to grow if Africa is to have any chance of meeting its energy needs while leapfrogging fossil-fuel grids to deliver clean energy. The iTAP considers that the paradigm shift potential lies in the ability of KawiSafi II to demonstrate that investing in an African clean-energy future is feasible and replicable.  

17. The iTAP considers that Acumen has a moderate likelihood of succeeding. Its concerns rest primarily on the ability of Acumen to raise capital for KawiSafi II from more mainstream investors, especially in the current higher interest rate environment. KawiSafi I relied extensively on investments from high-net-worth individuals and impact-first investors. KawiSafi II needs to make a step change to more commercial investors to contribute to a paradigm shift. Based on its assessment, iTAP is not wholly convinced that this goal will be achieved.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
Page 127  

## Achievable at the scale sought by KawiSafi II

Concerns stem from (i) the status of investor outreach and the type of investors further along in discussions, (ii) KawiSafi I investors re-upping and (iii) overall market conditions and appetite.

## 18. Although the KawiSafi I track record gives comfort around impacts and the screening capacity of the Acumen team, it is less persuasive when it comes to commercial outcomes that will matter to prospective investors. For instance, since 2016 KawiSafi I has deployed under USD 50 million and has only achieved one partial exit. Arguably this is in line with the initial funding proposal, but commercial investors in the current context will want to determine that KawiSafi II can deploy much larger amounts of capital and ultimately provide liquidity for Acumen investments. Indeed, if KawiSafi II is unable to source development opportunities at sufficient scale and quality, or is unable to successfully develop bankable financing opportunities, it may face constraints in terms of its ability to deploy its allocated capital.

## 19. Notwithstanding the above, iTAP does not consider it is a lost cause. For example, Acumen’s other GCF-supported funds (excluding KawiSafi I) have been able to attract a more diverse set of investors. Acumen can point to a portfolio that has grown its net asset value during a very difficult economic period. The macroeconomic environment over the past 3–4 years has included the COVID-19 lockdown, the conflict in Ukraine, and now the challenging macroeconomic situation with rising debt, falling currencies and inflation. Book value is no substitute for actual demonstrated market liquidity and monetization, but it does differentiate KawiSafi II from other efforts in the market that have stalled in fundraising.

## 20. The KawiSafi II team informed iTAP during the review that it is currently in advanced stages of due diligence with multiple investors, with more in earlier stages of evaluation. The iTAP does not contact potential investors as part of its assessment and understands that the GCF Secretariat is often unable to independently diligence and verify this sort of claim. However, iTAP takes some comfort from the AE’s claims that various institutional investors have confirmed to Acumen that they have successfully navigated their own investment committee approval stages. Presumably that means these investors are approved to commit time and financial resources to moving into commercial, legal, tax and environmental, social and governance due diligence.

## 21. If KawiSafi II can raise capital successfully, iTAP considers that investing in for-profit companies that seek to scale clean energy technologies and promote low-carbon growth can contribute positively to paradigm shift. Equity investment is critical for nascent sectors. However, paradigm shift will be achieved only if the fund is able to build a track record of performing, impactful and successful investments, which increases the confidence of asset owners and encourages new investment managers to structure new funds in this sector. Demonstrating commercial success can attract new actors and reduce the cost of capital into these sectors.

## 22. The investee companies themselves are intended to be financially sustainable enterprises that would continue to operate off their own balance sheets and revenues. Successful portfolio companies can attract additional investment in addition to the impacts that their underlying business models generate. Moreover, should KawiSafi II provide strong exits for its investors this could then have a significant paradigm shift potential by contributing to the credibility of climate-friendly investments in Africa. This could be a virtuous cycle that can truly support a paradigm shift in development trajectories.

## 23. Overall, iTAP rates the potential for KawiSafi II to effect paradigm shift as Medium, due to the above-mentioned concerns around raising funds and adaptation impacts.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential    Scale: High

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
## Page 128  

### 24. The funding proposal presents a credible pathway for sustainable development impacts in addition to the potential for knock-on effects around market development and demonstration impact for follow-on funding sources. As noted in section 1.3 above, the specific scope of these impacts depends on the ultimate makeup of the KawiSafi II portfolio and the success of those investments. At this point, iTAP can only make an educated guess on the basis of Acumen’s track record, investment thesis and indicative pipeline. However, the funding proposal and iTAP’s engagement with Acumen provides iTAP with sufficient comfort that KawiSafi II’s investments will be in support of business plans and technologies that can have a measurable beneficial impact in terms of climate change, and will generally support sustainable development.

### 25. Another important facet of the sustainable development potential of KawiSafi II is its focus on African markets. The region has significant development needs, while it is expected to bear the brunt of climate impacts. According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), temperature increases in sub-Saharan Africa are expected to be higher than the global mean temperature increase, having a negative impact on health, agriculture, and food security. According to the World Resources Institute, failure to meet the targets set by the Paris Agreement would mean that 118 million people in Africa will be pushed into extreme poverty by 2030.

### 26. The iTAP also notes that Africa contributes only 3 per cent to global GHG emissions; however, this statistic is misleading given Africa's lack of energy access and its corresponding low rate of consumption. Approximately 550 million people in sub-Saharan Africa do not have access to electricity and must rely on kerosene and diesel generators for lighting, while another 900 million people lack access to clean cooking fuels and technologies, causing rapid deforestation and dangerous levels of indoor air pollution. Africa’s energy consumption and manufacturing output needs to triple over the next two decades to create the economic opportunities and jobs that can lift its people out of poverty. If the Paris Agreement targets are to be met this increase in energy use must be built on a low-carbon matrix. This outcome is only possible if the companies that will be needed to provide low-carbon services and technologies are in place with sufficient capacity and funding. KawiSafi II can make an important contribution to developing this ecosystem of low-carbon businesses.

### 27. The iTAP notes the following specific potential sustainable development contributions:  
(a) Environmental co-benefits: reduction of air and water pollution and improved resource use through recycling and reuse applications. Replacement of kerosene, diesel, and other carbon-based fuels;  
(b) Social and health benefits: access to affordable, reliable, and sustainable energy is crucial for achieving many of the Sustainable Development Goals via advancements in food security; overall health and well-being; education; water supply; and sustainable industrialization. A well-established energy system supports all sectors, from health and education to agriculture, infrastructure, communications, and technology; and  
(c) Employment and economic impact: investing in renewable energy in Africa could create at least 26 million jobs by 2050. The renewable energy industry has already generated 370,000 jobs in off-grid electrification as of 2019, with strong representation of women workers and wages in line with or higher than national averages. According to the funding proposal, KawiSafi II aims to invest in innovative companies that promote inclusive and sustainable economic growth and aims to create 17,000 direct jobs and 33,000 indirect jobs. The funding proposal also credibly posits a focus on increasing gender inclusion in the early-stage climate investment ecosystem and partnering with business accelerators and incubators to increase the participation of women in their cohorts.

### 28. The funding proposal allows for investments in biofuels. The iTAP notes that investments in biofuels have a high risk of creating negative climate and sustainability problems.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
## Page 129  

if not well screened for and managed. First generation biofuels are generally produced from food crops, such as corn, soy, and sugarcane, which make them unattractive from a socioeconomic perspective; while second generation biofuels from “wastes” have displacement issues. The overwhelming majority of so-called wastes are already currently used for other processes and using them for biofuel production results in other processes using other types of raw material (e.g., the waste “bagasse” is also used as input material for paper production; used cooking oils and animal fats are used in foundries or in the chemical industry for soaps which then revert to using primary palm or soy oil). Thus “wastes” are end-of-life products but many of them have an energy use, are used as animal feed or as input product for other processes. Using such wastes can cause displacement emissions when diverted for biofuel production.  

Additionally, biofuel expansion can result in biodiversity loss, direct and indirect land-use change, and deforestation, and potentially higher total GHG emissions than using fossil fuels, plus other environmental and social problems.  

29. The iTAP considers that the funding proposal does not include a credible methodology for determining the direct as well as indirect GHG emissions from biofuels. Notwithstanding that biofuels are projected to play a small role in KawiSafi II, the lack of an effective screen against biofuels investments that are likely to lead to substantial environmental and social risks gives iTAP cause for concern. Biofuel investments could lead to adverse climate results and create a reputational risk for the GCF. Therefore, iTAP considers that the funding proposal needs to exclude financing of biofuel projects with GCF proceeds.  

30. Overall, iTAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of KawiSafi II as High.  
1.5 Needs of the recipient      Scale: High  

31. KawiSafi II requests investment from the GCF on the assumption that concessional funding is required to attract capital from private sector investors who may be risk adverse to Africa, to low-carbon sectors or to both.  

32. The iTAP considers that there is a clear need for additional finance to support Africa’s low-carbon sectors, particularly early-stage risk capital that can fund the scaling up of the low-carbon energy ecosystem. The funding proposal effectively demonstrates that all 10 targeted countries continue to face persistent funding gaps regarding the need to crowd-in private capital in support of greener and more sustainable economies.  

33. However, iTAP notes that the prioritized countries represent very different economic realities and considers that KawiSafi II will face a challenge in operating across 10 different economies each with their own conditions and needs. KawiSafi II will face real operational challenges in originating deal flow across such varied economies and having sufficient know-how to structure and manage a much more diverse portfolio than KawiSafi I.  

34. In terms of the needs of the recipients, iTAP notes that some of the priority markets are starting to demonstrate improved conditions in attracting finance. It is clear that there remains a real need for additional funding sources, but iTAP also notes with concern that, over the long term (10 years + 2-year renewal), there is a risk that GCF concessionality could contribute to market distortions in rapidly developing markets. This, coupled with the fact that other priority markets are tremendously underdeveloped, could potentially limit deal flow in those countries and increase pressure on KawiSafi II’s manager to deploy primarily in the quickly improving markets.  

35. The funding proposal has features that mitigate the above-mentioned risk, particularly the cap on GCF funding (25 per cent), concentration limits and ultimately the overall expertise and experience of Acumen as reflected in its management of multiple funds in the region.

Cumulatively, Acumen has directly invested USD 149 million in 157 companies operating across 13 countries.  

36. The iTAP also takes comfort from the waterfall structure and preferred return structure that appears to be reasonable and within the current expectations around blended finance. Under the waterfall structure, the GCF concessional fund only supports private sector capital. Additionally, the GCF will recover its equity investment prior to any distributions for the senior equity (private sector investors) preferred return. This mitigates the risk that the KawiSafi managers leverage GCF concessionality to provide a return to other investors in lieu of adequate portfolio performance. Additionally, the proposed preferred return for senior equity and for junior equity (GCF) come before any carried interest pay-out to managers. 37. Market intelligence can be a useful data point in ensuring that GCF concessionality is fit for purpose.  

38. Overall, iTAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of KawiSafi II as High.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.05  
## Page 131  

### Government Priorities and Country Ownership  
44. The iTAP considers that KawiSafi II is aligned with host country government priorities.  
The one area where iTAP would have preferred additional evidence is regarding the likelihood  
of raising capital from local markets, especially in target countries that have more developed  
financial markets such as Nigeria, Kenya, and South Africa. The iTAP would encourage capital  
raising from local investors and local government support as a strong indicator of country  
ownership. Overall, iTAP rates the country ownership of the funding proposal as Medium-High.  

### Efficiency and Effectiveness  
45. The AE is requesting that GCF invest USD 50 million of equity capital into a 10-year fund  
(plus up to two 1-year extensions, i.e. 12 years maximum). This vehicle, and GCF’s anchoring  
investment, is intended to enable Acumen to raise additional capital from private sector  
investors who are interested in the same goals but have a lower risk appetite. The proposed  
fund structure will be tiered and will include a junior and senior equity layer: USD 40 million in  
GCF funding would be in the form of junior equity; the remaining USD 10 million would be in the  
senior equity layer. GCF participation is intended to be material in providing de-risking  
enhancements to attract capital from private institutional capital providers.  

46. The junior equity is designed with two features: first loss protection (to manage  
downside risk); and a differential preferred return (to improve upside potential). The first loss  
protection absorbs any losses of KawiSafi II if the fund does not achieve positive returns. The  
differential preferred return allows the Senior A investors to receive distributions up to a higher  
preferred return before the junior equity participates in a lower preferred return, and then each  
would share in fund excess returns above the hurdle rate on a pro rata basis. The junior equity  
is intended to benefit only private investors in the Senior A tranche, not development finance  
investors. Concessional capital providers are expected to participate in a separate, Senior B,  
tranche.  

47. KawiSafi II also expects to raise a USD 10 million TAF, which is designed to augment the  
activities of the equity fund. The funding proposal requests USD 2.5 million of GCF grant capital  
for the TAF. This implies that GCF’s USD 2.5 million of grant capital could be leveraged on a 3:1  
ratio in the TAF. The proposed USD 10 million TAF is 5 per cent of the target fund size of USD  
200 million, which is in line with other similarly situated TAFs.  

48. GCF concessionality is expected to leverage the overall KawiSafi II structure to attract  
additional capital at the fund level on a 4:1 ratio; however, the investments made from the fund  
will support company balance sheets to raise debt and support follow-on equity rounds. If  
successful, this would multiply the capital mobilization impact of GCF’s investment along with  
commensurate social and climate impacts.  

### Investment Strategy  
49. KawiSafi II will use its capital to make equity, quasi-equity and convertible debt  
investments in companies that can achieve the desired environmental and social impact. The  
fund’s 10-year horizon is designed to provide KawiSafi II’s future portfolio companies several  
years to scale their businesses and impact. The KawiSafi II offer of early-stage equity capital and  
other similar financial arrangements can be impactful given the lack of equity finance in many  
African markets for low-carbon businesses. Lack of access to early-stage investment capital is a  
well-understood barrier to scaling up enterprises.  

50. KawiSafi II is a for-profit fund. For-profit funds have much greater impact potential in  
growing markets than grant-based facilities or other less commercial models. The need to

generate a return (albeit a concessionally supported return) permeates through all aspects of capital provision from company selection to follow-on investment decisions, portfolio management and so on. This brings efficiency and effectiveness to investment decisions and mitigates against unproductive time and effort. Additionally, only by demonstrating a return model can GCF money materially affect the ability to mobilize additional funding into key geographies and sectors.  

51. However, a for-profit fund only succeeds if the interests of the manager and investors are aligned. Fund managers that are perceived as profiting off management fees in lieu of a realistic carried interest will typically struggle to raise capital from truly commercial investors. In the case of KawiSafi II, a large percentage of raised capital will go to support management fees over the life of the fund. In part this is due to the long tenor of the fund and arguably the costliness of operating in the markets and sectors targeted by KawiSafi II. The iTAP considers that a reasonable argument can be made for the fee structure sought by Acumen. However, it considers that a better model for programmatic funds is for the GCF to provide flexibility on fees and allow fees to ultimately be determined by follow-on private sector investors.  

52. Broadly, iTAP views positively the cap of 25 per cent of GCF funding in KawiSafi II and the links between total GCF commitment and the actual amounts of private sector funding raised. The funding proposal envisions a subscription agreement that ties GCF funding to a calculation based on co-financing subscribed at closing. The 24-month fundraising period is also regarded as reasonable and the GCF veto rights on extensions as preferrable. These conditions add to the overall efficiency of the approach. The iTAP generally encourages the GCF Secretariat to avoid fund models where GCF funds are contributed in their entirety at closing without any linkage to actual third-party funds raised. As noted above, it is difficult for the GCF Secretariat to accurately assess the likelihood of follow-on private sector investment. It is best practice to place a reasonable onus on the fund sponsor to deliver on what it promises through limits on the percentage of GCF participation, drawdown approaches, short capital raise windows with GCF veto rights and so on.  

53. The iTAP also views favourably the fact that both the AE and the fund manager are contributing to the fund. This is standard in private sector funds and, if not present, is often a red flag for follow-on investors.  

54. Further, iTAP views favourably the fact that GCF funding is structured in such a way that it would only be catalysing investment by private capital and does not involve other public or quasi-public entities such as development finance institutions that may eventually participate in KawiSafi II. Private sector downside protection and return enhancement is reasonable, given the risk profile offered by the underlying KawiSafi II investments (exacerbated by the perception of higher risk in emerging markets, particularly Africa).  

55. The iTAP notes positively that KawiSafi II emphasizes its focus on collaboration in its investment sectors, seeking to serve as lead investor and attracting co-investors to contribute further funding. This is an important feature for mitigating against market distortions and using GCF concessionality to enhance the entire market ecosystem without diluting the return-seeking imperative. Oversight by the GCF Secretariat should seek to supervise how the KawiSafi II team is acting as market makers and not just passive market takers. The iTAP also appreciates that Acumen has demonstrated a track record in leading deals in its initial fund (KawiSafi I). According to the funding proposal, KawiSafi’s investments helped to attract nearly 14 times the initial amount in further equity and debt into the investee companies.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Part I: Introduction  

The KawiSafi Fund II is an ambitious $200 million venture equity Fund intending to utilise a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into emerging markets. The Fund will focus on 10 countries across the African continent namely: Kenya, DRC, Uganda, Rwanda, Ghana, Nigeria, South Africa, Côte d’Ivoire, Senegal, and Zambia and seek to address the existing barriers of allocation of sufficient climate finance to scale the deployment of low carbon technologies across the African continent.  

The Fund will seek to address gender barriers at three levels discussed further below.  

#### I. Barrier 1: Closing the Financing gap that exists in investing in women led/ founded enterprises;  

#### II. Barrier 2: Increasing participation and representation of women across the three areas of focus: 1) energy transition; 2) energy productivity; and 3) electric mobility; and  

#### III. Barrier 3: Increasing adaptive capacity of women to climate change across the countries of focus.  

> Where Ghana is mentioned in the Funding Proposal, its inclusion is pending the receipt of a compliant NOL and approval of the Board of GCF.  
> Where South Africa is mentioned in the Funding Proposal, its inclusion is pending the receipt of a compliant NOL and approval of the Board of GCF.  
> Where Senegal is mentioned in the Funding Proposal, its inclusion is pending the receipt of a compliant NOL and approval of the Board of GCF.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Barrier 1: Financing Gap in Investing in Women  

There have been many recent advances on women’s empowerment in Africa, including many changes in laws to promote equal rights for women; however, Africa still lags other regions on progress toward gender equality.  

- According to McKinsey, even for the women in formal employment in Africa, there is a significant drop of ~50% of the share of women in entry-level positions to those in top leadership roles.  
- The share of firms with women as top managers in Sub-Saharan Africa has been reported to be as low as 16%.  
- The funding landscape mirrors this reality. It is estimated that only 7% of emerging market venture capital goes to women-owned businesses.  

Female founders are underrepresented in the sectors that attract the most financing; moreover, research indicates that all-female teams remain less likely to receive financing than all-male teams, and they receive smaller amounts if they do (all-male founding teams receive US$25 for each US$1 going to all-female teams).  

### Barrier 2: Increasing participation and representation of women in the renewable energy industry with a focus on 1) energy transition; 2) energy productivity; and 3) electric mobility.  

- The solar value chain is generating thousands of employment opportunities in emerging markets, specifically driven by the off-grid energy segment. GOGLA estimated that the sector supported ~370,000 Full time equivalent (FTE) jobs across East, West and Central Africa, and across South Asia with over 50% of these jobs in sales and distribution. This is expected to grow to ~over 1 million jobs.  
- While data on female representation in the African renewable energy sector is not available, IRENA estimated 32% women’s employment including both grid-connected and off-grid renewable energy globally. This is expected to be even less at senior levels. Studies conducted in Uganda indicate that women in the Off Grid Solar (OGS) industry are represented in sales and distribution and as microentrepreneurs, and less as technicians/maintenance.

Artisans/manufacturers, or owners of energy firms. Women are therefore located more in the lower ranks of the OGS value chain that is less capital intensive.

○ % of off-grid solar firms with majority female ownership were less than 10%, with Rwanda being at 13%.

● Increasing representation of women in the renewable energy supply chain, including as entrepreneurs is critical. “Evidence has shown that women entrepreneurs in the solar value chain have contributed to challenging social norms and stereotypes of women’s roles due to their active participation outside of the domestic sphere and as owners of businesses. Women solar technicians have also challenged the assumption that these roles are the preserve of men. The visibility of women in this male dominated value chain has enabled some women to enter leadership positions at community level and increased incomes have shifted household power dynamics and influence over key household decisions.”11  

Key barriers to female participation in renewable energy globally include:  
○ Gender norms leading to low female participation in STEM fields as well as in renewable energy roles.  
○ Systematic unconscious biases and explicit discrimination in hiring for positions traditionally perceived as being more suitable to men.  
○ Poor company policies, leading to low female retention at energy service companies.  
○ Limited access to financing due to lower awareness, and unequal asset ownership.  
○ Lack of skills and awareness.  
○ Self-perception.  

### Barrier 3: Increasing the adaptive capacity of women to climate change across the countries of focus.  

● Climate change presents significant challenges to socio-economic development, especially for countries in sub-Saharan Africa where the majority of the economies depend on climate-sensitive sectors, including agriculture and forestry. Climate change is also expected to worsen existing poverty and exacerbate inequalities, especially for those disadvantaged by gender, age, and disability. It is widely acknowledged that women are more vulnerable to the devastating effects of climate change due largely to their limited access and control over productive and economic assets and resources that could help them to address the threats posed by climate change.  

● Gender inequalities remain among the deepest and most pervasive of all forms of inequalities. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change identifies gender as one of the socio-economic factors that influence the capacity to adapt to changing environmental and economic conditions. Nonetheless, women are not just helpless victims but powerful agents of change,

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Part II: Gender Assessment  

To better understand these barriers across each country of focus, the KawiSafi team conducted a comprehensive gender assessment to understand the roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women and men across all geographies of operation. This included a combination of desktop research, feedback received through the stakeholder consultation process (in person or through online webinars), and learnings from KawiSafi Fund I that invested in the distributed renewable energy sector across multiple countries but with a focus on East Africa (Kenya and Rwanda).  

The gender assessment was important to highlight the potential disparities in the vulnerabilities of men and women and how this is likely to be impacted/worsened by the effects of climate change. This information, among other criteria, will be used in the design and evaluation of potential interventions and assessment of impacts generated at the beneficiary level.  

The assessment reviewed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), Gender Inequality composite indices as well as other country-specific indicators. These are highlighted below:  

#### Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI)12  

The OECD Development Centre’s Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) is a cross-country measure of discrimination against women in social institutions (formal and informal laws, social norms, and practices) across 180 countries. The SIGI covers four dimensions of discriminatory social institutions, spanning major socio-economic areas that affect women’s lives:  

1. Discrimination in the family  
2. Restricted physical integrity  
3. Restricted access to productive and financial resources  
4. Restricted civil liberties  

The SIGI’s variables quantify discriminatory social institutions such as unequal inheritance rights, child marriage, violence against women, and unequal land and property rights.  

As highlighted below, the countries that KawiSafi Fund II intends to invest in are ranked between 22 and 46 on the SIGI. In these countries, in general, labour force participation rate is lower for women than for men. Discriminatory social norms that confine women to reproductive and care roles are among the leading causes of this difference.  

In addition, biased perceptions of women’s abilities and discriminatory educational norms tend to prevent women from accessing decent work and confine them to specific sectors of the economy. Biases related to boys’ and girls’ abilities shape educational choices – regarding science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields – and thus accentuate gender-based segregation in the labour force. Furthermore, discriminatory social norms and biases related to women’s access to markets, finance, training, and networks also hamper women’s entrepreneurship in these countries.

### Table 1: Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) by country  

Gender Inequality Index (GII)13: GII is a composite metric of gender inequality. A low GII value indicates low inequality between women and men, and vice-versa. GII reflects gender-based disadvantage in three dimensions— reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market. It shows the loss in potential human development due to inequality between female and male achievements in these dimensions. It ranges from 0, where women and men fare equally, to 1, where one gender fares as poorly as possible in all measured dimensions. The rankings of the countries covered by KawiSafi Fund II are highlighted below:

The team will tailor the approach to project roll out based on these clusters as highlighted below.

• Rwanda stands out globally for its efforts in advancing gender equality and women’s empowerment, particularly in labour force and political participation. Nearly two thirds of its parliamentary seats and 52 per cent of cabinet positions are held by women. Significant progress has also been made in many sectors.   
• Rwanda’s achievements include the decline of maternal mortality rates arising from women’s improved access to quality and affordable health care. Globally, Rwanda is leading the HeForShe Campaign with over 200,000 signatories and three commitments bridging the gender digital divide by 2020, triple girls’ enrolment in TVET and eradicate Gender-Based Violence. More recently, the country took the lead on the Generation Equality Action Coalition Technology and Innovation for Gender Equality and committed to closing the digital gender divide by 2026.  
• Rwanda’s impressive results are due to strong political commitment and institutional level accountability for Generation Equality. The government institutions that make up the National Gender Machinery: The Ministry of Gender and Family Promotion (MIGEPROF), the Gender Monitoring Office (GMO), the National Women's Council (NWC) and the Rwanda Women Parliamentary Forum (FFRP) constitute a uniquely strong and effective mechanism for mainstreaming and monitoring of gender equality.  
• Rwanda is a signatory of several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the African Union Protocol on the Rights of Women in Africa. The country has also adopted a number of gender-related policies and strategies, including the National Gender Policy, the National Gender and Family Promotion Strategy, and the National Gender-Based Violence Prevention and Response Strategy.  
• Gender-based violence (GBV) is a significant problem in Rwanda. According to a 2018 survey by the National Institute of Statistics of Rwanda, domestic violence is a major form of GBV in Rwanda. With more than half of women in Rwanda having experienced physical violence from an intimate partner. Forced marriage is another form of GBV in Rwanda. Although child marriage has declined in recent years, it remains a significant problem. According to the United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF), 4% of girls in Rwanda are married before the age of 15, and 22% are married before the age of 18. Forced marriage can have serious consequences for girls' health, education, and well-being, and can perpetuate cycles of poverty and gender inequality. Female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C) is also a form of GBV in Rwanda, although the prevalence is

### Political participation

As highlighted above, Rwanda has done particularly well in increasing female political participation and is reported to have a female majority in Parliament, with 61.4% in the Chamber of Deputies and 26 female members in the Senate (~36% in the Senate, as per 2021 reports).  

Article 10 of the Constitution of Rwanda states: "The State of Rwanda commits itself to upholding the following fundamental principles and ensuring their respect: building a State governed by the rule of law, a pluralistic democratic Government, equality of all Rwandans and between men and women which is affirmed by women occupying at least thirty percent (30%) of positions in decision-making organs."

In 2008, Rwanda elected the world’s first women-dominated legislature. Women also got one-third of all cabinet positions as well as the important posts of Supreme Court Chief Justice and Speaker of Parliament. Women initially held 39 out of 80 seats (48.75%) in 2003. They got 56% seats in 2008; 63% in 2013 and 61% in the 2018 election, even though reserved seats for women were 30% as per the constitution.
