• Rwanda has a population of 13.28M, with a female labour participation of 84% (2019). The country has a maternal mortality ratio of 248 deaths/100,000 live births (2021). The population with at least some secondary education (age 25 and older) was extremely low at 11% for women and 16% for men.  
• Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 77% and 20% of the population in employment respectively.  
• 78% of women are self-employed with over 78% of the female population estimated to be in vulnerable employment which is defined as contributing family workers and own-account workers as a percentage of total employment.  
• While Rwanda ranks high on the Gender Gap Report and reports high levels of labour force participation, men are more likely to have wage employment. In fact, a substantial percentage of women work without pay. Men are more likely than women to work in the formal and the informal sector where earnings are relatively high, and benefits are structured. Women’s concentration in unpaid family work suggests that cultural norms about unpaid care or domestic responsibilities play an important role in labour market decisions. Consequently, even if more wage employment becomes available, women’s access to such jobs may not be equal to men.  

### Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other  

• Rwanda has made noteworthy progress towards increasing women’s financial inclusion and closing gender data gaps. According to a 2016 FinScope survey¹⁶ 86% of women have access to formal or informal financial products and services, as compared to 90% of men. Of the 86% of women who have access to financial products and services, 63% are formally served through commercial banks, 60% with products or services from non-commercial financial institutions such as SACCOs and mobile money while 71% are informally served (mainly driven by the growth of informal savings groups). This falls short of the target of 80% formal financial inclusion for women. By comparison, 74% of men were found to have access to formal financial products and services; 28.5% of men use commercial banks, while 71% are served by formal non-bank institutions. The 2016 FinScope also revealed that a higher proportion of women (24%) than men (17%) relied exclusively on informal mechanisms for banking. There is also a significant gender gap in mobile money account ownership (46% of men versus 33% of women).¹⁷  

• The 2016 FinScope observed only a slight difference in savings rates with 16% of women not saving at all, as compared to 13% of men, and while women and men saved for similar reasons (to cover living expenses during times of hardship), women were more likely to save using informal savings groups. Additionally, women were more likely to borrow from informal groups or

• Rwanda has significantly promoted STEM education across all levels of study. For example, in  
2019, Rwanda introduced the newly developed education curriculum referred to as the "New  
Competence-Based Curriculum" for pre-primary up to upper secondary education. This new  
curriculum underpins building a knowledge-based and technology-led economy through well-  
adjusted STEM and Information, and Communication Technology (ICT) led education. 18 However,  
despite gender parity at primary level in Rwanda, girls are consistently underrepresented in STEM  
subjects at upper secondary and tertiary levels. Any type of underrepresentation inevitably leads  
to missed opportunities and the loss of mathematical talent – at both an individual and national  
level. The causes of such underrepresentation are rooted in social norms , whereby mathematics  
and science are too often believed to be the domain of men and boys. This leads not only to an  
all-too-frequent lack of female role models in STEM – especially in school settings – but also  
negative stereotypes around girls’ mathematical abilities, and lower levels of confidence among  
girls than among boys of similar attainment levels. It can also contribute to unconscious biases  
among teachers and school leaders, meaning that girls do not receive the encouragement they  
need to pursue these subject areas.  

### Barriers facing women headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally  
marginalized groups in Rwanda  

• Female headed households, Youth, elderly people, and other traditionally marginalized groups in  
Rwanda face several barriers in accessing energy and mobility. In Rwanda’s case, the legacy of  
the 1994 genocide resulted in a significant number of female -headed households and orphaned  
children. Limited financial resources and lack of access to credit are significant barriers that  
prevent these groups from purchasing energy-efficient technologies and vehicles. Inadequate  
infrastructure, particularly in rural areas, limits their mobility and access to markets, healthcare,  
and education. In addition, limited access to information and education on available technologies  
and transportation options hinders their ability to make informed decisions.  

• Cultural norms and societal expectations often limit women's access to mobility and energy  
decision-making. There is a need to address these barriers through targeted policies and  
programs that increase access to affordable energy and transportation options, promote financi al  
inclusion, and provide education and information to marginalized groups.

the government's efforts to promote gender equity in the energy sector. In 2019, the Ministry of  
Gender and Family Promotion, in collaboration with the Energy Development Corporation Limited  
(EDCL), launched the Women in Energy Program. The program seeks to promote the participation  
of women in the renewable energy sector through training, mentorship, and access to finance.  
Another effort to promote gender equity in the renewable energy sector is the establishment of  
the Rwanda Energy Group (REG) Women's Network. The network was formed in 2016 to s upport

### Women in energy productivity in Rwanda  

• The Productive Use of Energy (PUE) sector in Rwanda includes activities such as agriculture, agro-processing, and other income-generating activities that rely on access to energy. It also covers technology that enables green manufacturing and green last mile connectivity. While women play a critical role in these sectors, they often face significant barriers to accessing energy and participating fully in these economic activities.  

• According to a report by the International Finance Corporation, women in Rwanda are less likely to have access to energy services than men, with only 21% of women having access to electricity compared to 34% of men. This is due to a number of factors, including limited financial resources, lack of education and awareness, and cultural norms that limit women's mobility and ability to participate in decision-making. This lack of access to electricity limits women's ability to engage in income-generating activities that require energy, such as agro-processing, and can also impact their health and well-being. Additionally, women often have limited control over energy resources and decision-making processes related to their use, which can further constrain their participation in the productive use of energy sector.  

• To address these challenges, the Rwandan government has implemented a range of policies and programs aimed at promoting gender equity in the PUE sector. These efforts include the National Gender Policy, which emphasizes the importance of gender equity in all sectors, as well as the National Gender Machinery, which is responsible for coordinating gender mainstreaming efforts across government ministries. # KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Women in mobility in Rwanda  

In Rwanda, gender equity in the mobility and logistics sectors has been a priority for the government. However, women continue to face significant barriers and discrimination in accessing these sectors. According to a report by the International Trade Centre (ITC), 21 women face challenges such as lack of access to finance, limited access to networks and information, cultural biases, and inadequate skills and training. Women occupy only 11% of jobs in the transport and logistics industry in Rwanda. Moreover, women's participation in these sectors is often concentrated in lower-paying and less skilled jobs, such as sales and customer service roles. Women are also underrepresented in leadership and decision-making positions.  

According to a report by the World Bank,22 women in Rwanda are less likely to own and use motorcycles for transportation compared to men. This is largely due to socio-cultural norms and beliefs that prevent women from using certain modes of transportation. Women also face challenges in accessing finance to purchase motorcycles and in obtaining driving licences. In terms of the two-wheeler motorcycle transport industry in Rwanda, there are few women who own and operate motorcycle taxis. According to a study by the African Development Bank, women account for only 1% of motorcycle taxi operators in Rwanda. This is largely due to perceptions that motorcycle taxi driving is dangerous and not suitable for women.  

The root causes of these gender gaps are multifaceted and include cultural norms and biases that limit women's mobility and career opportunities, lack of access to education and training, and limited access to financing and networks.  

### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries  

The high percentage of women in vulnerable employment in Rwanda and the low levels of secondary and tertiary education, coupled with significant reliance on the agricultural sector for most women, means that women in Rwanda are likely to be disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards.  

Although data on gender and energy are still scarce, recent studies in Rwanda have provided some initial reports. The Government of Rwanda, through its Gender Monitoring Office, has compiled available data in the ‘Gender Profile in the Energy Sector’ report (GMO, 2018). The World Bank Group’s Multi-Tier Framework report on energy access in Rwanda provided recent data on energy access for male and female-headed households (Koo et al., 2018). In 2020, the Power Africa East Africa Energy Programme published an assessment on women’s participation in Rwanda’s energy sector. The following is consistently reported:  

#### I. Women spend more time than men acquiring fuels for cooking  

Nationally, this burden for women averages 73 minutes per day compared to 36 minutes for men. The time burden in rural areas is twice that in urban areas, but gender differences are similar. Women also spend twice as long as men on preparation activities to use the collected wood as fuel, such as chopping it into smaller pieces. Firewood collection remains a huge workload for women, limiting them from engaging in other productive activities.

### South Africa23  

• South Africa had a population of 59.3M people 32.7% being rural, as of 2021. Poverty rate in

manganese.

contraction to $302 billion in 2020; as a result, GDP per capita dropped to 2005 levels (US  
International Trade Administration).

providing equal rights for women and men are in place in the country – but some international

performance on educational attainment.

gender, among other characteristics. The Labour Relations Act is the main act that deals with  
sexual harassment in the workplace. South Africa adopted the Code of Good Practice on the  
Prevention and Elimination of Violence and Harassment in the Wor ld of Work in 2020 due to  
rising cases of sexual harassment in the workplace – mostly affecting people under vulnerable  
employment.

were reported in 2020, with many more cases going unreported. Physical and sexual violence  
are common forms of GBV in South Africa. Domestic violence is a major problem, with women

### 15  

often experiencing physical and sexual abuse at the hands of their partners. Rape and sexual assault are also prevalent, with many cases going unreported due to social stigma and fear of reprisals.  

• South Africa has one of the highest rates of violence against women in the world with between ~40-50K reported rapes per annum. Police data showed rapes and sexual offences went up 13 percent between 2017/18 and 2021/2022, while murders of women were up 52 percent in the first three months of 2022 compared to the same period last year.  

Psychological abuse is another form of GBV that is often overlooked. Women may be subjected to verbal abuse, emotional manipulation, and controlling behaviour that can have serious long-term effects on their mental health and well-being. Economic abuse is also a significant problem, with women often being denied access to financial resources and forced to depend on their partners for basic needs such as food, shelter, and healthcare. This can make it difficult for women to leave abusive relationships and can exacerbate their vulnerability to other forms of GBV.  

• The South African government has recognized the seriousness of the problem of GBV and has implemented several policies and interventions to address it. These include:  

1) The National Strategic Plan on Gender-Based Violence and Femicide: In 2020, the South African government launched a 5-year plan to address GBV and femicide in the country. The plan includes a range of interventions, including prevention, response, and support services for survivors of GBV.  

2) The Sexual Offences and Related Matters Amendment Act: This law was enacted in 2007 to strengthen the legal framework for addressing sexual offences and to provide better protection for survivors of sexual violence. The law includes provisions for improved investigation and prosecution of sexual offences, as well as better support services for survivors.  

3) The Domestic Violence Act: This law provides for the issuing of protection orders for survivors of domestic violence and includes provisions for counselling and support services for survivors. There is also a Gender-Based Violence Command Centre which is a toll-free helpline that provides counselling, support, and referrals for survivors of GBV as well as Thuthuzela Care Centers which provide support services for survivors of sexual violence, including medical care, counselling, and legal support. The centers are located in hospitals across the country and provide a one-stop service for survivors.  

Despite these interventions, GBV remains a severe problem in South Africa, and there is a need for continued efforts to prevent and address it. The government has committed to increasing resources for GBV prevention and response, and civil society organizations continue to advocate for more effective policies and interventions to address the problem.

### Political participation  
- In South Africa, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life. The country is often cited as a success story of women’s representation in government with one of the highest numbers of women in parliament in the world at 45%, and a raft of progressive women-friendly laws passed by the legislature.  
- Like other instances of high female political representation, such as Rwanda, women parliamentarians in South Africa are said to rarely mobilise around ‘women’s issues’ and in some cases have been openly critical of the feminist agenda, choosing instead to toe the party line.  

### Labour force participation  
- South Africa has a population of 60M, with a female labour participation of 46.2% (2021). The country has a maternal mortality ratio of 342 deaths/100,000 live births (2021). The female population with at least some secondary education (age 25 and older) was 31%.  
- Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 64% and 34% of the population in employment, respectively.  
- 77% of women are self-employed with over 68% of the female population estimated to be in vulnerable employment which is defined as contributing family workers and own-account workers as a percentage of total employment.  
- The gender pay gap – or the difference in wages between men and women for the same type of work or work of equal value remains an obstacle in achieving gender equality in South Africa. It affects women in the middle and upper wage bands the most.  
- Unequal monthly earnings persist in households with ~38% of homes that are managed by women reporting up to 40% less income to those managed by men. Also, 48% of female-headed households support extended family members compared to 23% of male-headed households doing the same. In addition, women face discrimination in the home and have a greater responsibility towards unpaid domestic work with power relations skewed in favour of men.

### Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other  

- Access to finance in South Africa is not equal, with race and gender being important variables in the lack of access. Black African women are disproportionately affected by these biases.  
- The Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment Act promotes “increasing t he extent to which Black women own and manage existing and new enterprises, and increasing their access to economic activities, infrastructure and skills training.” However, the Financial Sector Charter only specifies gender targets for staffing, considered to be low, and assumes positive outcomes in consumption and female participation as consumers overall.  
- Women in business face a number of barriers to accessing finance: namely:  
  1. Lower financial literacy among women as well as lack of awareness of financing options;  
  2. Institutional biases: only one out of South Africa’s four major banks has an intention to increase its share of women - owned enterprises, despite the significant underrepresentation;  
  3. Lack of BEE code targets for financial services outreach;  
  4. Lack of appropriate products: bank services and products, including savings products are often unaffordable, and the emphasis on collateralised and asset based lending disqualifies most women from accessing business loans.  

---

### Barriers facing women headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally marginalized groups in South Africa  

Traditionally marginalized groups in South Africa face a number of barriers in accessing energy and mobility. In South Africa, these include Black South Africans, particularly those living in rural areas or townships, women, people with disabilities, and LGBTQ+ individuals. One major challenge is the lack of affordable and reliable transportation options in many parts of the country, which can make it difficult for people to access job opportunities, education, and other essential services. This is particularly true in rural areas, where public transportation options are often limited or non-existent. Additionally, many people in these groups may not have access to modern forms of energy, such as electricity and clean cooking fuels, which can limit their ability to engage in economic activities and improve their quality of life.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Women in energy in South Africa  

- At last year’s UN Climate Conference, COP27, South Africa launched its US$8.5 billion Just Energy Transition Investment Plan. This was the first stage in implementing the Just Energy Transition Partnership (JETP) announced at COP26 the previous year. The JETP partners, which include France, Germany, the UK, US and EU, have pledged to raise some of the required finance via grants, but much of it will be mobilised from concessional and commercial financing. The investment plan earmarks funding for decommissioning coal power plants, expanding renewables and upgrading grid infrastructure, as well as investing in sustainable manufacturing, such as of new energy vehicles, and in skills and training.  

- Given the importance of implementing the just transition in South Africa, the conversation is dominated by concerns about how changes to industry will affect male employment. Yet women are affected too, with growing evidence that in coal communities Women will be even more vulnerable to the changes wrought by South Africa’s energy transition. With their male partners out of work, they may struggle to survive since they are already pushed to the margins of the economy, often working in the informal sector.  

- If South Africa’s energy transition is managed well, then it could increase opportunities for women. For example, the country’s growing solar PV and/or e-mobility sectors could provide opportunities and help to address the gender imbalance in employment and pay highlighted above. However, this will require awareness and capacity building.  

- According to a study by the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), women in South Africa make up only 26% of the renewable energy workforce, which is below the global average of 32%. This gender gap is attributed to several root causes, including limited access to education and training opportunities for women in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields, as well as cultural and societal norms that limit women's participation in the workforce.  

- The South African government has recognized the importance of promoting gender equity in the renewable energy sector and has implemented several policies to address the gender gap. For example, the Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) 2019 -2030 includes a target of achieving a 50/50 gender split in the renewable energy sector by 2030. The IRP also includes a gender mainstreaming strategy that seeks to address the root causes of gender inequality in the energy sector.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### South Africa  

#### Gender gaps in access to energy for productive use  

In South Africa, there are significant gender gaps in access to energy for productive use, which has implications for economic development and gender equality. According to the 2020 Energy Access Outlook report, women in South Africa are 26% less likely than men to have access to electricity for productive use. Women are also less likely to own or operate productive energy equipment, such as solar panels or cooking stoves.  

According to a report by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), women in South Africa are often excluded from accessing productive resources such as land, credit, and training, which limits their ability to engage in agricultural activities. This exclusion extends to the use of cooling technologies, which are often seen as a "male" domain. As a result, women are less likely to benefit from improved post-harvest practices and technologies that can reduce food loss and improve food quality.  

These gender gaps are often due to social norms and economic barriers that limit women's participation in the formal economy and restrict their access to credit and financing.  

#### Government policies and support for women’s entrepreneurship  

The South African government has recognized the importance of gender equity in energy access and has included gender considerations in its National Energy Efficiency Strategy. The government has also established policies and programs to support women's entrepreneurship, such as the Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality Bill, which aims to promote gender equality in all sectors of the economy.  

#### Women in Mobility and Logistics in South Africa  

Gender equity in the mobility and logistics sectors in South Africa remains a significant challenge, with a substantial gender gap in the industry. Women are underrepresented in this sector, particularly in decision-making positions, due to societal and cultural norms, as well as systemic gender biases.  

According to the World Economic Forum's Gender Gap Report 2021, South Africa ranks 112th out of 156 countries in terms of gender parity in labour force participation, highlighting the need for greater gender equity in the workforce, including the mobility and logistics sectors.  

There is limited data on the gender gap in the mobility and logistics sectors in South Africa. However, a study by the National Business Initiative found that women made up only 14% of employees in the logistics sector in South Africa in 2019. Additionally, women often face gender-based discrimination and harassment in the workplace, including sexual harassment and unequal pay.  

In terms of the two-wheeler motorcycle transport sector, there is a growing number of women riders in South Africa, particularly in the ride-hailing industry. However, women still face challenges such as harassment and safety concerns on the job. Organizations such as the International Transport Forum have highlighted the need for policies that promote women's safety and security in the transport sector, including the two-wheeler motorcycle transport sector.  

#### Vulnerability to climate change and needs of the beneficiaries  

South Africa has a high % of female employment in the services sector, indicating lower vulnerability to climate change. However, South Africa has a gender pay gap of between 23% and 35% (global average of 20%), which negatively impacts women's adaptive capacity in general.

• Kenya has also enacted laws on domestic violence, sexual offences, establishment of affirmative funds for women-led businesses, and increasing representation of women in public and elective office. Despite such developments, only 29% of women are considered empowered, according to UN Women and Kenya National Bureau of Statistics (KNBS) data published in 2020. Progress is still slow, hampered by inadequate implementation of laws, inadequate funding, weak accountability mechanisms and slow transformation of discriminatory and patriarchal gender norms, attitudes, and practices.  

• Adult literacy rates and labour force participation is still lower for women creating significant inequality in incomes.  

• Kenyan law protects against all forms of sexual harassment. Section 6 (2) of the Employment Act, Laws of Kenya states that all employers with more than 20 staff are supposed to create a policy prohibiting sexual harassment at the workplace. Sexual harassment is an offence punishable under Section 23 of the Sexual Offences Act, Laws of Kenya. A perpetrator of sexual harassment is liable to imprisonment for a term not less than 3 years or a fine, or both.  

• Kenya’s Social Institutions and Gender Index ranking was medium, with a 2019 value of 0.35. The country also scored 95 out of 156 in the 2021 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.692. Overall, these scores indicate that women in the country face high discrimination in the family and low access to resources.  

• Gender-based violence (GBV) is pervasive in Kenya, affecting women and girls of all ages, socio-economic backgrounds, and ethnicities. According to a 2014 survey by the Kenyan National Bureau of Statistics, 39% of women in Kenya have experienced physical violence at least once in their lifetime, while 14% have experienced sexual violence. Physical violence is one of the most common forms of GBV in Kenya, with women often experiencing violence at the hands of their partners or family members. Sexual violence is also a significant problem, with many cases going unreported due to stigma and fear of retaliation. Psychological abuse, such as verbal abuse and emotional manipulation, can also have dire consequences for survivors, including depression, anxiety, and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD). Economic abuse is another form of GBV that is often overlooked. Women may be denied access to financial resources and forced to depend on their partners for basic needs such as food, shelter, and healthcare. This can make it difficult for women to leave abusive relationships and can exacerbate their vulnerability to other forms of GBV.  

• Kenya has adopted a raft of critical legislations designed to address sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV). These legislations are welcome and have all the hallmarks of a country that is committed to facilitating gender equality. Kenya also pledged to end Gender-Based Violence by 2026 through full commitment to the implementation of GBV laws and policies as well as earmarking funding for this. Specific responses include:  

1) The National Policy on the Prevention and Response to Gender-Based Violence: This policy was launched in 2014 and provides a framework for preventing and responding to GBV in Kenya. The policy includes provisions for the establishment of GBV response centers, the training of service providers, and the development of GBV prevention programs;  

2) The Sexual Offences Act: This law was enacted in 2006 to strengthen the legal framework for addressing sexual offences in Kenya. The law includes provisions for improved investigation and prosecution of sexual offences, as well as better support services for survivors;  

3) The Protection Against Domestic Violence Act: This law

### **Civil Society Organizations in Kenya**  
• Civil society organizations in Kenya have also played a vital role in addressing GBV. These organizations work to provide support and services for survivors, as well as to advocate for policies and interventions that address the root causes of GBV. Some notable organizations include:

### **Political Participation**  
• In Kenya, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life. Despite this, women remain poorly represented at the national level despite continuous improvement. The general elections held in August 2022 saw the greatest number of female candidates across most positions with seven governors, three senators and 30 single constituency members elected were women.  
• However, Kenya still has the least representation of women among the six East African countries (excluding DRC), which have a uniform electoral quota. The National Gender and Equality Commission has recorded representation of ~ 24.36 per cent, far below the two-thirds gender principle.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Population and Labor Market  
- Kenya has a population of 46.69M, with a female labor participation of 70.2% (2021).  
- The country has a maternal mortality ratio of 342 deaths/100,000 live births (2021).  
- The female population with at least some secondary education (age 25 and older) was 31%.  

### Employment Structure  
- Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 64% and 34% of the population in employment, respectively.  
- 77% of women are self-employed with over 68% of the female population estimated to be in vulnerable employment, which is defined as contributing family workers and own-account workers as a percentage of total employment.  

### Impact of COVID-19  
- It is reported that COVID-19 significantly increased inequalities between men and women in education attainment, health outcomes, representation in parliament, and participation in the labor market.  

### Wage Disparities  
- Even within employment, significant disparities in wages exist between women and men.  
- Over the last three decades, efforts have occurred to close the gender wage gap in Kenya.  
- Legislation, in the form of the Employment Act, has not been fruitful in achieving pay parity for women.  
- In 2019, it was estimated that women earn 32% less than their male counterparts, compared to 23% globally.  

### Informal Economy  
- The informal economy in Kenya is a significant employer for women. However, the sector lacks standard employment contracts, social protection, and adequate pay, meaning that women are unable to obtain a decent livelihood, let alone have the same level of income as men.  

### Decision Making and Leadership  
- Gender inequality is most evident at decision-making levels.  
- In Kenya, women are underrepresented on company boards and senior management positions, as well as in policy making and governance.  
- Under-representation and the absence of female role models is even more acute in energy-related fields.  

### Leadership Challenges  
- The few women who do manage to secure top positions are often less well-connected with their peers than with their male colleagues.  
- Notwithstanding some changes over the last few decades, underlying cultural and social norms still shape the perceptions of men and women’s abilities, needs and prerogatives.  

### Household-Level Data  
- While data at household level is not available, we expect that similar to observations in other sectors (such as sanitation), increased gender equity could potentially have spill-over effects that result in households opting to improve their conditions – including the adoption of cleaner or modern lighting and cooking solutions, which is a significant opportunity.  

### Access to Financial Resources, Knowledge, and Skills  
- Driven by perceptions and misperceptions, only a low percentage of female students choose to study STEM courses.  
- The latest edition of the UNESCO Science Report, entitled *Towards 2030* (UNESCO, 2015), offers the most recent statistics on women’s participation in STEM fields.  
- The share of women graduating in the fields of engineering, physics, mathematics, and computer science is low in many industrialized and emerging countries alike.  

### Gender Imbalances in STEM  
- Gender imbalances among STEM students carry through to gender imbalances in STEM jobs – in the renewable energy sector as well as elsewhere.  
- IRENA’s survey finds that women occupy 28% of STEM positions.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Gender Disparities in STEM in Kenya  
• In Kenya STEM participation shows a clear gender disparity ranging from 30%–35%. Fewer women participate and even fewer complete their studies. In addition, their graduation scores are low compared to those of males. Further, the number of women earning university STEM degrees declines as they move through the educational ladder, this is attributable to institutional and socio-cultural barriers specifically gender stereotyping, sexual harassment, and family responsibilities. Gender biases were not only revealed in inequality in enrolment and completion but also in policies that favored male students in STEM disciplines.  

### Access to Credit in Kenya  
• In terms of access to credit, according to a 2021 FinAccess Household Survey, eight out of every ten Kenyans (83.7%) have access to formal financial services. The gap in financial access between men and women has continually improved, reducing from 8.5% in 2016, to 5.2% in 2019, then to 4.2% in 2021. This is also driven by the need for collateral – in the form of physical assets that would normally be held by men. According to an IFC report only 1% of women in Kenya own collateral.  

### Barriers Facing Women Headed Households, Youth, the Elderly, and Other Traditionally Marginalized Groups in Kenya  
• In Kenya, youth, the elderly, and other traditionally marginalized groups such as persons with disabilities, ethnic and religious minorities, pastoralists, and refugees who have historically faced social, economic, and political exclusion face various barriers in accessing energy and mobility. These groups are particularly affected by the lack of access to reliable and affordable energy and transportation options, which limits their economic opportunities and social mobility.  
• Youth, for example, face challenges in accessing energy for education and entrepreneurship, while the elderly often lack mobility options and may have difficulty accessing health care facilities. Other traditionally marginalized groups, such as women and people with disabilities, may face additional challenges due to social and cultural barriers. For instance, women may have limited access to financial resources and face discrimination in accessing employment opportunities in the transportation sector. In addition, people with disabilities may face physical and infrastructure-related barriers to accessing transportation services. These barriers contribute to a cycle of poverty and inequality for these groups, limiting their ability to fully participate in the economy and society at large.  

### Women in Energy in Kenya  
• According to reports by the AfDB and ENERGIA, 35% of the total staff and 15% of the technical leadership positions in the Ministry of Energy’s headquarters are filled by women. Kenya showcases inspiring examples of women’s involvement in senior leadership roles. In all state corporations in energy, there is some representation of women at the levels of heads of department and managers, and a woman was heading the Kenya Electricity Generating Company

- The renewable energy sector in Kenya is rapidly growing, with the government and private sector investing heavily in this area. However, there are significant gender gaps in the sector, particularly in terms of employment and leadership opportunities. According to a study by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), women are underrepresented in the renewable energy sector in Kenya, with men accounting for 72% of the workforce in the sector. Women are also less likely to hold leadership positions, with only 22% of leadership roles in the sector held by women.

#### One of the root causes of the gender gap in the renewable energy sector in Kenya is the lack of access to education and training opportunities for women. Women are less likely to have access to technical and vocational training, which is often required for employment in the sector. Additionally, societal norms and expectations often discourage women from pursuing careers in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields, which are critical for success in the renewable energy sector.

#### Efforts to improve gender equity in the renewable energy sector in Kenya include initiatives to increase access to education and training for women, promote gender-responsive policies and practices in the workplace, and support women's entrepreneurship in the sector. For example, the UNDP has partnered with the government of Kenya and other stakeholders to launch the Gender and Energy Programme, which aims to promote gender equity in the renewable energy sector through capacity building, policy advocacy, and research. Additionally, organizations like the African Women in Energy and Power (AWEP) and Women in Sustainable Energy and Entrepreneurship (WISEE) are working to support women's participation in the sector through mentorship, networking, and advocacy.

### Women in Energy productivity in Kenya  

- In the productive use of energy sector, women tend to be less involved in decision-making processes, lack access to finance, and face societal and cultural barriers. For instance, a survey conducted by Power Africa found that only 17% of women participate in decision-making in the sector, and only 13% of women have access to finance for their businesses.

#### Cultural beliefs perpetuate gender norms, which limit women's participation in economic activities outside the home. In the agricultural cooling sector, women also face similar challenges. In Kenya, post-harvest losses due to lack of cooling technologies are estimated at 20–30%, with significant impacts on smallholder farmers, who are predominantly women. Women are responsible for post-harvest management and storage, and the lack of access to cooling technologies makes it difficult to preserve crops and maintain quality. This affects their income and ability to access markets. In general, women often have limited access to finance, information, and technologies, which limits their productivity and income. In addition, they are often responsible for unpaid care work, which limits their ability to engage in income-generating activities.

- Gender equity in the mobility and logistics sector in Kenya faces several challenges. Women are underrepresented in the sector, particularly in leadership positions. According to a report by the

## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### 4. Ghana  

- Ghana made considerable progress toward democracy over the past 20 years under a multi-party system, and its independent judiciary has gained the confidence of the populace. Ghana consistently ranks among the top three nations in Africa for press and speech freedom. (World Bank).  
- In the three years preceding the COVID-19 pandemic, Ghana enjoyed average annual economic growth approaching 7%, making it one of the world’s fastest-growing economies. Despite the economic slowdown brought on by the pandemic, Ghana still managed to sustain a growth rate of 0.5 percent in 2020 and it bounced back with a growth of 5.4 percent in 2021 thanks to dynamic agriculture and services sectors.  

- Ghana’s Social Institutions and Gender Index ranking was medium, with a 2019 value of 0.345. The country was ranked 107 out of 156 in the 2021 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.67. Ghana’s ranking in the Gap Index was as a result of high performance in the female labour force participation rate, and enrolment in education at both primary and secondary education. However, gaps remain in income earned between the two sexes as well as political participation.  

- Ghana is a signatory of several international conventions related to gender equality and women's rights. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (also known as the Maputo Protocol), and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), which include a specific goal (Goal 5) on gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls. Ghana has also taken steps to integrate these conventions and goals into its national development policies and strategies.  

- Ghana has taken steps to integrate gender-related international conventions and goals into its national development policies and strategies. The country has developed a Gender Policy Framework which seeks to promote gender equality and women's empowerment by mainstreaming gender into all aspects of development planning and implementation. Additionally, Ghana has developed a National Gender and Children Policy and a National Gender Action Plan to guide the implementation of gender mainstreaming in all sectors of the economy.  

- Gender-based violence (GBV) is prevalent in Ghana with physical violence being one of the most common forms of GBV in Ghana, with women often experiencing violence at the hands of their partners or family members. According to a 2017 survey by the Ghana Statistical Service, 28% of women in Ghana have experienced physical violence at least once in their lifetime, while 14% have experienced sexual violence. The Ghana Demographic and Health Survey (GDHS) 2017–2018 reported that 21% of women aged 15-49 in Ghana have experienced physical violence since age 15, and 9% have experienced sexual violence. Source: Ghana Statistical Service (GSS), Ghana Health Service (GHS), and ICF International. 2019. Ghana Demographic and Health Survey 2017–2018. Rockville, Maryland, USA: GSS, GHS, and ICF. The same GDHS report found that 15% of women aged 15-49 have experienced emotional violence, such as humiliation or belittlement, and 4% have experienced economic violence, such as being prevented from working or having access to money. The Domestic Violence and Victim Support Unit (DOVVSU) of the Ghana Police Service reported that there were 9,146 cases of domestic violence reported in 2019. Source: Ghana News Agency. (2020).

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### • The Ghanaian government has taken steps to address the problem of GBV, including the development of policies and interventions aimed at preventing and responding to GBV. These include:  

1. **The Domestic Violence Act**: This law was enacted in 2007 to provide legal protection for survivors of domestic violence. The law includes provisions for restraining orders, counselling and support services, and improved investigation and prosecution of domestic violence cases.  
2. **The National Policy on Gender**: This policy was launched in 2015 to provide a framework for promoting gender equality and addressing GBV in Ghana. The policy includes provisions for the establishment of gender desks in police stations and the development of gender-sensitive laws and policies.  
3. **The Domestic Violence Secretariat**: This is a government agency that provides support services for survivors of domestic violence. The agency works to provide counselling and legal aid services, as well as to advocate for policies and interventions that address the root causes of GBV.  

### • Ghana does not have criminal penalties for sexual harassment in the workplace. However, Ghana has developed civil penalties in Labour Act, 2003, Secs. 63(3)(b), 64 and 175.

## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Occupations across the world  
In Ghana, that percentage of women graduating from STEM-related academic disciplines is only 20%. This gender gap has profound human rights implications and repercussions for the scientific, technological, and economic advancement of countries. Unequal access to education, gendered choices for academic programs is strongly influenced by the traditional belief that boys are more competent in mathematics than girls, and other social and cultural factors about opportunities for employment and implications for future life.

### According to the 2017 Findex  
54 percent of women in Ghana have formal financial accounts, meaning that there is an 8-percentage point gender difference. Efforts are underway to close this gap using digital financial services (DFS). While the policy environment is supportive of growth in DFS, certain barriers exist that disproportionately impact women – including access to mobile phones; lower levels of digital and financial literacy as well as negative cultural norms that create pessimistic views of digital finance.

### Barriers facing women headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally marginalized groups in Ghana  
According to a report by the World Bank43 women, in particular, are disproportionately affected, as they have lower levels of access to transportation and electricity than men. This is due to a variety of factors, including social norms that restrict women's mobility and employment opportunities, limited access to finance, and inadequate infrastructure. Youth also face challenges, including limited access to education and training opportunities in the energy and transportation sectors. The elderly, especially those living in rural areas, often lack access to reliable energy sources, which can impact their health and quality of life. Addressing these barriers will require a comprehensive approach, including efforts to improve infrastructure, increase access to finance, and address social norms that limit opportunities for marginalized groups.

### Women in energy in Ghana  
Research has established that energy policies that do not explicitly target women often result in inequities in energy access for women and men. Cultural roles and societal norms limit wo men’s access to modern energy; and without equipping institutions to address these limitations, inequities in access to energy will persist. It is important to involve women in all the processes, including the supply chain of renewable energy services as either entrepreneurs or employees.

### Low capacity of staff of the energy sector government agencies  
Is also a major challenge in mainstreaming gender into energy and climate change interventions in the country. Inadequate knowledge and skills to mainstream gender into climate change stems from the fact that policy and programme staff have yet to fully engage with climate change as a relevant issue. The inadequate staff strength compounds the challenge of low capacity, affecting the planning and implementation of gender inclusive energy programmes for the sector.44

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Gender gaps in the energy sector  

Women are underrepresented in the energy sector globally, and this is particularly true for technical and leadership positions. In Ghana, this trend is evident, with women occupying 20% of employment positions in the sector and fewer technical and leadership positions in the renewable energy sector compared to men. The root causes of these gaps include limited access to education and training, cultural and social biases, and discriminatory practices in the workplace.

#### Discrimination and bias in the workplace  

Discrimination and bias in the workplace are significant barriers to gender equity in the renewable energy sector in Ghana. A study by the African Development Bank46 found that women in the energy sector in West Africa face significant gender-based discrimination in recruitment, training, promotion, and retention. The study also highlighted that women are often excluded from decision-making processes and leadership positions, which further perpetuates gender gaps in the sector.

#### Women in Energy productivity in Ghana47  

In Ghana, the productive use of energy, agricultural cooling, and manufacturing sectors face a significant gender gap, with women being underrepresented. According to a report by the Ghanaian Ministry of Energy, only 18% of the energy sector's workforce are women, and women's participation in the manufacturing sector is even lower at around 12%. Women are also concentrated in low-skilled jobs and often face discrimination and bias in hiring and promotions, limiting their career advancement and economic opportunities. Root causes of gender gaps in these sectors include socio-cultural norms and biases, limited access to education and training opportunities, and limited access to finance and productive resources. Women's roles as caregivers and household managers also often limit their ability to participate in the formal workforce.

#### Efforts to improve gender equity  

Efforts to improve gender equity in these sectors include policy and legal frameworks such as Ghana's National Gender Policy, which seeks to promote gender equality in all sectors of the economy. The government of Ghana also launched the Women in Energy, Climate and Leadership Initiative in 2020 to increase women's participation in the energy sector. Additionally, civil society organizations such as the African Women's Development Fund (AWDF) provide training and support for women entrepreneurs in the manufacturing and agricultural sectors, helping to improve their access to finance and markets.

### Women in Mobility and Logistics in Ghana48  

Gender equity in the mobility and logistics sectors in Ghana faces some challenges, with women being disproportionately affected. According to a study by the Ghanaian Ministry of Transport, women are underrepresented in the transport sector, constituting only about 10% of the workforce. The study found that women in Ghana face social and cultural barriers to accessing transportation, such as restrictions on their mobility due to societal expectations of women's roles as caregivers and concerns for their safety while travelling. ### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries  

- While climate change poses serious environmental hazards to all. Research has shown that perceptions and knowledge regarding the causes and impacts of these hazards are differentiated by gender, age, educational status, and place-based variables, with women generally showing a lower level of awareness about climate change than their male counterparts.  
- The results indicate further that irrespective of age, educational attainment and where people live, women were found to be overall more vulnerable, despite experiencing similar levels of exposure as the men, by virtue of their limited access to productive resources, poor conditions of housing, low participation in adaptation decision-making, as well as the heavy domestic responsibilities placed on them.  

### Ghana  

- Ghana currently relies heavily on hydropower, with three plants composing 41 percent of Ghana’s available electric capacity. Ghana’s primary energy supply comes from petroleum, biomass, and hydropower.  
- In terms of cooking, biomass is the main source of energy for cooking and heating in households. Wood and charcoal are the dominant sources of cooking fuel for households in Ghana. Close to one-third of households in Ghana use charcoal as their main fuel for cooking. The next most predominant fuel for cooking is wood, accounting for 33.3 percent of households. Other energy sources used by households for cooking include LPG, electricity, kerosene, crop residues, sawdust, and animal waste. Rural households primarily use wood for cooking (63.0 percent). Gas and charcoal are more prevalent in urban coastal (50.2 percent) and urban savannah (48.2 percent) households. Wood (81.6 percent) and crop residues (3.4 percent) are more prevalent in rural savannah households for cooking than in the other ecological zones. Wood is the most common energy source for cooking in the Upper West, Northern, Volta and Brong Ahafo regions.  
- The depletion of forest wood from unsustainable exploitation means women and girls walk longer distances to fetch firewood for cooking and for charcoal production.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Zambia’s Legal Framework and Policy Integration  

Zambia is a signatory to various gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).  

Zambia has taken steps to integrate these conventions and goals into its national policy frameworks, including the 7th National Development Plan (2017-2021) and the National Gender Policy.  

The 7th National Development Plan has a focus on gender equality and women's empowerment, with specific targets related to increasing women's representation in decision-making positions and reducing gender-based violence.  

The National Gender Policy seeks to mainstream gender in all sectors and promote women's empowerment through increased access to resources and opportunities.  

Additionally, Zambia has established a Ministry of Gender to lead and coordinate gender-related initiatives and mainstream gender in all government policies and programs.  

The country has also implemented various programs aimed at promoting gender equality and women's empowerment, including the Girls' Education and Women's Empowerment and Livelihoods (GEWEL) program, which aims to increase girls' enrolment and retention in schools and improve economic opportunities for women in targeted districts.  

### Gender-Based Violence (GBV) in Zambia  

Gender-based violence (GBV) is characterized by high rates of physical, sexual, and psychological harm.  

According to a study conducted by the Zambia Demographic and Health Survey (ZDHS) in 2018:  
- 46% of women aged 15-49 in Zambia have experienced physical violence.  
- 22% have experienced sexual violence.  
- 42% of women have experienced emotional violence, such as being insulted or belittled.  
- 13% have experienced economic violence, such as having their income taken away or being prevented from working.  

The Zambian government has implemented several policies and programs aimed at addressing GBV, including:  
- The Anti-Gender-Based Violence Act of 2011, which criminalizes various forms of violence against women and girls.  
- The National Strategy on Gender-Based Violence (2019-2023), which aims to prevent and respond to GBV through various measures, including awareness-raising, survivor support services, and law enforcement.  

Civil society organizations have also been active in addressing GBV in Zambia. For example, the Zambia Police Victim Support Unit (VSU) works closely with non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to provide services to survivors of GBV, such as counselling and legal support.  

### Legal Framework and Workplace Sexual Harassment  

The legal framework in Zambia provides legal protection from sexual harassment.  

It is reported that Zambia has the highest rate of sexual harassment in the workplace among Southern African Development Community countries (SADC, 2014).

### Political participation  
- Women have the same rights as men to vote and run for election in Zambia. Yet Zambia ranks 126th in terms of women’s political participation, only 16.8% (28 out of 167) of its parliamentarians are women. The government cites the lack of quotas as a contributor to the low level of women participating in decision-making and leadership positions.  

### Labour force participation  
- Zambia is a lower middle-income country with one of the highest rates of income inequality in the world. 58% Zambians earn less than the international poverty line of $1.90 per day (compared to 41% across Sub-Saharan Africa) and three quarters of the poor live in rural areas.  
- As one of Sub-Saharan Africa’s most urbanised economies, it is dominated by the services and manufacturing sector, notably copper mining and processing, and construction. 71.26% of adult women are employed (23rd out of the 118 low and middle-income countries reporting data in 2019), and women make up 48.3% of the overall labour force.  
- Zambian women are most likely to work in low-income, informal occupations. 54% of employed women work in agriculture, compared to 44% of all men. 42% of employed women and men work in the services sector. In addition to agriculture, many women, especially those in urban areas engage in micro and small businesses which are characterised by informality. An employment survey in Zambia in 2014 reported that 84% of female employment is in the informal sector with the majority of women running unregistered businesses. This informality means that they have limited access to information, training and finance that would help them grow their businesses.  
- Zambia’s statutes grant women full legal rights before the law, however parallel customary and religious legal regimes do not always recognize the rights of women to own or inherit land and other assets.

### Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other  

There has been considerable progress in reducing the financial access gender gap in Zambia. The Bank of Zambia began to consistently measure financial inclusion with a special focus on women over a decade ago. Results of these surveys indicate an increase in financial inclusion of women, partially explained by an increase in the use of mobile money. It is estimated that 58.5 percent of women are now financially included, compared to 33.3 percent in 2015. The gender gap in access to formal financial services has come down to 5.8 percent in 2020 from 9.9 percent in 2015, demonstrating commendable progress.  

However, this progress needs to be maintained to fully close the gap.  

---

### Barriers facing women headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally marginalized groups in Zambia  

The lack of infrastructure and affordable transportation options in rural areas, where many of the marginalized groups reside, make it difficult to access education, health care, and economic opportunities. High unemployment rates among youth also limit their ability to purchase and maintain their own transportation.  

Moreover, the elderly often lack access to proper transportation and mobility aids, limiting their mobility and independence. Women, particularly those in rural areas, also face gender-based barriers, including limited access to financing and restrictive gender roles. According to the World Bank⁴⁹, these barriers to mobility and energy access disproportionately affect women, youth, and low-income households in Zambia.  

---

### Women in energy in Zambia⁵⁰  

The participation of women in the energy sector value chain in Zambia is very limited, especially as entrepreneurs or business leaders in renewable energy. In terms of energy access, the majority rural population is especially energy poor – over 90% of rural Zambians are without access to energy. Poor women are especially constrained considering their role in the provision of cooking energy and productive uses of energy for rural enterprises. Yet evidence shows that women’s experiences and knowledge are critical in addressing climate change. They have extensive knowledge on weather patterns, ecosystems, and management of natural resources.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Women in Energy productivity in Zambia  

According to a report by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) 51 and the Global Environment Facility (GEF), women in Zambia are often excluded from decision-making processes, lack access to finance and credit facilities, and face limited access to productive resources such as land and technology. In the energy sector, women are underrepresented in both technical and managerial roles.  

In the agricultural sector, women play a crucial role in crop production and livestock management, yet they often have limited access to productive resources such as land, credit, and inputs. According to a report by the International Food Policy Research Institute (IFPRI), women in Zambia face social and cultural barriers that limit their access to and control over productive resources, including land, which is often held by men. Women also lack access to extension services, technology, and credit facilities.  

In the manufacturing sector, women are also underrepresented, particularly in technical and managerial roles. According to a report by the International Finance Corporation (IFC), women in Zambia face several challenges, including limited access to education and training, cultural and social biases, and discriminatory practices that limit their ability to participate and advance in the manufacturing sector.  

### Women in Mobility and Logistics in Zambia 52  

The transportation sector in Zambia, particularly the informal sector, has traditionally been male dominated, with women accounting for only a small fraction of operators and workers. The lack of access to financing and capital, as well as cultural and social norms, are among the root causes of gender gaps in the sector. Women are often excluded from training programs and do not have access to information and resources necessary to enter the sector. Additionally, women often face discrimination and harassment on the job.  

Data from the Zambia Transport Sector Improvement Project indicates that only 10% of registered commercial vehicle owners are women, and women account for only 4% of registered taxi drivers. Furthermore, a study by the International Labour Organization (ILO) found that women in the transport sector in Zambia tend

### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiarie s.  

• Agriculture constitutes 9% of the GDP of Zambia and is the primary source of livelihood of the  
rural population. Reduced productivity of arable and pastoral farming, caused by drought,  
waterlogging of fields, heat-stress of livestock, and increased disease is likely to reduce the  
revenues that can be made from agriculture. It is estimated that the total average annual loss in  
the agricultural sector will increase from 29 to 180 million USD by the end of the century. This  
will disproportionately impact women's livelihood as they are significantly represented in  
agriculture.   
• Most of the agriculture in Zambia is small-scale and subsistence in nature, with women travelling  
to markets to sell their goods. Direct impacts of climate change are likely to impact th ese  
operations particularly badly as they lack the ability to adapt to climate change (such as installing  
groundwater irrigation of crops during droughts) or to take on alternative livelihoods if crops fail.

biodiversity, and the world’s second-largest rainforest. However, most people in DRC have not  
benefited from this wealth. A long history of conflict, political upheaval and instability, and  
authoritarian rule have led to a grave, ongoing humanitarian crisis. In addition, there  has been  
forced displacement of populations. These features have not changed significantly since the end  
of the Congo Wars in 2003.

- The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is a signatory of several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the African Union Protocol on the Rights of Women in Africa.
- The country has taken steps to integrate these conventions and goals into its national policies, including the National Gender Policy (2008) and the National Strategy for the Advancement of Women (2010–2015). The National Gender Policy aims to promote gender equality and women's empowerment across all sectors of society, while the National Strategy for the Advancement of Women seeks to increase women's participation in decision-making processes and eliminate all forms of violence against women. Despite these efforts, challenges remain in implementing these policies and ensuring gender equity in the country. These challenges include limited resources and capacity, as well as social and cultural norms that perpetuate gender inequality.
- Gender-based violence (GBV) is a pervasive issue in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), according to the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA). Two-thirds of Congolese women have experienced some form of GBV in their lifetime. The DRC government has made some efforts to address GBV through policy and legislative measures. The country's Family Code, adopted in 1987 and revised in 2016, provides legal protection against GBV and sexual violence. The 2006 Law on Sexual Violence criminalizes sexual violence, including rape, and provides for harsh penalties for perpetrators. In addition, the National Strategy for Combating GBV (2014–2017) aimed to strengthen the government's response to GBV, including through prevention, protection, and provision of services for survivors. However, the situation remains dire, experts have noted.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Gender-based violence (GBV)  
- 48% of women in the Democratic Republic of the Congo have to walk for more than an hour to reach a health facility.  
- Insecurity in eastern DRC has led to conflict-related sexual violence. In 2020, the UN Organization Stabilization Mission in the Democratic Republic of the Congo documented over 1,000 cases of conflict-related sexual violence.  
- Some organizations, including a coalition of civil society organizations, have noted a “widespread phenomenon” of domestic violence across the country. One report shared that sexual violence against women and girls in the DRC is considered endemic. According to a report by Women's International League for Peace and Freedom, there is no law explicitly prohibiting domestic violence.  

### Political participation  
- Political participation of women is limited - following Ordinance No. 17/025 establishing the powers of ministries, there have been 5 women, or 10.8% of the 46 members of the Government; in the National Assembly, there are only 49 women out of 500 elected deputies; and the Senate has six women out of 108 senators.  
- The 2015 revision of the electoral law dropped the 30% quota for women, which could be viewed as a challenge to increasing women’s political participation.  
- The provincial and local level has even lower representation of women in political, economic, and administrative affairs.  

### Economic and social inequality  
- The fragile political situation in the DRC as a result of longstanding conflict has contributed to the majority of the population being faced with limited access to education, health, and employment opportunities.  
- Economic and social inequality among the population continues to widen with ~64% living under the poverty line.  
- Strong male-dominant gender norms determine gender relations in the country. While discriminatory legislation has been revised (specifically the Family Code revised in 2016 – which stipulated that men are the head of the household and women were obligated to obey them), traditional gender norms and harmful practices such as early and forced marriage still exist with a >30% rate of girls in the country married between the age of 15 and 18, and high adolescent birth rates.  

### Labour force participation  
- The DRC has a population of 92.3M, with a female labour participation of 61% (2021).  
- The country has a maternal mortality ratio of 473 deaths/100,000 live births (2021).  
- The female population with at least some secondary education (age 25 and older) was 40%, indicating a relatively high literacy rate that should support access to employment opportunities.  

### Employment sectors  
- Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 77% and 19% of the population in employment, respectively.  
- Women have less access to skilled jobs compared to men, with the highest participation of women in the agricultural sector (~77% female employment), micro businesses, and the informal sector.  
- In 2014, it was reported that women’s unemployment rate was 6.7% compared to 9.4% for men, and only 45.3% of the population aged between 15 and 24 years was economically active. Among 15–24-year-olds, the female-male

### Access to Financial Resources, Knowledge, and Skills  

#### Gender Norms Restrict Women’s Access to Resources and Assets  

- Assets obtained within marriage are registered under the husband’s name, and regarded as assets of the husband, his parents, and brothers.  
- Legally, marital property is administered by the husband, and women and men do not have equal ownership rights to immovable property.  
- Women can inherit according to the law, but they cannot own a house due to prevailing social norms.  
- Women are not legally recognized to be heads of households, and there is no prohibition on discrimination based on marital status in access to credit.  

#### Land Concessions  

- Legally, there should not be any discrimination based on gender for the right to land concessions, but attitudes and customs tend to discriminate against women.  
- Despite the nationalisation of all land and introduction of formal legislation governing land use rights, a significant percentage of land remains under customary law.  
- Women, especially those who are married, thus have limited access to land.  
- Even though women make up the majority of the agricultural labor force, ownership remains held by men in most instances.

• Further, women are not able to make productive investments for productive assets, such as improved inputs, technologies, seeds, and fertilisers, as their incomes are controlled by men which keeps their productivity low. Limited access to resources and assets increases women’s energy poverty – and women as both consumers and suppliers of energy remain invisible in the sector. Energy access rates of women tend to be lower than that of men due to disproportionate access and consumption.  

• More than 95% of the DRC population is unbanked – compared to the continental average of 76%. Such an underdeveloped banking sector makes domestic credit scarce and expensive - particularly for micro, small, and medium enterprises – in 2013, only 7% of Congolese firms used banks to finance investment. Further, The Family Code, which was revised in 2017, forbade women from opening bank accounts without prior consent from men. And while this has been repealed, norms and attitudes will inevitably take long to change, and we expect that the barriers experienced by women continue to exist.  

## Women in energy in the Democratic Republic of Congo  

• The participation of women and youth across the energy value chain is extremely limited – sector policies do not address the challenges they face, and barriers such as lack of access to credit and training limit the possibilities for women and youth to develop and use clean energy or low carbon technologies and clean cooking solutions.  

• The proposed KawiSafi Fund II aims to improve the DRC’s social and economic development by reducing energy poverty in rural areas. Energy encompasses all productive, subsistence and leisure activities - but women and men do not have equal access to energy due to gender-defined roles in energy production, distribution and use in households, communities, and the private sector. Women and youth can play a crucial role in energy production, distribution and use if they are given the requisite access to finance, skills, and capacity and an enabling environment to participate in the sector. Promoting the use of clean cooking solutions would decrease the time burden faced by women and girls; and positively impact public health, income generating opportunities, education, environmental management, and gender equality.  

## Women in Energy Productivity in the DRC  

• Researchers found that female farmers face far more barriers than their male counterparts in the DRC, which leads to greater food insecurity and poverty. Women farmers have limited income and, as a result, have less access to agricultural inputs and productive use technologies. Some of the barriers to productive use technologies come from gender norms relegating female farming to the informal sector and some come from issues across genders including weak transportation infrastructure, limited access to information systems about the market, and difficult climate trends.  

• Men and women farmers in the DRC experience the negative impacts of climate change. Some researchers noted that women appear to be less adaptive to climate change than men due to resource constraints and less access to information. In one study, researchers found that women farmers in DRC were more likely to rely on indigenous knowledge of the local environment.

### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries  

- While there is limited data on % of women in professional roles, 91.5% of women are reported to be self-employed while over 90% are in vulnerable employment indicating a lack of female penetration in most professional formal settings.

### DRC  

- Despite efforts towards electrification in the last decade (from 11% in 2009 to 19% in 2017), the DRC is the second country in Africa for the most people without grid electricity access (68 million people), after Nigeria. This is most striking in rural areas, where access to energy is estimated at 1%.  
- Energy poverty is attributed to factors such as cost tariffs, poor utility performance and lack of financial viability, compounded by governance issues. (GOGLA Country Brief)  
- The investment needs of the power sector exceed the government’s fiscal capacity, and private capital is needed to meet the funding gap, especially after the COVID-19 pandemic. Furthermore, the high degree of the country’s fragility – weak governance, conflict and security issues and poor transport infrastructure – continues to lead to underinvestment in the national grid and urgency of alternative decentralized solutions. Meanwhile, only 3% of the population has access to clean cooking solutions, meaning 115 million people lack access, representing another critical area for investment.  

## 7. Uganda  

- Uganda is a low-income country in East Africa with a total population of 45.7 million people, GDP per capita of $817 and poverty rate of 42%. Uganda is included in KawiSafi’s Country Archetype 1: Replicate proven energy access model, composed of frontier markets with strong development potential to leapfrog from fossil-fuel grids to clean economy-wide development.  
- Archetype 1 countries fall behind the rest of Sub-Saharan Africa on key poverty and energy-related indicators.  
- Energy access is only 27% in Uganda and is a key driver of economic growth and poverty reduction, without which countries will struggle to meet their development goals. There is a strong case to invest in basic clean energy solutions – such as off-grid solar and clean cooking stoves – that have proven success in neighbouring countries and could be replicated in the same fashion in these nascent markets.  
- Uganda was ranked 99 out of 120 in the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of 0.451 and 61 out of 146 in the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.724.  
- Uganda is a signatory to various gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action.  
- The Ugandan government has made efforts to integrate these conventions into national policy through the development of various policies and strategies, including the National Policy on Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment, the National Action Plan on Women, Peace and Security, and the Gender in Education Policy. These policies aim to address gender inequalities and discrimination in various sectors, including

#### There is currently no stand-alone legislation that addresses sexual harassment

### Violence against women  
• Violence against women has recently taken new, more sophisticated forms. An increasing number of women are, for instance, reporting cyber-bullying and abuse through social media and smartphones.  

### Political participation  
• In Uganda, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees women’s participation in political life by reserving one seat for women in each of the country’s 39 electoral districts.  
• Women parliamentarians are elected through separate women’s elections in addition to the constituency MPs who are elected through a first past the post system. 34.9% (160 out of 459) members of Uganda’s unicameral legislature are women, 93% of them were elected through reserved seats.  

### Labour force participation  
• According to the World Bank, 49.3% of the labour force is female. Uganda is one of the poorest countries in the world, with a GDP of under $30bn dollars, and one in five of its citizens still living in extreme poverty.  
• 68% of adult women are employed (30th out of the 118 low and middle-income countries reporting data in 2019), and women make up 49.3% of the overall labour force. They are most likely to work in low-income, informal occupations. Women account for 75% of agricultural workers and nearly 42% of women in the workforce are unpaid family workers.
