### 45  

a government report revealed a severe drop between 2009 and 2014 in the percentage of girls passing biology and mathematics examinations specifically. This gender gap reduces the number of women who pursue STEM careers such as engineering. For instance, currently less than 30% of science researchers in Uganda are women. While the Ugandan Government has put measures in place in response to this gap, such as the 2018 Gender in Education Policy, the performance of girls and women in national STEM examinations continues to be poorer than that of boys and men.  

- This has been attributed to gender bias amongst teachers which is a significant barrier to achieving gender equality as teachers play a pivotal role in setting classroom norms and attitudes. There is now a push in Uganda to provide continuous professional development for teachers to improve their teaching quality, gender responsiveness, and inclusiveness, especially related to girls and STEM subjects.  

### 63  

- On access to financial services, a study by FSD Uganda highlights that there is hardly a gender gap between men and women accessing financial services in Uganda. However, women are shown to be more likely to be served by informal channels. While these provide savings and credit services; they are also likely to be more expensive than formal channels and their informal structures create opportunities for exploitation. Women excluded from formal financial services also tend to have less capacity to meet KYC requirements of formal channels than those who currently access financial services. They are also less likely to own a mobile phone or have access to the internet (only 12% of excluded women have a phone, and only 1% have internet access).

Women in energy in Uganda  

- Adequate representation of females and males in the workplace is vital for achieving institutional gender equality norms. The Ministry of Energy in Uganda is male dominated, with only approximately 30% female staff, and working mostly in the lower echelons of the ministry’s structures and administrative roles (EOC, 2018). However, women are making inroads into the management of the sector:  

  - 2 of the 3 ministers are women (a minister and a minister of State).  
  - 2 of the 3 directors are women.  
  - 3 of the 10 department heads are women.  
  - 4 of the 11 CEOs of affiliated agencies are women.  

- Gender-disaggregated data from private companies in the energy sector is not readily available. Women’s limited representation in the energy sector is partly attributed to the limited number of women engaging in STEM courses as highlighted above.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  
### Page 187  

• Nigeria has the largest market in Africa with a population of over 206 million (2020). It is one of Africa’s key oil producers, producing high-value, low-sulphur crude oil. The economy is heavily dependent on oil and has a GDP of USD 432.3B (2020). The country had an electrification rate of 55% (2020) and GHG emissions of 354.3MT in 2018. (World Bank). Nigeria's economy improved after the pandemic induced recession in 2020, but macroeconomic stability declined. Inflation is soaring and plunging millions of Nigerians into poverty because of global commodities shocks, the falling value of the currency, trade restrictions, and monetization of the deficit. Nigeria has also been unable to benefit from the rising oil prices since 2021 since oil output has reached historic lows and gasoline subsidies continue to eat up a growing portion of the country's gross oil revenues. 39% of Nigerians (83 million) in 2018 lived in poverty, and another 25% (53 million) were considered vulnerable. Between 2019 and 2024, there will be an additional 7.7 million Nigerians living in extreme poverty as population growth continues to exceed efforts to reduce poverty.

• Nigeria was ranked 101 out of 120 in the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of .460 and 123 out of 146 in the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.639.

• Nigeria is a signatory to several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights, the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. These conventions have been integrated into national policies through the National Gender Policy of 2006 and the Violence Against Persons (Prohibition) Act of 2015, among others. The National Gender Policy seeks to promote gender equality and women's empowerment by mainstreaming gender considerations into all areas of national development, while the Violence Against Persons (Prohibition) Act criminalizes all forms of violence against persons, including physical, sexual, and psychological violence, and provides legal and institutional frameworks for combating such violence.

• Additionally, the Nigerian government has taken steps to integrate gender into its national development plans. For instance, the country's Vision 20:2020 plan includes gender-sensitive targets and indicators for poverty reduction, education, health, and political participation, while the National Economic Empowerment and Development Strategy (NEEDS) seeks to promote gender equality in economic and political spheres. The government has also established the National Gender Policy Implementation Committee and the National Gender-Based Violence Management Committee to oversee the implementation of gender-related policies and programs. However, despite these efforts, gender inequalities persist in Nigeria, particularly in terms of women's representation in leadership positions and access to education, health, and economic opportunities.

• At present, there is no law prohibiting sexual harassment, but state authorities may prosecute violent harassment under assault statutes (US Department of State, 2017). In Nigeria, sexual harassment is widespread where the practice of demanding sexual favours in exchange for

#### Gender-based violence (GBV)  
- GBV is a pervasive problem in Nigeria and it is often underreported, with high impunity for perpetrators.
- A significant portion of women in Nigeria have experienced gender-based violence.
- Almost one in three (31%) women aged 15–49 have experienced physical violence in Nigeria.
- Half of girl children who experienced sexual violence, experienced it at home.
- Root causes of GBV in Nigeria include gender inequality, harmful cultural practices, poverty, and weak legal frameworks.
- There have been efforts by the Nigerian government and civil society organizations to address GBV, including:
  - Adoption of the Violence Against Persons Prohibition (VAPP) Act in 2015
  - Establishment of sexual assault referral centers
  - Campaigns to raise awareness about the issue
- However, implementation of these measures has been inadequate, and more needs to be done to prevent and respond to GBV in Nigeria.

#### Political participation  
- In Nigeria, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life.
- The number of women in parliament is small compared to men.

### Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other  
- According to a study by the Gender Centre of Excellence, 70 formal financial inclusion remains low in Nigeria: ~3 percent of adult Nigerians have borrowed from formal sources, and 45% have a formal account at either a bank or microfinance institution (EFInA Access to Finance Survey 2020). The most common reasons for not having a formal account are negative perceptions of formal institutions, poor access to banks, or not having enough income for savings. This suggests that the broader ecosystem of formal financial services is not conducive toward inclusion — either through low demand or poor provision of the right financial products. However, despite low access levels across the board, it is estimated that 98% of Nigerian women are left out of formal credit markets, a statistic exacerbated by low education, limited decision-making power, and poor access, as with rural populations.

### Behaviours  
For older women especially, more effort is required to instil trust in financial institutions, and require approaches that include face-to-face interactions, and services integrated within existing social networks.  

### Women in energy in Nigeria  
- The Nigerian distributed renewable energy (DRE) sector employed ~50,000 Nigerians in 2021, showing a fast recovery post-pandemic. The DRE sector is expected to continue its momentum; job growth is projected to be almost 25% per annum between 2021 and 2023, thereby recording nearly 75,000 jobs in 2023.  
- In addition, the number of direct DRE jobs is fast approaching that of the oil and gas sector, which is currently estimated at 65,000.  
- The Solar home system (SHS) segment is the most dominant sector in terms of job creation, accounting for 95% of total direct employment in 2021.  

### Various initiatives are emerging to support increased inclusion of women in the DRE sector  
- In Nigeria, Solar Sister recruits, trains and supports women to deliver clean energy directly to homes in rural communities.  
- Over 86% of Solar Sister’s staff consists of women energy managers, trainers, business coaches and technicians.  
- Its business model is focused on building a women-driven last mile distribution network to bring energy access to underserved communities.  
- It recruits, trains, and provides mentorship to African women to build sustainable businesses selling portable solar lamps, mobile phone chargers and clean cookstoves.  

### These efforts are paying off  
- It is reported that the participation rate of women in the DRE sector in Nigeria currently stands at 35%.  
- This is a lower rate of women’s participation than the overall economy in Nigeria, which currently stands at 44.2%. However, this percentage of women is higher than participation in oil and gas and is expected to grow to almost 40% by 2023.  

### Women in Energy Productivity in Nigeria  
- Women smallholder farmers constitute 37% of the Nigerian labor force.  
- They play a critical role in food and agricultural production as they account for much of the food produced for domestic consumption, and help drive food processing, marketing, and preservation.  
- Much like many other KSVII markets, Nigerian women face similar agricultural constraints.  

### USAID wrote in a report  
- If women had equitable access to finance, land, technology, and training as men that they can make meaningful contributions to eradicating poverty and hunger in Nigeria.  

### Power Africa found  
- Targeted marketing was needed to reach female farmers with productive use opportunities.  
- Additional research from Power Africa also found that women disproportionately benefit from cold storage as they are involved in several important sectors for cold storage including fishing.  

### Women in Mobility and Logistics in Nigeria  
- Women have had limited employment opportunities in the Nigerian transportation sector.  
- Researchers have found that men discourage bus and truck owners from engaging their wives in transportation sectors.  
- In addition, nursing mothers are not offered employment in the transportation sector. Most respondents agree that employers see women as inferior and do not offer them employment opportunities.

The low participation of women in the transport sector is a trend that has been gradually reversing around the world, thus, it is pertinent to address this gender bias experienced in Kogi state because its transportation sector has a high labor-absorption capacity.  

### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of beneficiaries  

- Climate change has led to increased rainfall in southeast Nigeria, causing serious flooding.  
  Women in the Amauzari community worked to channel floodwaters by building out drainage to combat gully erosion. They used sand-filling and landscaping to help reconstruct devastated farm roads, as well as reclaimed abandoned, eroded farmlands. Community members transformed impenetrable crust-lands into productive farmland by rebuilding the fertility of degraded soils through tree-planting, cover cropping, contour tillage, and cut-off drainage.  

- More than 90 million Nigerians lack access to electricity. This figure represents 55% of the total population, placing Nigeria in the top three countries with the largest energy deficit in the world (along with Ethiopia and the Democratic Republic of Congo). In addition, close to 95 million people are fully reliant on traditional wood stoves for cooking. Energy infrastructure is unequally distributed throughout the country, following the typical urban-rural divide. While most power plants, transmission lines, and distribution lines are located around urban centres like Lagos, other parts of the country are isolated from the grid. As a result, less than 25% of Nigerians in rural areas have access to the grid. (Power for All)  

- Despite strong grid coverage in urban areas, it is estimated that the majority of the Nigerian population with grid access suffer significant outages. According to the World Bank, Nigeria records the largest number and duration of grid power outages in Africa. This creates large constraints with those who can afford it turning to diesel back-up generators, which are costly and polluting.  

- Women are the mainstream users and often producers of energy and also form the fastest growing cohort of entrepreneurs and business owners in the country. Women are therefore a key resource in the energy service delivery process though still underutilised. They are primarily viewed only as energy consumers even while it is the women that experience energy poverty much more severely than men. There is great opportunity for collaboration with women on both private sector and community energy projects that can contribute to ending energy poverty in the country.

Senegal’s economy grew by more than 6% per year between 2014 and 2018. Real GDP growth stood at 0.87% in 2020, down from 4.4% in 2019, and 6.2% in 2018. (World Bank). The electricity access rate in Senegal is among the highest in Sub-Saharan Africa with a national access rate of 70%. # KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  
### Page 192  

Senegal was ranked 87 out of 120 in the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of 0.370 and 112 out of 146 in the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.668.  

Senegal’s 2001 constitution guarantees equality between women and men in its article 7. Senegal is also a signatory to several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights.  

The country, in 2010, Senegal adopted the National Gender and Sustainable Development Policy, which aims to promote gender equality and the empowerment of women in all sectors. Additionally, in 2019, Senegal passed a law criminalizing rape and other forms of sexual violence, which had previously been difficult to prosecute due to legal loopholes and societal stigma.  

The country also ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (in 1985), and the Optional Protocol on violence against women (in 2000).  

Sexual harassment is covered under Art. 319 bis of the Criminal Code. The Criminal Code defines sexual harassment as the abusive use of authority to “harass a person through orders, threats, coercion, or any other means, in order to obtain sexual “favours”. It is punishable by prison terms of six months to three years and a fine of 50,000 to 500,000 Francs CFA.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Gender Based Violence (GBV)  
- According to Senegal’s 2017 Demographic and Health Survey, 27% of women aged 15 to 49 had experienced physical violence from age 15 onwards – in 55% of cases, at the hands of a husband or partner.  
- The survey showed Gender Based Violence (GBV) to be the most widespread yet least visible human rights violation: 68% of violence survivors aged 15 to 49 never spoke of it nor sought help.  
- Other abuses include forced marriages, and female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C), sexual harassment, rape, and human trafficking.  
- LGBTQ individuals and sex workers also face high levels of violence and discrimination.  
- International government entities have noted the lack of investigation of and accountability as a challenge.  

### Violence against Children  
- Violence against children, in particular sexual violence, continues to be a major challenge in Senegal, with an estimated number of about 15,000 girls aged 15–17 victims of sexual violence.  

### Political Participation  
- In Senegal, women have the same rights to vote and contest elections as men.  
- According to the Law on Equality of Men and Women in Electoral Lists adopted in 2012, candidate lists put forth by parties are required to have gender parity and alternation, to be legally admissible.  
- Consequently, 43% (71 out of 165) members of the legislature are women.  
- Senegal has gained international recognition for its efforts towards political participation of women through the adoption of the Gender Parity Law (2010).  

### Labour Force Participation  
- Senegal is a low income, mixed economy country that is driven by the service sector (tourism, construction) and commodity exports like fish, phosphates, and groundnuts.  
- There is also a large, rainfed subsistence agriculture sector that is prone to frequent droughts.  
- 36% of adult women are employed (ranks 100th out of the 118 low and middle-income countries reporting data in 2019), and women make up 40.4% of the overall labour force.  
- Only 24% of employed women work in agriculture, compared to 33% of all men.  
- Nearly 70% women work in the services sector, compared to 48% men.  
- One in five management positions are held by women.  
- There are no laws in Senegal mandating non-discrimination based on gender in hiring, nor laws penalising or preventing the dismissal of pregnant women.  
- Further, although civil law mandates equal rights to inheritance, parallel customary and religious legal regimes do not always recognize the rights of women to own or inherit land and other assets.

### Access to Financial Resources, Knowledge, and Skills  

- Women and girls in Senegal face barriers in the supply of and in their demand for financial products and services, the wider enabling environment as well as in their socio-cultural context.  
  - On the supply side, the sparse number of financial access points disproportionately impacts women. This is in a context where women and girls’ mobility is constrained due to socio-cultural norms as women are expected to undertake the majority of unpaid care responsibilities in the household, and may face restrictions in leaving the house without a male relative’s permission.  
    - For instance, 40.6% of women aged 15 to 49 find it normal to be beaten by their husbands if they leave the house without asking for permission.  
  - While mobile money could overcome these mobility and access point constraints, barriers remain related to access to a mobile phone and level of awareness of mobile money accounts and knowledge on usage. Further, low literacy rates among women hamper their ability to access mobile money.  

- In terms of the enabling environment, social norms related to inheritance and land and property ownership result in the majority of women in Senegal (85%, aged 15–49) not owning a house or land that could be used as collateral to access credit.  
  - For example, in the agricultural sector, women account for 70% of the labour force, but they hold only 3% of cultivated land.  
  - Consequently, women resort to informal mechanisms for credit access and savings, which lack any form of consumer protection.  

### Women in Energy Sector in Senegal  

- The Government of Senegal has made energy sector development a key component of the Plan Sénégal Emergent, which aims to modernise the country’s economy and achieve a wide number of ambitious socio-economic development objectives, as well as ensuring greater gender equality.  
  - Priorities include lowering the cost of energy by reducing dependence on imported liquid fuels and increasing electricity access—particularly in rural areas.  
  - Senegal has significant potential to develop solar and wind power, together with large offshore natural gas resources that will come onstream in 2023.  
  - The Senegalese authorities aim to achieve universal access by 2025 through a combination of on-and off-grid solutions.  

### Women in Energy Productivity in Senegal  

- Lack of access to efficient electrical tools and appliances is a major barrier for women entrepreneurs seeking to improve their productivity in marginalised off-grid rural regions across sub-Saharan Africa.  
  - Such technologies offer critical advantages to women entrepreneurs in regions hit hard by the impacts of climate change.  
  - As rainfall becomes more erratic, solar irrigation reduces farmer dependence on rain-fed agriculture, enabling them to produce healthier crops with increased yields by using water from local rivers and lakes.  
  - Solar refrigeration allows

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Farming and Food Security  
Women farmers in Senegal are often faced with high levels of on-farm wastage, which jeopardizes local food security.  

### Women in Mobility and Logistics in Senegal  
• Women continually move to urban areas of Senegal, creating new transportation needs for women and girls.  
• GGGI’s report on Senegal’s green energy transition noted the need for stronger public transportation planning so that public transportation is inclusive and gender responsive.  
• Gender-responsive climate programs must take into consideration women’s needs in transport such as their safety; affordability of the fares, schedule for the transport services fitting their needs.  
• Ensuring that women and girls are central to the planning and development of the transport programs in urban development.  

### Vulnerability to Climate Change and Needs of Beneficiaries  
• The primary effects of climate change in Senegal are desertification and land degradation.  
• Desertification is a combination of droughts and erosion that result from insufficient rainfall, destruction of pastures and degradation of ecosystems.  
• Land degradation is characterized by the salinization of agricultural land, water and wind erosion, soil infertility and reduction of agricultural diversity.  

• Extreme disparities in weather conditions affect food security, livelihoods and job opportunities for rural communities, predominantly women.  
• Women comprise 70 percent of the agricultural workforce in Senegal and are responsible for producing 80 percent of the agricultural yield.  
• Considering their labor and income are dependent on the rains, women are vulnerable to the most extreme conditions of poverty because it limits access to food and water, increases job insecurity, makes self-sufficiency difficult and limits their ability to improve their socio-economic status.  

• To adapt to soil and land degradation, women have adopted practices that are intensive to make the most of the lacklustre rainfall, but those practices have proven to diminish the soils.  
• Another deterrent to productivity beyond climate change is a lack of access to modern technology used for agriculture, which subjects women to tougher labor conditions.  

• While climate change disproportionately affects women in Senegal, women are also the leaders in finding solutions to the effects of climate change with groups such as Regroupement des femmes de Popenguine pour la Protection de la Nature (RFPPN) who have taken charge of reforestation efforts in the Popenguine region of Southern Senegal.  

### Energy Access  
• Successful policies have increased energy access in Senegal to almost 70% of the population.  
• With full access expected to be achieved by 2025. While the electricity grid accounts for the majority of the population with access, decentralized solutions are still expected to be critical for the most remote populations.  

• LPG is used for cooking by almost 30% of the population today, one of the highest shares in sub-Saharan Africa.  
• It is expected to remain the main clean cooking fuel in 2030. However, affordability challenges remain to expand this access to the 70% who currently rely on biomass for cooking.

These conventions have been integrated into Ivory Coast's national policy through the development of various strategies and plans, such as the National Gender Policy (2014–2023) and the National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence (2016–2020). The National Gender Policy aims to promote gender equality and empower women in all sectors of the society, while the National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence seeks to prevent and respond to gender-based violence through various measures.

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Gender-based Violence  
• Despite these efforts, domestic violence is widespread throughout Côte d’Ivoire, is particular sexual, psychological, and economic violence (ICCPR, 2015; MFFAS & UNFPA, 2008). However, it is considered an intimate problem and is therefore rarely reported (MFFAS & UNFPA, 2008).  
• Victims mostly remain silent regarding gender-based violence and most cases are not reported to the authorities (MSFWC, 2014). Gender-based violence cases are often settled amicably through traditional justice systems, as those cases are often considered a private matter (MSFWC, 2014). Going to court is a public process and may mean for victims exposing themselves to stigma and exclusion from their families and communities (MSFWC, 2014).  

### Female-headed households  
• Female-headed households face different economic and social constraints than other types of families in Côte d’Ivoire. With fewer male laborers than other households, female-headed households in agriculture have lower income and less access to the formal market.  

### Political participation  
• In Côte d’Ivoire, women have the same rights to vote and contest elections as men. Côte d'Ivoire passed a law in 2019 that requires political parties to meet a female candidate quota of 30% for parliamentary elections, however this has not been achieved.  
• It is reported that while election processes are held in all regions, women are not given priority when there is only one position available for them, which is the case in many regions making the 30% quota almost impossible to achieve in most local contexts. Political parties are heavily male dominated, with men making most of the decisions at national and divisional levels.  

### Labour force participation  
• Agriculture is a significant economic driver in the Country and although they do not own large farms, women are an important workforce, especially in the production and marketing of food crops. They are present at 29.9% in the agricultural sector; 31.6% in industry; 59.5% in trade and 39.5% in services.  
• While the % of labour participation for women (15 years and above) in Côte d’Ivoire is estimated at 46%, most of the employment is in the informal sector with less than 4% of women having formal jobs, compared to 8% for men. While the employment rate is observed to be higher for people with higher levels of education, it is observed that women are still more vulnerable to unemployment than men.  
• Gender-related behaviours and practices in the labour market in Côte d’Ivoire are rooted in the traditional patriarchal system, which is unfavourable to the promotion of women’s activities. The precarious and informal nature of women’s work is mainly explained by the principles of separation and hierarchy in society. Thus, the man is considered the head of the family and must therefore work to meet the family’s needs, while the role of the woman is essentially domestic. Consequently, it is easy to understand why, despite the existence of employment opportunities, women are excluded or reduced to precarious or marginal jobs.  

### Female Labour force participation rate (% ages 15 and older)  
48.3%

### Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other  

- According to research by CGAP84 financial inclusion in Cote d-Ivoire is low with only 45% of the population having any access to formal financial service. However, this exclusion remains high and is not even. Women are 45% more likely to be excluded than men. The data shows that the most financially excluded segment is uneducated rural women, 91 percent of whom are financially excluded. Urban women fare better, but still not as well as men. Further, all the gains in financial inclusion made in the last five years have been purely driven by access to mobile money with only ~7% of the sampled population having access to traditional banks.  

- A major barrier is that fewer Ivorian women own mobile phones, especially in rural areas. GSMA’s assessment of Côte d’Ivoire in 2017 showed that 20 percent fewer women than men owned phones. This gap is estimated to be even higher in rural areas. Financial innumeracy is also a factor. The Financial Inclusion Insights survey shows that half the population of Côte d’Ivoire does not have financial numeracy skills or the ability to navigate a phone menu or send text messages. Innumeracy disproportionately affects women in Côte d’Ivoire, where only 46% of women are numerate versus 62% of men.  

- While literacy levels are low across the board, it is expected that representation of women in traditionally male dominated fields is significantly low due to existing patriarchal structures and biases.  

---

### Women in energy in Cote d'Ivoire  

- According to reports85 women are largely excluded from policymaking in the energy sector in Côte d’Ivoire, as well as from employment opportunities, especially at professional/technical levels (engineers, electricians) in the key policymaking, oversight, and operational agencies. This mirrors the realities of women across other sectors and is not unique to energy.  

---

### Women in Energy Productivity in Cote d’Ivoire  

- A 2016 African Development Bank report shared that women have limited access to productive use of energy products and would benefit from timesaving and business productivity increases

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  
### Page 199  

#### Women in Mobility in Cote d'Ivoire  

- Côte d’Ivoire is one of the most urbanized countries in Sub-Saharan Africa, and the Greater Abidjan Area (GAA) is its engine of growth. More than half—56 percent— of Côte d’Ivoire’s population lives in urban centers, with urbanization increasing at 5 percent yearly, albeit with high spatial disparity between the GAA and other cities. By 2050, the share of urban population will increase to two-thirds.  

- Despite significant public investments in road infrastructure in recent years, GAA suffers from unreliable urban transport, high transport costs, and growing congestion, which hinders its competitiveness. Urban mobility has become a major challenge: the population growth and economic development of Abidjan have a significant influence on the demand for transport, and this pressure is reinforced by the weaknesses of the current public transport supply, which is dominated by informality, lack of coordination, obsolescence, inadequacy, and shortage of system management personnel.  

- Women are under-represented in the transport sector worldwide, and Côte d’Ivoire is no exception. Women represent only 8 percent of the SOTRA workforce (of about 4,000 employees) and are mostly employed in clerical jobs (only 10 women are drivers or work in bus maintenance). Few women (less than 20) are woroworo, taxi, or Gbaka drivers in GAA, although it is believed that a slightly higher proportion of women own vehicles.  

#### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries  

- Labour participation of women in the country is concentrated in agriculture. Agriculture is indicated as one of the sectors that will experience the highest impacts of climate change given changes in precipitation and weather patterns, this leads to the need for increased adaptation responses to address the potential impacts of climate change.  

- ~86% of women are self-employed. This indicates a higher level of vulnerability to effects of climate change and overall lower adaptive capacity to climate events or impacts. Further, 85% of the female population in Cote d’Ivoire is indicated to be in vulnerable employment. This is critical as it indicates that most women in these countries are likely to be disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards. The wage gap and poor access to physical and knowledge resources also indicates lower resilience to potential effects of climate change for this population.  

- Women are both providers and consumers of energy. The gender division of labour assigns the bulk of household (domestic) tasks to women. These include often spending considerable time (and energy) in provisioning their households with fuel for cooking and other purposes, which primarily involves fetching fuelwood. Since cooking is also primarily the responsibility of women, they are also likely to be the principal consumers of energy at the household level. The presence

# KawiSafi Fund II: Annex 8  
## Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan  

### Part III: Lessons learnt from KawiSafi Fund I  

KawiSafi Fund II also derives insight from the experience with KawiSafi Fund I. The KawiSafi team conducted annual gender assessments for all portfolio companies.  

- As of Dec 21, the KawiSafi portfolio had an average gender score of 6.05, indicating above average performance. The portfolio was aligned with the 2X Challenge with 30% of companies meeting the direct criteria. However, key areas for improvement included representation of women in senior management and Board roles; availability of training for gender-specific safety and security issues; and representation of female-led companies in the funding pipeline.  

- The KawiSafi I Technical Assistance Facility supports gender initiatives, including skill enhancement for low-income women, having trained 64 women during 2021; increased awareness on importance of clean energy products; and support for increased financial inclusion for women.  

- The findings are highlighted below:  

#### Policies, Training and Targets  

All KawiSafi portfolio companies had general policies relating to  
1) non-discrimination and harassment;  
2) diversity and inclusion;  
3) accommodation of persons with disabilities; this indicates general alignment to these issues.  

50% of KawiSafi portfolio companies had policies specific to gender parity. However, less than 50% of these companies had any monitoring or targets related to any of the above issues (including gender); this was driven by a lack of resources by these companies and highlighted the need for TA support.  

#### Employment and Management  

31% of total employed staff of the portfolio companies were women as of 31 Dec 21. This however varied significantly from 64% in one company (Opes Solutions - large manufacturing company) to 24% (InspiraFarms - small installer). Women's roles however remained junior and in more administrative and sales positions with little representation in senior management and engineering.  

#### Board membership  

2 out of 10 of the Companies did not have a female Board member as of 31 Dec 2021. Specifically: InspiraFarms and Bboxx. This was despite this being one of the areas highlighted as part of the ESG action plan. KawiSafi however worked with 3 companies (Sistema.bio, Opes and Lendable) to appoint women to their Board.  

#### Consumers  

It is estimated that 32.4% and 31% of households in Kenya and Rwanda are headed by females respectively. Women are the primary users of energy in the household and are among the prime beneficiaries of access to modern energy services. Numerous

## Part V: Gender Action Plan  

### KawiSafi Fund II: Gender Action Plan  

#### Impact statement:  
To drive integration, responsiveness, and participation of women in the African distributed energy,  
productivity, and electric mobility ecosystem. This includes, but not limited to, support for female led and owned  
businesses; increased representation through employment at Fund and portfolio company level; promotion of senior  
female leadership and increase in female access and participation across the distributed energy and clean mobility value  
chain.  

#### Outcome Statements:  

1) Enhancement of economic activities, livelihoods, and empowerment of women by driving the development of decent  
jobs for women across the three industry segments KawiSafi Fund II intends to invest in across Africa.  

2) Support increased access of venture capital funding to female led and owned businesses across Africa.  

3) Enhance access to low carbon technologies for women through gender integrated product design and distribution.  

4) Increase participation of women in the clean mobility industry  

5) Reduce historical gender disparity in skills development across technical fields - including solar PV and other clean  
generation technologies; battery technology; electrical and mechanical engineering, among others.  

#### Output 1:  
Understand baseline performance of portfolio companies across outcome areas.  

#### Output 2:  
Throughout the life of the program (i) partner with KawiSafi portfolio companies to drive employment of women  
across their organisations, including representation in senior leadership; (ii) Partner with ecosystem partners working on  
improving skills and capabilities of women across the segments of focus, to improve female employability.  

#### Output 3:  
Throughout the life of the program, support increased venture capital allocation to female owned/led  
businesses.  

#### Output 4:  
Throughout the life of the program, (i) directly engage KawiSafi portfolio companies to drive increased female  
access of low carbon technologies as well as support sale of products that disproportionately improve women's wellbeing  
and (ii) through partnership with both portfolio companies and industry associations, support improvement of quality of  
service offered to female consumers and users - including but not limited to, increased safety and comfort in e-mobility  
applications.  

#### Indicators and Targets Timeline Responsibilities  

Portfolio level activities will be measured against the Gender Action Plans that will be developed post-investment. Ecosystem Activities  
that will be implemented through the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) will have no baseline.
